            "exact_quote": "For $\\varepsilon>0$, put $h=(d-\\varepsilon)_+$.",
            "source_object": "stmt-lem-transport"
          }
        ],
        "id": "transport-membership-and-norms",
        "quantifiers": [
          {
            "anchor": "stmt-lem-transport",
            "exact_quote": "Every representation, with matching matrix amplifications, satisfies $$\\label{eq:transport-represented-norm}\n \\|\\pi^{(m)}(wkw^*)\\|=\\|\\pi^{(n)}(k)\\|\n \\qquad(k\\in\\operatorname{Her}(h)).$$ The products in these formulas are algebra elements; $w$ itself is only asserted to lie in the bidual.*\n\n</div>\n\n<details id=\"proof-stmt-lem-transport\"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transport</summary>\n\n*Proof.* Compress a stabilization comparison coefficient to the first $m$ target and first $n$ source coordinates. These scalar multiplier projections give $s\\in M_{m,n}(C)$ with $\\|d-s^*vs\\|<\\varepsilon/2$. Put $z=v^{1/2}s h$. Order in the unitization and functional calculus for $d$ give $$\\label{eq:transport-order}\n \\frac{\\varepsilon}{2}h^2\n \\le z^*z=h s^*vs h\n \\le(\\|d\\|+\\varepsilon/2)h^2.$$ For the lower bound, use $h d h\\ge\\varepsilon h^2$. The two inequalities and Lemma <a href=\"#lem:comparison-rules\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:comparison-rules\">4</a>(i) show $\\operatorname{Her}(z^*z)=\\operatorname{Her}(h)$. Consequently $\\operatorname{Her}(|z|)=\\operatorname{Her}(h)$ and $e_{z^*z}=e_h$. Let $z=w|z|$ be the rectangular polar decomposition in the bidual. Then $w^*w=e_h$; the factorization through $v^{1/2}$ gives $ww^*=e_{zz^*}\\le e_v$.\n\nFor $k\\in\\operatorname{Her}(h)=\\operatorname{Her}(|z|)$, take cutoff functions $g_j$ for $|z|$, zero near zero, with $g_j(|z|)k\\to k$. Since $$w g_j(|z|)=z\\,\\frac{g_j(|z|)}{|z|}\\in M_{m,n}(C),$$ norm closure gives $wk\\in M_{m,n}(C)$. If $k\\ge0$, then $wkw^*=(wk^{1/2})(wk^{1/2})^*$ belongs to $M_m(C)$. It is supported under $e_v$, hence belongs to $\\operatorname{Her}(v)$. Linear decomposition handles arbitrary $k$. The identity $w^*w=e_h$ proves multiplicativity and injectivity. It also proves <a href=\"#eq:transport-cutoffs\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:transport-cutoffs\">[eq:transport-cutoffs]</a>: functional calculus vanishing at zero commutes with the homomorphism, and the two squares of $w(l-\\tau)_+^{1/2}$ are the asserted positives.\n\nFor <a href=\"#eq:transport-hereditary-image\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:transport-hereditary-image\">[eq:transport-hereditary-image]</a>, normal functional calculus gives $e_{wlw^*}=we_lw^*$. Thus the image of $\\operatorname{Her}(l)$ is supported under $e_{wlw^*}$, which gives one inclusion by Lemma <a href=\"#lem:comparison-rules\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:comparison-rules\">4</a>(i). A sandwich by the image of $l$ has the form $$(wlw^*)b(wlw^*)\n =w\\bigl((wl)^*b(wl)\\bigr)w^*.$$ The inner algebra element is supported on both sides by $e_l$, so lies in $\\operatorname{Her}(l)$. Since conjugation is an injective $*$-homomorphism on $\\operatorname{Her}(l)$, its image is norm closed; taking closures gives the reverse inclusion. Finally, the normal extension of $\\pi$ carries <a href=\"#eq:transport-supports\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:transport-supports\">[eq:transport-supports]</a> to the same identities with represented supports. Conjugating back by the represented $w^*$ recovers $\\pi^{(n)}(k)$. The two contractive conjugations give both inequalities in <a href=\"#eq:transport-represented-norm\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:transport-represented-norm\">[eq:transport-represented-norm]</a>. ◻\n\n</details>\n\nIf $d_0\\in M_\\ell(C)_+$ and $d=d_0^{\\oplus r}\\in\nM_{r\\ell}(C)_+$, split $w\\in M_{m,r\\ell}(C^{**})$ into block columns $w_i\\in M_{m,\\ell}(C^{**})$. Then $$w_i^*w_j=\\delta_{ij}e_{(d_0-\\varepsilon)_+}.$$ The $r$ copies have orthogonal ranges and the same initial support. The single rectangular map also transports every entry of a matrix over the common hereditary algebra. Returning to the notation of Lemma <a href=\"#lem:transport\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:transport\">5</a>, for $j\\ge1$ the same support identities apply to $1_j\\otimes w\\in M_{jm,jn}(C^{**})$. Thus, for every $K\\in M_j(\\operatorname{Her}_{M_n(C)}(h))$, entrywise conjugation gives an algebra matrix and preserves its norm in every representation, with the matching $jm$ and $jn$ amplifications.",
            "source_object": "stmt-lem-transport"
          }
        ],

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The arbitrary-entry diagonalization result supplies existence of algebra elements with prescribed norm errors, but no finite input representation or executable procedure for computing those elements. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The square-zero construction preserves nonzero images across primitive quotients; it specifies no simulation state, evolution rule, or rendering operation. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Cutoff transport takes place through a partial isometry in the universal bidual. Algebra membership of its products does not provide a computable representation suitable for a numerical or symbolic utility. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The universal support contraction is obtained by a contradiction involving bounded products. The source gives no numerical value for the universal factor or finite procedure for selecting the required hereditary contractions. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Quasi-compact primitive norm slices support finite-cover arguments, but the source gives no finite encoding of primitive ideals or operations for constructing and querying such covers. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Finite matrix evaluations characterize universal polynomial norms through a supremum over arbitrary matrix sizes. This does not specify a bounded matrix workload, accelerator kernel, or stopping criterion. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The universal contraction obstruction concerns exactness and approximate reducing copies of matrix contractions. It supplies no adversarial model, computational hardness assumption, or cryptographic construction. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The forbidden tuple compares joint matrix norms at every matrix level. Its finite compressions give pointwise approximation of fixed tests, rather than a training method or a generalization guarantee from sampled tests. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Bounded diagonalization controls coefficient norms and approximation errors under proper infiniteness. It supplies neither a finite feasible-set representation nor an objective and computable optimization method. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Permanence under bounded products is an operator-algebraic comparison result requiring a common norm bound on comparison rows. It does not construct a communication protocol or establish consistency of replicated states. |
| other (operator algebra structure theory) | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | The source constructs a square-zero element in a prescribed hereditary subalgebra that remains nonzero throughout a prescribed quasi-compact set of primitive quotients. Its initial and range squares supply orthogonal Cuntz-equivalent positives, supporting amplification removal and structural comparison. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-universal-individual-premise** (hypothesis): The general diagonalization theorem requires proper infiniteness of every positive element of the ambient algebra. Testing only the selected diagonal inputs does not establish this premise. Blocked use: ['Invoke arbitrary-entry diagonalization after verifying infiniteness only for the supplied inputs.']
- **limit-common-amplification** (hypothesis): Amplification removal requires a single integer chosen for the whole algebra and valid for every scalar positive element, including the scalar cutoffs used later. Blocked use: ['Replace the common amplification premise by element-dependent amplification witnesses.']
- **limit-nonexact-amplification-removal** (formal_scope): The amplification-removal theorem assumes exactness. Its conclusion does not establish the weak-to-ordinary implication for arbitrary nonexact algebras. Blocked use: ['Remove fixed amplification in an arbitrary nonexact algebra using the exact theorem.']
- **limit-absorption-regularity** (hypothesis): The tensorial absorption conclusion requires separability and nuclearity in addition to the common amplification premise. Blocked use: ['Infer absorption for a nonseparable or nonnuclear algebra solely from the general strong-infiniteness theorem.']
- **limit-bidual-carrier-membership** (formal_scope): Cutoff transport asserts bidual membership for the carrier itself. Algebra membership is established for its specified products with the cutoff hereditary algebra. Blocked use: ['Use the transport carrier itself as an original algebra element.', 'Omit the cutoff hereditary membership checks for transported products.']
- **limit-graph-choice-order** (hypothesis): Graph measurements are fixed before the freely chosen entries. The first positive input must be rich, and the graph coefficients must satisfy the stated strict budget. Blocked use: ['Recompute the fixed measurements after selecting the entries.', 'Apply the graph proposition to a first input without establishing richness.']
- **limit-conditional-defect-control** (hypothesis): Conditional shrinking requires both the represented norm signal of a rich first output and the defect nonvanishing implication in every representation. A single detected representation does not suffice. Blocked use: ['Conclude a global support compression bound from nonvanishing in only selected representations.']
- **limit-actual-range-not-closure** (formal_scope): The spectral cutoff argument uses the actual operator range. It does not supply a closed-range assertion. Blocked use: ['Replace the actual-range inclusion by an unsupported closed-range claim.']
- **limit-four-entry-existential-selection** (nonconstructive): The avoiding entries are obtained by contradiction using directed finite tests, a tail ultrafilter, and an irreducible representation. The proof supplies no effective selection bound for a successful index. Blocked use: ['Treat measurement avoidance as a terminating finite search with a source-provided bound.']
- **limit-product-uniform-control** (hypothesis): Comparison in a bounded product requires comparison rows with a common norm bound. Coordinatewise witnesses alone need not form a coefficient in the bounded product. Blocked use: ['Assemble unbounded coordinate comparison coefficients into a product-algebra coefficient.']
- **limit-uniform-factor-effectivity** (nonconstructive): The universal support factor is proved to exist through a bounded-product contradiction. A numerical value and an effective procedure for choosing the associated hereditary contractions are ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Claim a computable iteration count from a numerical universal factor supplied by the paper.']
- **limit-uniform-mixed-input** (hypothesis): Uniform shrinking and its iteration require the mixed contraction to belong to the algebra itself. The uniform statement does not cover arbitrary separate unitizations of product coordinates. Blocked use: ['Extend the uniform product argument to mixed entries from arbitrary individual unitizations.']
- **limit-diagonal-cutoff-coefficient-bound** (hypothesis): The bounded diagonalization estimate assumes positive contraction diagonals and the stated restrictions on cutoff and comparison tolerances. Its coefficient ceiling is not an unrestricted bound for arbitrary inputs. Blocked use: ['Apply the displayed coefficient ceiling without normalizing the diagonals or checking the tolerance restrictions.']
- **limit-tuple-universal-tests** (approximation): The forbidden tuple excludes simultaneous agreement at every matrix level. Its matrix compressions approximate each fixed test pointwise, without asserting sufficiency of a fixed finite test list. Blocked use: ['Certify the universal tuple obstruction from a fixed finite collection of matrix tests.', 'Infer uniform convergence over all matrix tests from pointwise compression convergence.']
- **limit-joint-tuple-transport** (hypothesis): Nuclear avoidance transports the whole tuple through a common rectangular carrier. Separate carriers for individual entries do not establish preservation of joint matrix norms. Blocked use: ['Replace the common tuple carrier by independently chosen carriers while retaining the joint norm conclusion.']
- **limit-local-rich-representation-dependence** (formal_scope): The local rich witness is selected for an irreducible representation detecting the input. The lemma alone does not provide a single witness retaining all representation signals. Blocked use: ['Use one local witness as a global replacement without the merger and ideal assembly arguments.']
- **limit-nuclear-merger-richness** (hypothesis): The merger starts with rich positive contractions and uses the common amplification hypothesis. Nuclear shrinking guarantees richness of its first output; richness of the second output is not asserted. Blocked use: ['Assume both shrinking outputs are rich.', 'Discard the orthogonal amplified copies used to establish richness of the merged sum.']
- **limit-uniform-gap-adjoint-copy** (hypothesis): The uniform gap requires the same finite-dimensional copy to approximately intertwine both the contraction and its adjoint. It quantifies over all finite matrix contractions. Blocked use: ['Use only the contraction intertwining relation.', 'Use different isometries for the contraction and its adjoint.', 'Replace the universal matrix-model premise by sampled models.']
- **limit-polynomial-space-fixed-evaluation** (formal_scope): The polynomial-space constant depends on the fixed space and its representatives. The exactness-gap proof fixes its evaluation before varying matrix amplification, tensor compression, or the ideal approximate-unit net. Blocked use: ['Treat the polynomial-space constant as independent of the polynomial space.', 'Choose a different evaluation for each approximate-unit cutoff while using the fixed-evaluation tensor argument.']
- **limit-perturbation-support-and-options** (hypothesis): Uniform perturbation requires the stated support identities and common amplified comparisons. Hereditary membership requires the target positives to belong to the hereditary subalgebra; selfadjointness additionally requires positive orthogonal outer factors. Blocked use: ['Request a selfadjoint perturbation without positive orthogonal outer factors.', 'Request hereditary membership without the hereditary target hypothesis.', 'Apply the quotient floor without the support identities or common comparisons.']
- **limit-primitive-slice-topology** (formal_scope): Non-strict positive primitive norm slices are quasi-compact without a Hausdorff assumption. The proof does not assert that these slices are closed. Blocked use: ['Use closedness or Hausdorff separation as a consequence of primitive norm-slice quasi-compactness.']
- **limit-finite-coupling-selection** (nonconstructive): Finite coupling selects a finite subcover of locally available primitive neighborhoods and uses existential perturbations. This finite assembly is not a finite encoded witness-selection algorithm. Blocked use: ['Assign a computational complexity or termination certificate to the abstract finite-cover selection.']
- **limit-square-zero-localization-premises** (hypothesis): Square-zero localization requires exactness, the common amplification property, a quasi-compact primitive subset, and a hereditary subalgebra nonzero at every point of that subset. Blocked use: ['Require a nonzero quotient image at a point where the hereditary subalgebra vanishes.', 'Apply the global localization conclusion to an arbitrary primitive subset without quasi-compactness.']
- **limit-halving-fixed-slice** (formal_scope): Amplification removal repeats hereditary halving on the same primitive norm slice, retaining nonzero images there. The final comparison limit keeps the original positive target fixed. Blocked use: ['Change the covered primitive slice between halving stages without preserving the original coverage.', 'Pass to a comparison limit while also varying the target without an additional argument.']
- **limit-quantitative-floor-choice-order** (hypothesis): Quantitative separation fixes the quotient norm floor before choosing the product accuracy. The resulting positives need not have norms uniformly bounded as the product parameter decreases. Blocked use: ['Assume a uniform norm ceiling for the separated positives.', 'Choose the quotient floor as a function of the later product accuracy while claiming the stated independence.']
- **limit-bounded-implementation-spectral-premise** (hypothesis): The bounded implementation lemma requires comparison into the specified cutoff above a positive spectral floor and a positive target of norm at most the stated bound. Blocked use: ['Apply its implementer ceiling to comparison into the uncut positive target.', 'Use the coefficient ceiling without a positive spectral floor.']
- **limit-no-finite-executable-certificate** (unknown): A finite executable decision procedure for the universal positive-element premise, a witness-selection runtime bound, and a finite certificate deciding the full infiniteness conclusions are ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Publish the abstract infiniteness or absorption conclusions as an executable numerical certification routine.']

In an irreducible representation of the resulting image of $C$, the first pair would give an isometry with nonzero defect, while the last pair would rule out that defect.\n\nAll formulas described as fixed below depend only on $X,B_i$ and the displayed constants. Define $$\\label{eq:four-first-fixed}\n x=(X-\\alpha)_+,\\qquad V=(X-\\beta)_+,\\qquad\n Q=(h1-B_1B_1^*)_+,\\qquad U=xQ^2x.$$ Here $Q\\in\\widetilde C_+$, whereas $x,V,U\\in C_+$. Let $\\operatorname{Fin}(C)$ denote the set of finite subsets of $C$, and order $$\\Lambda=\\mathbb N_{\\ge1}\\times\\operatorname{Fin}(C),$$ by the usual order in the first coordinate and inclusion in the second. At $\\lambda=(m,E)$, put $\\eta=1/m$, so $\\eta\\to0$ along this directed set, and every fixed $w\\in C$ eventually belongs to $E$.\n\n\n*The first two entries.* The first pair will force $UwV=0$ for every fixed $w\\in C$ in a limiting image of $C$. At the index $(m,E)$, we test only the finitely many $w\\in E$. A positive sum lets one norm bound control all of these tests: set $$\\label{eq:four-D}\n A_\\eta=(U-\\eta)_+,\\qquad\n D_0=A_\\eta\\left(\\sum_{w\\in E}wVw^*\\right)A_\\eta,\\qquad\n D=g_\\eta(D_0),$$ where $g_\\eta$ is a ramp that is zero on $[0,\\eta]$ and one on $[2\\eta,\\infty)$. In fact, $$D_0=\\sum_{w\\in E}\n (A_\\eta wV^{1/2})(A_\\eta wV^{1/2})^*.$$ Thus a small norm for $D_0$ makes each $A_\\eta wV^{1/2}$ small. We have $$D_0\\in\\operatorname{Her}(A_\\eta)\\cap\\operatorname{Id}(V),\\qquad\n e_D\\le e_{(D_0-\\eta)_+}\\le e_{A_\\eta}\\le e_x.$$ Since $V$ is properly infinite, ideal comparison gives $D_0\\oplus D_0\\precsim V$. Apply Lemma <a href=\"#lem:transport\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:transport\">5</a> with cutoff $\\eta$, split the carrier into two columns, and multiply these columns by $D^{1/2}$. Taking adjoints of the resulting two elements gives contractions $T_1,T_2\\in C$ with $$\\label{eq:four-first-entries}\n T_iT_j^*=\\delta_{ij}D,\\qquad e_VT_i^*=T_i^*\n \\quad(i,j=1,2).$$ Their left supports lie under $e_D$ and their right supports under $e_V$. Both are under $e_x\\le e_{(X-\\alpha_0)_+}$; they therefore belong to the required hereditary algebra.\n\n\nConsider a representation satisfying <a href=\"#eq:four-forbidden\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-forbidden\">[eq:four-forbidden]</a> for the entries at this index, and omit its symbols. Then $B_1B_1^*=D$ and $Q=(h1-D)_+$. The spectral subspace of $D$ for $(h,\\infty)$ is killed by $Q$ and lies in $e_D\\mathcal H\\subset e_{(U-\\eta)_+}\\mathcal H$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:actual-range\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:actual-range\">10</a> shows that this subspace is zero. Thus $$\\label{eq:four-first-bounds}\n B_1B_1^*=D\\le h1,\\qquad \\|D_0\\|\\le2\\eta\n \\quad\\text{in this representation}.$$ For the second conclusion, if the norm of $D_0$ were at least $2\\eta$, spectral mapping and the plateau of $g_\\eta$ would give $\\|D\\|=1>h$.\n\n\n*A fixed prospective defect and the last two entries.* Choose ramps $$H=h_0(X),\\qquad K=k_0(X)$$ so that $h_0$ vanishes on a neighborhood of $[0,\\alpha]$ and equals one on $[\\beta,\\infty)$, and $k_0$ vanishes on a neighborhood of $[0,\\alpha_0]$ and equals one on $[\\alpha,\\infty)$. In particular $KH=H$. Set $$\\label{eq:four-r}\n r_i=h^{-1/2}B_i^*H\\quad(i=1,2),\\qquad\n b_0=(1-H^2)^{1/2}.$$ The reason for these definitions is the following conditional calculation. In the limiting irreducible representation below, the first pair will give $$r_i^*r_j=\\delta_{ij}H^2,\\qquad Hr_i=r_i\\quad(i,j=1,2),\n \\qquad H\\ne0.$$ These relations are not being asserted in $C$ at this stage. They imply $b_0r_i=0$, and hence $$v=r_1+b_0,\\qquad v^*v=H^2+b_0^2=1,\\qquad v^*r_2=0.$$ Since $r_2^*r_2=H^2\\ne0$, the isometry $v$ has a nonzero defect projection $1-vv^*$. This is the scaling-element construction of Blackadar–Cuntz (B. E. Blackadar and Cuntz 1982) [[BlackadarCuntz1982](#ref-BlackadarCuntz1982)], in the form recalled in (Pasnicu and Rørdam 2007, Remark 2.4) [[PasnicuRordam2007](#ref-PasnicuRordam2007)]; the second entry supplies the additional nonzero orthogonal range.\n\n\nTo choose the last two entries before taking any limit, we encode this prospective defect by one fixed element of $C$. Compression by $K$ puts the formula in the required hereditary algebra, and clipping makes it a positive contraction even before the preceding relations hold. Let $\\chi(t)=\\min\\{1,\\max\\{0,t\\}\\}$ on $\\mathbb R$, and define $$\\label{eq:four-P0}\n P_0=\\chi\\!\\left(\n K\\bigl[1-(r_1+b_0)(r_1+b_0)^*\\bigr]K\n \\right)\\in C.$$ The compressed selfadjoint element is in $C$ and supported under $e_K$; since $\\chi(0)=0$, continuous functional calculus in $\\operatorname{Her}(K)$ gives $$e_{P_0}\\le e_K\\le e_{(X-\\alpha_0)_+}.$$ This membership and support statement uses no relation from a representation.\n\n\nDefine the following additional fixed elements: $$\\label{eq:four-second-fixed}\n x'=(P_0-1/2)_+,\\qquad\n Q'=(h1-B_3B_3^*)_+,\\qquad R=x'(Q')^2x'.$$ At the same index $\\eta$, property $\\textup{(P)}$ gives $$((R-\\eta)_+)^{\\oplus2}\\precsim(R-\\eta)_+.$$ Apply Lemma <a href=\"#lem:transport\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:transport\">5</a> to this comparison with cutoff $\\eta$. If $t_R$ is a ramp of $R$, zero on $[0,2\\eta]$ and one on $[4\\eta,\\infty)$, the two carrier columns times $t_R$ give contractions $T_3,T_4\\in C$ such that $$\\label{eq:four-last-entries}\n T_3^*T_4=0,\\qquad T_3^*T_3=T_4^*T_4=t_R^2.$$ Their left supports lie under $e_{(R-\\eta)_+}$ and their right supports under $e_{(R-2\\eta)_+}$. Both are under $e_{x'}\\le e_{(X-\\alpha_0)_+}$. Transport preserves the represented norms, so $$\\label{eq:four-last-norm}\n \\|\\pi(R)\\|>4\\eta\\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n \\|\\pi(T_3)\\|=1.$$\n\nIn a representation satisfying <a href=\"#eq:four-forbidden\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-forbidden\">[eq:four-forbidden]</a>, $B_3=T_3$. Apply Lemma <a href=\"#lem:actual-range\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:actual-range\">10</a> to $x',Q',R$. The spectral subspace of $B_3B_3^*$ above $h$ is killed by $Q'$ and lies under $e_{(R-\\eta)_+}$, so it is zero. Equation <a href=\"#eq:four-last-norm\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-last-norm\">[eq:four-last-norm]</a> then gives $$\\label{eq:four-last-bounds}\n B_3B_3^*\\le h1,\\qquad \\|R\\|\\le4\\eta\n \\quad\\text{in this representation}.$$\n\n*Passing only fixed elements to an ultraproduct.* Suppose, for a contradiction, that every index in $\\Lambda$ admits a representation satisfying <a href=\"#eq:four-forbidden\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-forbidden\">[eq:four-forbidden]</a>. Choose one, denoted $\\pi_\\lambda$, on its nondegenerate essential subspace. There is no size issue in this choice: from any such representation, choose a vector detecting $\\|\\pi_\\lambda(X)\\|>s$ and restrict to its reducing cyclic subrepresentation. A set of cyclic representations suffices. Choose an ultrafilter $\\omega$ containing all tails of $\\Lambda$, and form the norm ultraproduct $$\\mathcal B=\\prod_{\\omega}B(\\mathcal H_\\lambda)\n =\\frac{\\prod_\\lambda B(\\mathcal H_\\lambda)}\n {\\{(z_\\lambda):\\lim_{\\omega}\\|z_\\lambda\\|=0\\}}.$$ The norm of a class is $\\lim_{\\omega}\\|z_\\lambda\\|$. Let $\\Phi:C\\to\\mathcal B$ be $\\Phi(z)=[(\\pi_\\lambda(z))]$, and write $B=\\Phi(C)$. The image is a closed $C^*$-subalgebra. The unital extensions carry the unitized formulas into $C^*(B,1_{\\mathcal B})$. We suppress $\\Phi$ on fixed elements from now on.\n\n\n\n\nFor any fixed $w\\in C$, eventually $w\\in E$. Positivity of the summands in <a href=\"#eq:four-D\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-D\">[eq:four-D]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:four-first-bounds\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-first-bounds\">[eq:four-first-bounds]</a> yield $$\\|\\pi_\\lambda((U-\\eta)_+wV^{1/2})\\|^2\\le2\\eta$$ on that tail. Since $\\|U-(U-\\eta)_+\\|\\le\\eta$, $$\\label{eq:four-UCV-estimate}\n \\|\\pi_\\lambda(UwV)\\|\n \\le\\sqrt{2\\eta}\\,\\|V\\|^{1/2}\n      +\\eta\\|w\\|\\|V\\|\\longrightarrow0.$$ It follows that $UBV=0$ in the image algebra. The other exact relations and bounds at each index give $$\\label{eq:four-ultraproduct}\n \\begin{gathered}\n \\|X\\|\\ge s,\\qquad B_1B_1^*\\le h1,\\qquad\n B_iB_j^*=\\delta_{ij}B_1B_1^*,\\quad HB_i^*=B_i^*\n       \\quad(i,j=1,2),\\\\\n UBV=0,\\qquad R=0,\\qquad B_3B_3^*\\le h1,\\qquad\n B_3^*B_4=0,\\quad B_3^*B_3=B_4^*B_4.\n \\end{gathered}$$ For $HB_i^*=B_i^*$, use $e_VT_i^*=T_i^*$ and $H=1$ on the support of $V$. Fix also a continuous $h_P:[0,1]\\to[0,1]$ with $h_P(0)=0$ and $h_P=1$ on $[1/2,1]$. The left support of $T_4$ lies under $e_{x'}$, so we obtain $$\\label{eq:four-B4-support}\n h_P(P_0)B_4=B_4.$$ Every element in <a href=\"#eq:four-ultraproduct\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-ultraproduct\">[eq:four-ultraproduct]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:four-B4-support\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-B4-support\">[eq:four-B4-support]</a> is the image of a fixed algebra element or of its fixed unitized functional calculus. We have not placed the varying $T_i,D,D_0,t_R$ in $B$, and we do not require proper infiniteness of $B$ or of $\\mathcal B$. The directed finite sets in <a href=\"#eq:four-UCV-estimate\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-UCV-estimate\">[eq:four-UCV-estimate]</a> handle all $w\\in C$ without a separability assumption.\n\n*A nonzero defect gives the contradiction.* Since $\\|X\\|\\ge s>\\beta$, the image $V=(X-\\beta)_+$ is nonzero. Choose an irreducible representation of $B$ in which $V\\ne0$. Extend this nondegenerate representation to $C^*(B,1_{\\mathcal B})=B+\\mathbb C1_{\\mathcal B}$ by sending $1_{\\mathcal B}$ to the identity operator; this remains valid if $B$ has a unit different from $1_{\\mathcal B}$. Thus the unitized expressions $Q,b_0,Q'$ and the defect below have their stated represented meanings. Again suppress the representation symbols. The closed span of $BV\\mathcal H$ is a nonzero reducing subspace, and hence all of $\\mathcal H$. The relation $UBV=0$ therefore gives $U=0$. Put $D_1=B_1B_1^*$. Since $D_1\\le h1$, we have $Q=h1-D_1$, and $$0=U=(Qx)^*(Qx)\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad D_1x=hx.$$ By closure $D_1=h1$ on $e_x\\mathcal H$. The support of $H$ lies there. Equations <a href=\"#eq:four-r\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-r\">[eq:four-r]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:four-ultraproduct\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-ultraproduct\">[eq:four-ultraproduct]</a> give $$\\label{eq:four-isometry-relations}\n r_i^*r_j=\\delta_{ij}H^2,\\qquad Hr_i=r_i\n \\quad(i,j=1,2).$$ Also $HV=V\\ne0$, so $H\\ne0$ and $r_2\\ne0$.\n\nThe conditional calculation preceding <a href=\"#eq:four-P0\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-P0\">[eq:four-P0]</a> now applies: $v=r_1+b_0$ is an isometry with $v^*r_2=0$. Its defect projection $$P=1-vv^*$$ is nonzero, since $Pr_2=r_2\\ne0$.\n\n\nWe check that this projection is the represented fixed element $P_0$, a point for which both sides of the cutoff matter. The scalar ramps give $KH=H$. From $Hr_1=r_1$ follows $Kr_1=r_1$, and the definition $r_1=h^{-1/2}B_1^*H$ gives $r_1K=r_1$. Also $K$ commutes with $b_0$. Expanding the defect gives $$P=H^2-r_1r_1^*-r_1b_0-b_0r_1^*.$$ Each term is fixed by multiplication by $K$ on both sides. Therefore $KPK=P$, and $\\chi(P)=P$ because $P$ is a projection. Formula <a href=\"#eq:four-P0\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-P0\">[eq:four-P0]</a> yields $$\\label{eq:four-P0-is-P}\n P_0=P.$$\n\n\nNow $x'=(P_0-1/2)_+=P/2$. Put $D_3=B_3B_3^*\\le h1$; then $Q'=h1-D_3$. The relation $R=0$ gives $Q'x'=0$, so $$\\label{eq:four-last-contradiction}\n D_3P=hP.$$ Equations <a href=\"#eq:four-B4-support\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-B4-support\">[eq:four-B4-support]</a> and <a href=\"#eq:four-P0-is-P\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-P0-is-P\">[eq:four-P0-is-P]</a> say $B_4=PB_4$. But $B_3^*B_4=0$, so $D_3B_4=0$, whereas <a href=\"#eq:four-last-contradiction\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-last-contradiction\">[eq:four-last-contradiction]</a> gives $D_3B_4=hB_4$. Thus $B_4=0$, and $B_3^*B_3=B_4^*B_4$ gives $B_3=0$. This contradicts <a href=\"#eq:four-last-contradiction\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-last-contradiction\">[eq:four-last-contradiction]</a>, since $h>0$ and $P\\ne0$.\n\nThe contradiction shows that some index has no representation satisfying <a href=\"#eq:four-forbidden\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:four-forbidden\">[eq:four-forbidden]</a>. The four entries chosen at that index prove the proposition. ◻",
          "source_id": "PM-303.1"
        }

        {
          "anchor": "proof-stmt-lem-uniform-matrix-bound-2",
          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-lem-uniform-matrix-bound</summary>\n\n\n*Proof of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:uniform-gap\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:uniform-gap\">24</a>.* If the Proposition were false, for every integer $j\\geq1$ there would be a unital exact algebra $B_j$ and a contraction $v_j\\in B_j$ satisfying <a href=\"#eq:forbidden-models\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:forbidden-models\">[eq:forbidden-models]</a> with $1/j$ in place of $\\gamma$. Let $\\Phi_j:\\mathcal U\\to B_j$ send $u$ to $v_j$. Fix a finite-dimensional polynomial space $E$. Before choosing any matrix amplification, tensor factor, compression, or ideal, choose one integer $j=j(E)\\geq\\max\\{1,2C_E\\}$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:uniform-matrix-bound\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:uniform-matrix-bound\">27</a> then gives $$\\label{eq:fixed-j-matrix-bound}\n \\|Z\\|\\leq2\\|\\Phi_j^{(r)}(Z)\\|\n \\qquad(r\\geq1,\\ Z\\in M_r(E)).$$\n\n\nThis same $j$ works after tensoring with an arbitrary $C^*$-algebra $C$: for every $z\\in E\\odot C$, $$\\label{eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound}\n \\|z\\|\\leq2\\|(\\Phi_j\\otimes\\operatorname{id}_C)z\\|,$$ where $\\odot$ denotes the algebraic tensor product and all norms are spatial. The assertion is immediate if $C=0$. Otherwise, represent $C$ faithfully on a nonzero Hilbert space $K$, and use faithful representations of $\\mathcal U$ and $B_j$ for their respective spatial tensor products. Compress the $C$ representation to a nonzero finite-dimensional subspace $K_0\\subset K$. If $z=\\sum_i P_i\\otimes c_i$, its compression has coefficients $P_{K_0}c_i|_{K_0}\\in M_d(\\mathbb C)$ and, after interchanging the tensor factors, belongs to $M_d(E)$. Inequality <a href=\"#eq:fixed-j-matrix-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:fixed-j-matrix-bound\">[eq:fixed-j-matrix-bound]</a> bounds its norm by twice the norm of the corresponding compression on the $B_j$ side, which is at most $2\\|(\\Phi_j\\otimes\\operatorname{id}_C)z\\|$. The norms of these compressions have supremum $\\|z\\|$: as $K_0$ increases, the projections $1\\otimes P_{K_0}$ converge strongly to the identity, and their two-sided compressions converge strongly to $z$. This proves <a href=\"#eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound\">[eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound]</a>.\n\nNow fix any ideal $I\\subset C$, with quotient map $q:C\\to C/I$ and a positive contractive approximate unit $(e_\\lambda)$ of $I$. For $z\\in E\\odot C$, every $z(1\\otimes(1-e_\\lambda))$ still belongs to $E\\odot C$. Consequently <a href=\"#eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound\">[eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound]</a> applies to the entire net with the single index $j(E)$. Formula <a href=\"#eq:tensor-quotient-norm\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:tensor-quotient-norm\">[eq:tensor-quotient-norm]</a> and exactness of $B_j$ give $$\\begin{aligned}\n \\|z+\\mathcal U\\otimes_{\\min}I\\|\n &=\\lim_\\lambda\\|z(1\\otimes(1-e_\\lambda))\\|\\\\\n &\\leq2\\lim_\\lambda\n   \\|(\\Phi_j\\otimes\\operatorname{id}_C)(z)(1\\otimes(1-e_\\lambda))\\|\\\\\n &=2\\|(\\Phi_j\\otimes\\operatorname{id}_{C/I})((\\operatorname{id}_\\mathcal U\\otimes q)z)\\|\\\\\n &\\leq2\\|(\\operatorname{id}_\\mathcal U\\otimes q)z\\|.\n\\end{aligned}$$ Finite sums of polynomial tensors are dense in $\\mathcal U\\otimes_{\\min}C$. Although $j(E)$ may vary with the polynomial space, the resulting constant $2$ does not. The last inequality therefore extends by continuity to every $z\\in\\mathcal U\\otimes_{\\min}C$. It says that the canonical surjective homomorphism $$\\kappa:(\\mathcal U\\otimes_{\\min}C)/(\\mathcal U\\otimes_{\\min}I)\n \\longrightarrow\\mathcal U\\otimes_{\\min}(C/I)$$ has zero kernel: if $\\kappa(z+\\mathcal U\\otimes_{\\min}I)=0$, the quotient class has norm zero. An injective $*$-homomorphism of $C^*$-algebras is isometric, so the fixed factor $2$ suffices to prove that $\\kappa$ is an isomorphism. Since $C,I$ were arbitrary, $\\mathcal U$ would be exact, contrary to Lemma <a href=\"#lem:nonexact-universal\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:nonexact-universal\">25</a>. ◻",

          "exact_quote": "<summary>Proof of stmt-prop-square-zero</summary>\n\n\n*Proof of Theorem <a href=\"#thm:exact-infiniteness\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:exact-infiniteness\">2</a>.* Fix $a\\in C_+$ and $r>0$. The set $K_0=K(a,r)$ is quasi-compact by Lemma <a href=\"#lem:primitive-compactness\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:primitive-compactness\">34</a>. If it is empty, every primitive image of $(a-r)_+$ is zero, so $(a-r)_+=0$, and $((a-r)_+)^{\\oplus2}\\precsim a$ is immediate. Suppose that $K_0$ is nonempty. Proposition <a href=\"#prop:square-zero\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:square-zero\">36</a> applied to $\\operatorname{Her}(a)$ gives $x\\in\\operatorname{Her}(a)$ with $x^2=0$ and $x_P\\ne0$ throughout $K_0$. Set $z_1=x^*x$. Its Cuntz-equivalent positive $xx^*$ is orthogonal to it, since $(x^*x)(xx^*)=x^*x^2x^*=0$. Therefore $$z_1^{\\oplus2}\\sim_{\\mathrm{Cu}}z_1\\oplus xx^*\n \\sim_{\\mathrm{Cu}}z_1+xx^*\\precsim a,\n \\qquad (z_1)_P\\ne0\\quad(P\\in K_0).$$ Repeat the construction inside $\\operatorname{Her}(z_1)$, and then inside successive hereditary subalgebras, always over the same $K_0$. It yields $z_j\\in C_+$ nonzero throughout $K_0$ and $z_{j+1}^{\\oplus2}\\precsim z_j$. Finite direct sums and transitivity give $$z_j^{\\oplus 2^j}\\precsim a.$$ Choose a finite $j\\ge1$ with $2^j\\ge n$. Dropping extra summands gives $z_j^{\\oplus n}\\precsim a$. If $(z_j)_P=0$, then $P\\notin K_0$, so $\\|a_P\\|<r$ and $((a-r)_+)_P=0$. The primitive ideal test in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:ideals-cutoffs\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:ideals-cutoffs\">6</a> therefore gives $(a-r)_+\\in\\operatorname{Id}(z_j)$. Condition <span class=\"upright\">(F$_n$)</span> makes $z_j^{\\oplus n}$ properly infinite because $z_j$ is a scalar positive element. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:ideal-comparison\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:ideal-comparison\">28</a>, used with two copies on the left, then implies $$((a-r)_+)^{\\oplus2}\\precsim z_j^{\\oplus n}\\precsim a.$$ Only scalar positives $z_j\\in C$ have been tested against <span class=\"upright\">(F$_n$)</span>. Finally, $\\|a-(a-r)_+\\|\\le r$. As $r\\downarrow0$, norm-limit closure of comparison with the fixed target $a$ gives $$\\label{eq:individual-infiniteness}\n a\\oplus a\\precsim a\\qquad(a\\in C_+).$$ This includes $a=0$; when $n=1$, it also follows directly from the hypothesis. Therefore $C$ satisfies <span class=\"upright\">(P)</span>. Theorem <a href=\"#thm:individual-to-strong\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:individual-to-strong\">1</a> now gives strong pure infiniteness, completing the proof. ◻",

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 303 (`lean/docs/303.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Weak pure infiniteness and $\mathcal O_\infty$ absorption](../../preprints/Weak-pure-infiniteness-and-O-infinity-absorption-September-25-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization proves that a complex $C^*$-algebra in which every positive element is properly infinite is strongly purely infinite, with the full positive-element diagonalization property. No exactness, nuclearity, unitality, or simplicity assumption is needed for this implication.
>
> For exact algebras, proper infiniteness of one fixed finite amplification of every positive element already implies individual proper infiniteness and strong pure infiniteness.

Comparator source `ExactInfiniteness.lean` (7117 bytes, sha256 `f2faec1c49b77624bbe2aa3d5f34a4e6791f626d104ba675401e167ec37bfe06`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
universe u v
open scoped TensorProduct ENNReal ComplexOrder
open UniformSpace
namespace WeakPureInfiniteness

section CompletedOperators

variable {domain codomain : Type*}
  [NormedAddCommGroup domain] [NormedSpace ℂ domain]
  [NormedAddCommGroup codomain] [NormedSpace ℂ codomain]

lemma norm_completion_le (operator : domain →L[ℂ] codomain) :
    ‖operator.completion‖ ≤ ‖operator‖ := by
  apply ContinuousLinearMap.opNorm_le_bound _ (norm_nonneg operator)
  intro vector
  induction vector using Completion.induction_on with
  | hp => exact isClosed_le (by fun_prop) (by fun_prop)
  | ih vector => simpa using operator.le_opNorm vector

lemma norm_completion (operator : domain →L[ℂ] codomain) :
    ‖operator.completion‖ = ‖operator‖ := by
  apply le_antisymm (norm_completion_le operator)
  apply ContinuousLinearMap.opNorm_le_bound _ (norm_nonneg _)
  intro vector
  simpa using operator.completion.le_opNorm (vector : Completion domain)

end CompletedOperators

structure ComplexHilbert where
  Carrier : Type u
  [normedAddCommGroup : NormedAddCommGroup Carrier]
  [innerProductSpace : InnerProductSpace ℂ Carrier]
  [completeSpace : CompleteSpace Carrier]

attribute [instance] ComplexHilbert.normedAddCommGroup
  ComplexHilbert.innerProductSpace ComplexHilbert.completeSpace
instance : CoeSort ComplexHilbert.{u} (Type u) := ⟨ComplexHilbert.Carrier⟩

instance completedTensorCStar (H K : ComplexHilbert.{u}) :
    CStarAlgebra (Completion (H ⊗[ℂ] K) →L[ℂ] Completion (H ⊗[ℂ] K)) := inferInstance

instance hilbertOperatorsCStar (H : ComplexHilbert.{u}) :
    CStarAlgebra (H →L[ℂ] H) := inferInstance

structure SpatialRepresentation (A : Type u) (B : Type v)
    [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra B] where
  left : ComplexHilbert.{max u v}
  right : ComplexHilbert.{max u v}
  leftRep : A →⋆ₙₐ[ℂ] (left →L[ℂ] left)
  rightRep : B →⋆ₙₐ[ℂ] (right →L[ℂ] right)

abbrev SpatialRepresentation.hilbert {A : Type u} {B : Type v}
    [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra B]
    (π : SpatialRepresentation A B) : ComplexHilbert.{max u v} where
  Carrier := Completion (π.left ⊗[ℂ] π.right)

abbrev SpatialRepresentation.space {A : Type u} {B : Type v}
    [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra B]
    (π : SpatialRepresentation A B) := Completion (π.left ⊗[ℂ] π.right)

def SpatialAmbient (A : Type u) (B : Type v)
    [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra B] : Type (max u v + 1) :=
  lp (fun π : SpatialRepresentation A B => π.space →L[ℂ] π.space) ∞

instance spatialAmbientCStar (A : Type u) (B : Type v)
    [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra B] :
    NonUnitalCStarAlgebra (SpatialAmbient A B) := by
  unfold SpatialAmbient
  infer_instance

instance spatialAmbientCoeFun (A : Type u) (B : Type v)
    [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra B] :
    CoeFun (SpatialAmbient A B) (fun _ => ∀ π : SpatialRepresentation A B, π.space →L[ℂ] π.space) :=
  ⟨fun z => z.val⟩

def spatialElementary {A : Type u} {B : Type v}
    [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra B]
    (a : A) (b : B) : SpatialAmbient A B :=
  ⟨fun π => (TensorProduct.mapL (π.leftRep a) (π.rightRep b)).completion,
    memℓp_infty ⟨‖a‖ * ‖b‖, by
      rintro _ ⟨π, rfl⟩
      dsimp only
      rw [norm_completion]
      exact (TensorProduct.norm_mapL_le _ _).trans
        (mul_le_mul (NonUnitalStarAlgHom.norm_apply_le π.leftRep a) (NonUnitalStarAlgHom.norm_apply_le π.rightRep b)
          (norm_nonneg _) (norm_nonneg _))⟩⟩

def minimalTensorSubalgebra (A : Type u) (B : Type v)
    [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra B] :
    NonUnitalStarSubalgebra ℂ (SpatialAmbient A B) :=
  (NonUnitalStarAlgebra.adjoin ℂ (Set.range fun p : A × B => spatialElementary p.1 p.2)).topologicalClosure

abbrev MinimalTensor (A : Type u) (B : Type v)
    [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra B] := minimalTensorSubalgebra A B

instance (A : Type u) (B : Type v) [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra B] :
    NonUnitalCStarAlgebra (MinimalTensor A B) := by
  letI : IsClosed ((minimalTensorSubalgebra A B : NonUnitalStarSubalgebra ℂ
    (SpatialAmbient A B)) : Set (SpatialAmbient A B)) :=
    isClosed_closure
  exact inferInstance

section Exactness

variable {A : Type u} {C D : Type v}
  [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra C] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra D]

def SpatialRepresentation.precompRight (π : SpatialRepresentation A D)
    (q : C →⋆ₙₐ[ℂ] D) : SpatialRepresentation A C where
  left := π.left
  right := π.right
  leftRep := π.leftRep
  rightRep := π.rightRep.comp q

def tensorKernelIdeal (q : C →⋆ₙₐ[ℂ] D) : NonUnitalStarSubalgebra ℂ (SpatialAmbient A C) :=
  (NonUnitalStarAlgebra.adjoin ℂ
    {z | ∃ a : A, ∃ c : C, q c = 0 ∧ z = spatialElementary a c}).topologicalClosure

end Exactness

def Exact (A : Type u) [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] : Prop :=
  ∀ (C D : Type u) [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra C] [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra D]
    (q : C →⋆ₙₐ[ℂ] D), Function.Surjective q →
    ∀ z : MinimalTensor A C,
      (∀ π : SpatialRepresentation A D, (z.val : SpatialAmbient A C) (π.precompRight q) = 0) →
      z.val ∈ tensorKernelIdeal (A := A) q

section Definitions

variable {A : Type*} [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [PartialOrder A] [StarOrderedRing A]
variable {m k : Type*} [Fintype m] [Fintype k]

def CuntzSubequiv (a : CStarMatrix m m A) (b : CStarMatrix k k A) : Prop :=
  ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε → ∃ v : CStarMatrix k m A, ‖v.conjTranspose * b * v - a‖ < ε

def blockSum (a : CStarMatrix m m A) (b : CStarMatrix k k A) :
    CStarMatrix (m ⊕ k) (m ⊕ k) A :=
  CStarMatrix.ofMatrix (Matrix.fromBlocks (CStarMatrix.ofMatrix.symm a) 0 0
    (CStarMatrix.ofMatrix.symm b))

def ProperlyInfinite (a : CStarMatrix m m A) : Prop :=
  CuntzSubequiv (blockSum a a) a

def diagonalCopies (n : ℕ) (a : A) : CStarMatrix (Fin n) (Fin n) A :=
  CStarMatrix.ofMatrix (Matrix.diagonal fun _ => a)

variable (A)

def WeaklyPurelyInfinite : Prop :=
  ∃ n : ℕ, 1 ≤ n ∧ ∀ a : A, 0 ≤ a → a ≠ 0 → ProperlyInfinite (diagonalCopies n a)

def StronglyPurelyInfinite : Prop :=
  ∀ a b : A, 0 ≤ a → 0 ≤ b → ∀ ε : ℝ, 0 < ε →
    ∃ s t : A, ‖star s * (a * a) * s - a * a‖ < ε ∧
      ‖star t * (b * b) * t - b * b‖ < ε ∧ ‖star s * a * b * t‖ < ε

end Definitions

theorem exact_weaklyPurelyInfinite_individuallyProperlyInfinite_any
    {A : Type u} [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [PartialOrder A] [StarOrderedRing A]
    (hA : Exact A) (hW : WeaklyPurelyInfinite A) {a : A} (ha : 0 ≤ a) :
    ProperlyInfinite (diagonalCopies 1 a) := by
  sorry

theorem exact_weaklyPurelyInfinite_stronglyPurelyInfinite
    (A : Type u) [NonUnitalCStarAlgebra A] [PartialOrder A] [StarOrderedRing A]
    (hExact : Exact A) (hWeak : WeaklyPurelyInfinite A) : StronglyPurelyInfinite A := by
  sorry

end WeakPureInfiniteness

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove that every complex <i>C</i><sup>∗</sup>-algebra in which each positive element is properly infinite is strongly purely infinite. This answers the ordinary-to-strong part of Kirchberg–Rørdam's comparison question. For exact algebras, we also prove that proper infiniteness of one fixed finite amplification of every positive element implies proper infiniteness of each positive element. Consequently, every separable nuclear algebra with this fixed-amplification property absorbs $`\mathcal O_\infty`$, without assumptions of unitality or simplicity.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Pure infiniteness expresses the ability of positive elements of a $C^*$-algebra to contain several copies of themselves in Cuntz comparison. For a nonsimple algebra, an individual comparison and the simultaneous comparison of two elements are different demands: the latter must also control a mixed entry. We prove that proper infiniteness of every positive element already provides this compatibility. We then give two ways to remove a fixed finite amplification, one for exact algebras and an independent construction for nuclear algebras. In the separable nuclear case the result yields absorption of the Cuntz algebra $\mathcal O_\infty$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Infiniteness notions and main results

All $C^*$-algebras are complex and may be nonunital. Let $\mathcal K$ denote the compact operators on an infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert space. For $u,v\in(C\otimes\mathcal K)_+$, write $u\precsim v$ if some sequence $x_j\in C\otimes\mathcal K$ satisfies $x_j^*vx_j\to u$ in norm, and write $u\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}v$ if comparison holds in both directions. This is stable Cuntz comparison (Cuntz 1978) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Cuntz1978](#ref-Cuntz1978)]. Finite matrices over $C$ occupy the standard corners of $C\otimes\mathcal K$; comparisons between different sizes use zero padding in the other coordinates. Write $h^{\oplus n}$ for the direct sum of $n$ copies of $h$. A positive element $h$ is *properly infinite* if $h\oplus h\precsim h$. We include zero as properly infinite. For $h\in C_+$, let $$\operatorname{Her}(h)=\overline{hCh},\qquad
 \operatorname{Id}(h)=\text{the closed two-sided ideal of \(C\) generated by \(h\)}.$$

We will use the following two comparison conditions: <a id="eq:property-P"></a><a id="eq:property-F"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &h\text{ is properly infinite for every }h\in C_+,
 \tag{P}\label{eq:property-P}\\
 &h^{\oplus n}\text{ is properly infinite for every }h\in C_+,
 \quad\text{for one fixed integer }n\ge1.
 \tag{\(F_n\)}\label{eq:property-F}
\end{aligned}$$ In $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$, the same $n$ is chosen before all positive elements are tested. It therefore applies to every scalar spectral cutoff used later. The premise concerns scalar positives in $C$, with their indicated amplifications.

<a id="eq:SPI-definition"></a>
Condition $\textup{(P)}$ is equivalent to pure infiniteness in the nonsimple sense of Kirchberg–Rørdam (Kirchberg and Rørdam 2000, Theorem 4.16) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[KR2000](#ref-KR2000)]; the equivalence is also recalled in (Schafhauser, Tikuisis, and White 2026, sec. 21) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[STW99problems](#ref-STW99problems)]. The existence of an $n$ satisfying $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$ is *weak pure infiniteness* in the fixed-amplification sense. We use the following formulation of *strong pure infiniteness*, valid for arbitrary $C^*$-algebras (Kirchberg and Sierakowski 2016, Definition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[KS](#ref-KS)]: for every $x,y\in C_+$ and $\varepsilon>0$, there are $s,t\in C$ such that $$\label{eq:SPI-definition}
 \|s^*x^2s-x^2\|<\varepsilon,\qquad
 \|t^*y^2t-y^2\|<\varepsilon,\qquad
 \|s^*xy t\|<\varepsilon.$$ The coefficients in this formulation have no prescribed norm bound.

Kirchberg–Rørdam introduced the weak and strong notions in (Kirchberg and Rørdam 2002) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[KR2002](#ref-KR2002)]. Strong pure infiniteness implies ordinary pure infiniteness, and $\textup{(P)}$ implies $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$ with $n=1$. The reverse implications form the comparison question in Kirchberg–Rørdam’s Question 9.5, as recalled in (Schafhauser, Tikuisis, and White 2026, sec. 21, Problem LXXII) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[STW99problems](#ref-STW99problems)]. In version 2 of that problem collection, the question asks whether the three notions agree in general and in the nuclear setting. Our first theorem proves the implication from ordinary to strong pure infiniteness for arbitrary complex $C^*$-algebras, in the following more flexible form.

<a id="stmt-thm-individual-to-strong"></a>
<div id="thm:individual-to-strong" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:main-diagonalization"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *Let $C$ be a complex $C^*$-algebra satisfying $\textup{(P)}$. For all $a,b\in C_+$, $c\in C$, and $\varepsilon>0$, there are $s,t\in C$ such that $$\label{eq:main-diagonalization}
 \|s^*as-a\|<\varepsilon,\qquad
 \|t^*bt-b\|<\varepsilon,\qquad
 \|s^*ct\|<\varepsilon.$$ Consequently $C$ is strongly purely infinite.*

</div>

The cross term $c$ is arbitrary and need not be a product of the diagonal inputs. Taking $(a,b,c)=(x^2,y^2,xy)$ gives the stated consequence. Arbitrary-entry diagonalization is a known equivalent form of strong pure infiniteness (Kirchberg and Sierakowski 2016, Remarks 3.2(i)–(ii)) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[KS](#ref-KS)]; our proof obtains it directly from $\textup{(P)}$. It also gives coefficient bounds for positive contraction inputs that are independent of the requested mixed-entry accuracy; see Proposition <a href="#prop:bounded-diagonalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bounded-diagonalization">16</a>.

Recall that a $C^*$-algebra is exact if its minimal tensor product preserves short exact sequences. Exactness supplies the additional uniformity needed to remove the fixed amplification.

<a id="stmt-thm-exact-infiniteness"></a>
<div id="thm:exact-infiniteness" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2**. *Let $C$ be an exact complex $C^*$-algebra. Suppose that there is one integer $n\ge1$ such that $h^{\oplus n}$ is properly infinite for every $h\in C_+$. Then every positive element of $C$ is properly infinite. Consequently $C$ is strongly purely infinite.*

</div>

These theorems give a positive answer to the ordinary-to-strong part of Problem LXXII in general and to the full comparison question for exact algebras, hence also for nuclear algebras. The weak-to-ordinary implication for arbitrary nonexact algebras remains outside these conclusions. The statements include nonseparable and nonunital algebras. Section <a href="#sec:nuclear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:nuclear">6</a> gives an independent proof of the first conclusion of Theorem <a href="#thm:exact-infiniteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:exact-infiniteness">2</a> under nuclearity. Its statement is a restriction of that theorem, while its finite-tuple obstruction and merger of hereditary witnesses provide a different method. The same method extends to exact algebras by the deeper theorem that exactness passes to quotients; see Remark <a href="#rem:tuple-exact" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="rem:tuple-exact">23</a>. The direct exact proof avoids that permanence theorem.

Let $\mathcal O_\infty$ be the Cuntz algebra generated by countably many isometries with mutually orthogonal ranges. Kirchberg–Rørdam proved the equivalence between strong pure infiniteness and $\mathcal O_\infty$-absorption for separable nuclear algebras that are stable or have an approximate unit of projections (Kirchberg and Rørdam 2002) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[KR2002](#ref-KR2002)]. Kirchberg’s later central-sequence results cover general nonunital separable nuclear algebras (Kirchberg 2006, Proposition 4.4 and Remark 4.6) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Kirchberg2006](#ref-Kirchberg2006)]; see also the paragraph following (Kirchberg and Sierakowski 2016, Definition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[KS](#ref-KS)]. At that scope they give the following tensorial consequence.

<a id="stmt-cor-absorption"></a>
<div id="cor:absorption" class="corollary">

**Corollary 3**. *Let $A$ be a separable nuclear complex $C^*$-algebra. If there is one integer $n\ge1$ such that $h^{\oplus n}$ is properly infinite for every $h\in A_+$, then $$A\cong A\otimes_{\min}\mathcal O_\infty.$$*

</div>

For separable nuclear algebras, therefore, weak pure infiniteness, pure infiniteness, strong pure infiniteness, and $\mathcal O_\infty$-absorption are equivalent. The reverse implication from absorption to strong pure infiniteness is due to Kirchberg–Rørdam (Kirchberg and Rørdam 2002) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[KR2002](#ref-KR2002)]; see also the discussion after (Kirchberg and Sierakowski 2016, Definition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[KS](#ref-KS)]. Thus a single finite amplification test on positive elements characterizes this tensorial regularity even when the algebra is nonsimple or nonunital.

For example, the cone $C_0((0,1])\otimes\mathcal O_\infty$ is separable nuclear and absorbs $\mathcal O_\infty$ by the self-absorption of $\mathcal O_\infty$. It is nonsimple, nonunital, and has no nonzero projections: the norm of a continuous projection-valued function takes only the values zero and one and must tend to zero at the missing endpoint.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Earlier results and the role of ideal structure

The comparison question has been settled under several structural conditions. Kirchberg–Rørdam treat simple, real-rank-zero, and approximately divisible cases (Kirchberg and Rørdam 2002) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[KR2002](#ref-KR2002)]; the real-rank-zero conclusion is also recalled in (Schafhauser, Tikuisis, and White 2026, sec. 21) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[STW99problems](#ref-STW99problems)]. Blanchard–Kirchberg prove that ordinary pure infiniteness implies strong pure infiniteness when the primitive ideal space is Hausdorff (Blanchard and Kirchberg 2004, Theorem 5.8) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BlanchardKirchberg2004](#ref-BlanchardKirchberg2004)]. Rørdam’s proof of (Rørdam 2004, Theorem 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Rordam2004Z](#ref-Rordam2004Z)] gives the same implication for $\mathcal Z$-absorbing purely infinite algebras, where $\mathcal Z$ is the Jiang–Su algebra. These two implications do not require exactness or nuclearity.

Pasnicu–Rørdam establish equivalence of the three infiniteness notions for algebras with the ideal property, meaning that projections separate ideals (Pasnicu and Rørdam 2007, Proposition 2.14) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[PasnicuRordam2007](#ref-PasnicuRordam2007)]. For algebras of topological dimension zero, Pasnicu–Rouzbehani prove the implication from ordinary to strong pure infiniteness (Pasnicu and Rouzbehani 2025, Theorems 3.10(i) and 3.13) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[PasnicuRouzbehani2025](#ref-PasnicuRouzbehani2025)]. Here topological dimension zero means that the primitive ideal space has a basis of compact open sets. Theorem <a href="#thm:individual-to-strong" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:individual-to-strong">1</a> requires no restriction on this space or on the ideal structure.

The weak-to-ordinary implication is closely related to the problem of finding sufficiently many orthogonal equivalent elements in a hereditary subalgebra. Kirchberg–Rørdam showed that the Global Glimm Halving property permits this implication (Kirchberg and Rørdam 2002, Proposition 4.15) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[KR2002](#ref-KR2002)]; see (Schafhauser, Tikuisis, and White 2026, Theorem 39) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[STW99problems](#ref-STW99problems)]. An equivalent formulation of the Global Glimm Property is that, for every $a\in C_+$ and $\varepsilon>0$, there is $x\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ with $x^2=0$ such that $(a-\varepsilon)_+$ belongs to the closed two-sided ideal generated by $x$ (Thiel and Vilalta 2023, Remark 3.2 and Theorem 3.6(3)) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[TV2023](#ref-TV2023)]. Elliott–Rouzbehani prove weak-to-ordinary pure infiniteness in topological dimension zero (Elliott and Rouzbehani 2023) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[ER2023](#ref-ER2023)]. Ng–Thiel–Vilalta recover that implication through the Global Glimm Property in a proof that includes nonseparable algebras (Ng, Thiel, and Vilalta 2026, Theorem 2.3 and Corollary 2.5) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[NTV2026](#ref-NTV2026)]. The initial and range squares of a square-zero element are orthogonal and Cuntz equivalent. Our exact argument constructs such elements while retaining a nonzero image throughout a prescribed set of primitive quotients. The relevant sets are the norm slices $\{P\in\operatorname{Prim}(C):\|h+P\|\ge r\}$, where $h\in C_+$ and $r>0$; they are quasi-compact even when $\operatorname{Prim}(C)$ is not Hausdorff. Repeated halving on the same slice then supplies enough copies to remove the fixed amplification. The nuclear argument instead combines representation-dependent positive elements into one element that covers a prescribed cutoff.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Proof strategy and the independent routes

Two tasks drive the argument. To pass from $\textup{(P)}$ to strong pure infiniteness, one must choose two hereditary corners that retain the relevant parts of their inputs while making the mixed term small. To remove $n$ from $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$, one must first produce sufficiently many equivalent orthogonal pieces inside a scalar hereditary subalgebra. Figure <a href="#fig:proof-routes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:proof-routes">1</a> separates these tasks and the two methods for the second one.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Comparison transport.

Section <a href="#sec:foundations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:foundations">2</a> supplies the common tool. A Cuntz comparison, after a spectral cutoff, can be implemented by a rectangular partial isometry in the bidual. Its products with the cutoff hereditary algebra lie in the original algebra, and conjugation preserves norms in every representation, also at matrix levels. Thus one can place several orthogonal copies in a hereditary subalgebra and transport an entire finite tuple through the same map. These are stronger implementation data than a comparison of classes alone.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### From individual to simultaneous comparison.

For $h\ge0$, let $e_h$ be its support projection in the universal bidual. Starting from positive contractions $a,b$ and a contraction $c$, the first goal is to find positive contractions $p\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$, $q\in\operatorname{Her}(b)$ with $$\|e_pce_q\|<1,$$ while requiring represented norm one of $p$ or $q$ whenever the corresponding input has represented norm greater than $1/2$. This retention of represented norms is what later gives ideal comparison.

Section <a href="#sec:graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:graph">3</a> builds one corner as a graph inside several orthogonal copies of a cutoff of $a$. The graph has finitely many freely chosen algebra entries. If the positive defect $b(1-c^*p^2c)b$ vanishes in a representation that detects the relevant part of $b$, equality in contraction inequalities forces all of those entries to equal fixed measurements, computed before the entries were chosen. Under $\textup{(P)}$, Section <a href="#sec:four-variables" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:four-variables">4</a> excludes these equalities with four entries. The first pair forces an isometry with a nonzero defect, using the scaling-element construction of Blackadar–Cuntz (B. E. Blackadar and Cuntz 1982; Pasnicu and Rørdam 2007) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[BlackadarCuntz1982](#ref-BlackadarCuntz1982), [PasnicuRordam2007](#ref-PasnicuRordam2007)]; the second pair is forced both into and away from that defect. Spectral cutoffs place the relevant supports in actual operator ranges, and a directed family of finite tests makes the contradiction work without separability. Two ideal cutoffs then give the strict support estimate above.

Section <a href="#sec:separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:separation">5</a> upgrades strict contraction to arbitrarily small compression. Uniformly bounded comparison rows show that $\textup{(P)}$ passes to bounded products. A product contradiction therefore supplies one contraction factor below one, and iteration preserves both represented norm signals while reducing the mixed compression. Finally, high spectral parts of $a,b$ are compared into the separated corners. The coefficients have bounds determined by the diagonal cutoffs, independent of the requested mixed-entry accuracy. This order of choices completes Theorem <a href="#thm:individual-to-strong" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:individual-to-strong">1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### A separate nuclear proof of amplification removal.

Section <a href="#sec:nuclear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:nuclear">6</a> reuses the graph construction with a different obstruction. A finite tuple witnessing nonexactness has linear matrix norms that no tuple in an exact algebra can realize at every matrix level. Finite-dimensional compressions nevertheless approximate each fixed norm. Under $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$, one rectangular carrier transports each entire compressed tuple into $M_n(C)$. If every choice failed to exclude the graph relations, their limiting norms would occur in a quotient of $M_n(C)$. Nuclearity makes this quotient nuclear, hence exact, giving a contradiction. The matrix norm and fixed-element quotient arguments are related to Pisier’s operator-space localization methods (Pisier 1995, 162–65) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Pisier1995](#ref-Pisier1995)].

It remains to obtain the positive inputs that the graph needs and combine them. The fixed-amplification hypothesis and Kadison transitivity (Kadison 1957) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Kadison1957](#ref-Kadison1957)] give, at each irreducible representation detecting a positive $u$, a positive contraction in $\operatorname{Her}(u)$ whose every spectral cutoff is properly infinite and whose norm in that representation is one. This element may depend on the representation. Repeated graph shrinking and a polar deformation merge finitely many such elements while retaining all their norm signals. A finite ideal argument covers each cutoff of $u$ by one merged element and then compares two copies of that cutoff back to $u$. This proves $\textup{(P)}$ under nuclearity, independently of the exact argument. The use of hereditary elements to cover ideals is related to the filling-family criteria of (Kirchberg and Sierakowski 2016, Theorem 1.1 and Definitions 4.2, 4.7) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[KS](#ref-KS)]; here the merger supplies the simultaneous norm control.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### The exact proof and its quantitative completion.

Section <a href="#sec:exactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:exactness">7</a> starts from the nonexact universal unital $C^*$-algebra of one contraction. Its finite matrix tests and its embedded copy of $C^*(\mathbb F_2)$ were studied by Courtney–Sherman (Courtney and Sherman 2020) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[CS](#ref-CS)], with Wassermann’s nonexact free-group example as a predecessor (Wassermann 1976) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Wassermann1976](#ref-Wassermann1976)]. Finite-word compression at all matrix levels turns nonexactness into one positive error threshold: no contraction in a unital exact algebra can contain approximately reducing copies of every finite matrix contraction. The same copy must approximately intertwine both the contraction and its adjoint.

Combining this obstruction with $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$ gives a perturbation that prevents cancellation simultaneously in every primitive quotient of a prescribed norm slice. Its lower norm bound is proportional to the perturbation size, with a constant independent of the slice and of $n$. Section <a href="#sec:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:localization">8</a> applies this estimate through three finite coupling steps. For a hereditary subalgebra nonzero on a prescribed quasi-compact subset of $\operatorname{Prim}(C)$, it constructs a square-zero element nonzero at every point of that subset. Repeated halving on one fixed norm slice puts $n$ copies of a positive element below the input in Cuntz comparison; ideal comparison and a cutoff limit prove $\textup{(P)}$. The general theorem then completes Theorem <a href="#thm:exact-infiniteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:exact-infiniteness">2</a>.

Section <a href="#sec:quantitative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:quantitative">9</a> gives an alternative final step under exactness. It constructs positives in two prescribed hereditary subalgebras with arbitrarily small product, while retaining a quotient norm floor fixed before the product error is chosen. Two compatible inverse perturbations preserve the two floors. Clipping the later comparison coefficients bounds their norms, so the small product survives implementation and gives strong pure infiniteness directly. Finally, for a separable nuclear algebra, the central-sequence absorption criterion applied in Section <a href="#sec:nuclear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:nuclear">6</a> gives Corollary <a href="#cor:absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:absorption">3</a>.

<a id="sec:foundations"></a>
### Comparison and transport

The diagonalization argument repeatedly places several copies of a positive element inside one hereditary subalgebra. Comparison of Cuntz classes alone does not record enough information for this use: the copies must have a common implementation, their products must remain in the algebra, and their norms must agree in every representation. We establish these facts here, together with the ideal and cutoff rules used to retain norm signals.

Cuntz introduced the comparison relation in (Cuntz 1978) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Cuntz1978](#ref-Cuntz1978)]. The proper-target ideal comparison in Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-rules" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-rules">4</a>(iii) is the standard consequence recorded in (Kirchberg and Rørdam 2000, Proposition 3.5(ii)) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[KR2000](#ref-KR2000)]; it is also used with that reference in (Kwaśniewski and Meyer 2023, proof of Proposition 2.38) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[KwasniewskiMeyer2023](#ref-KwasniewskiMeyer2023)]. The cutoff implementation below is related to Rørdam’s comparison lemma (Rørdam 1992, Proposition 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Rordam1992](#ref-Rordam1992)], in the form recalled in (Antoine, Perera, and Thiel 2020, sec. 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[AntoinePereraThiel2020](#ref-AntoinePereraThiel2020)]. We give the rectangular and representation statements needed here with their proofs.

All comparisons below are stable: finite positive matrices are placed in a common stabilization by padding with zeros. For a $C^*$-algebra $D$ and $h\in D_+$, let $\operatorname{Id}_D(h)$ denote the closed two-sided ideal generated by $h$, and put $$\operatorname{Her}_D(h)=\overline{hDh},\qquad
 e_h=\mathbf 1_{(0,\infty)}(h)\in D^{**}.$$ We omit the subscript when the ambient algebra is clear. Every support projection is taken in the universal bidual. Rectangular matrices over $C^{**}$ are viewed in the corresponding corners of a matrix algebra over $C^{**}$. The normal extension of a representation sends $e_h$ to $e_{\pi(h)}$: apply normality to $h(h+1/j)^{-1}\uparrow e_h$. When using a unit or a support projection in a degenerate representation, we restrict to its essential subspace. This leaves the norm of every represented algebra element unchanged.

<a id="stmt-lem-comparison-rules"></a>
<div id="lem:comparison-rules" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Elementary comparison rules). *Let $D$ be a $C^*$-algebra.*

1.  *For $h\in D_+$, $$\operatorname{Her}_D(h)=D\cap e_hD^{**}e_h.$$ If $0\le u\le K v$ for $u,v\in D_+$ and $K\ge0$, then $u\in\operatorname{Her}(v)$ and $u\precsim v$. More generally, every positive element of $\operatorname{Her}(v)$ is Cuntz subequivalent to $v$.*

2.  *For a rectangular algebra matrix $z$, one has $z^*z\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}zz^*$. Cuntz subequivalence is transitive, is preserved by block sums, and is closed under norm limits in its first variable when the second variable is fixed. Every $*$-homomorphism preserves Cuntz subequivalence and equivalence, after amplification. For integers $m,n\ge1$, $d\in M_n(D)_+$, and $v\in M_m(D)_+$, stable comparison $d\precsim v$ is equivalent to the existence of $r_j\in M_{m,n}(D)$ with $r_j^*vr_j\to d$ in norm.*

3.  *If $d,v$ are positive elements in a common stabilization, $d\in\operatorname{Id}(v)$, and $v$ is properly infinite, then $d\precsim v$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-comparison-rules"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-comparison-rules</summary>

*Proof.* We first prove the bidual description in (i), including its norm closure assertion. One inclusion follows on $hDh$ and then by closure. Conversely, suppose $u\in D_+$ and $e_hu=u$. Take increasing continuous positive contractions $f_j$ with $f_j(0)=0$ and $f_j(t)\uparrow1$ for $t>0$. On the compact state space of the unitization, the continuous functions $$\varphi\longmapsto
 \varphi\bigl(u(1-f_j(h))u\bigr)$$ decrease pointwise to zero. Indeed, the normal extension of each state evaluates the strong limit $f_j(h)\uparrow e_h$. Dini’s theorem makes this convergence uniform. The positive norm formula and $(1-f_j(h))^2\le1-f_j(h)$ then give $\|(1-f_j(h))u\|\to0$. Thus $u=\lim_j f_j(h)u f_j(h)\in\operatorname{Her}(h)$. Here $f_j(h)\in\operatorname{Her}(h)$ follows from continuous functional calculus, by first cutting $f_j$ off near zero. For a general element supported on both sides by $e_h$, apply the positive case to the positive and negative parts of its real and imaginary parts.

If $0\le u\le Kv$, compression by $1-e_v$ gives $u^{1/2}(1-e_v)=0$. Hence $u$ is supported by $e_v$ and belongs to $\operatorname{Her}(v)$. If $u\in\operatorname{Her}(v)_+$, take a cutoff approximate identity $g_j(v)$ for $\operatorname{Her}(v)$, with every $g_j$ zero near zero. The quotient function below is continuous when extended by zero at zero, and gives elements $$x_j=\frac{g_j(v)}{\sqrt v}\,u^{1/2}\in D,\qquad
 x_j^*v x_j=u^{1/2}g_j(v)^2u^{1/2}\longrightarrow u.$$ This proves the comparison assertion.

For (ii), the rectangular coefficient $z(z^*z+1/j)^{-1/2}$ compares $z^*z$ to $zz^*$, since its conjugation of $zz^*$ equals $$(z^*z)^2(z^*z+1/j)^{-1}\longrightarrow z^*z.$$ Apply the same calculation to $z^*$ for the reverse comparison. The inverses lie in unitizations and are multiplied by algebra matrices. Block-diagonal coefficients prove preservation under block sums. For transitivity, first approximate $u$ by $x^*vx$, then approximate $v$ by $y^*wy$ within the requested error divided by $\max\{1,\|x\|^2\}$; the coefficient $yx$ compares $u$ to $w$. A diagonal choice of coefficients proves closure under norm limits in the first variable.

If $\phi:D\to E$ is a $*$-homomorphism, apply $\phi\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal K}$ to a comparison sequence; continuity preserves its norm limit. Applying this in both directions preserves equivalence. For finite $d,v$, let $P_n,P_m$ be the scalar coordinate projections in the multiplier algebra of $D\otimes\mathcal K$. If $x_j^*vx_j\to d$ in the stabilization, then $$r_j=P_mx_jP_n\in M_{m,n}(D),\qquad
 r_j^*vr_j=P_nx_j^*vx_jP_n\longrightarrow d.$$ Conversely, rectangular coefficients embed in the stabilization and give the same convergence there. The multiplier compressions retain algebra membership without a unit, and the argument includes zero matrices.

For (iii), work in the common stabilization $D$. The closed span of $Dv^{1/2}D$ is $\operatorname{Id}_D(v)$, so finite sums $\sum_{i=1}^r x_i v^{1/2}y_i$ approximate $d^{1/2}$. For such a sum, define $$X=[x_1\ \cdots\ x_r]\in M_{1,r}(D),\qquad
 Y=[y_1\ \cdots\ y_r]^T\in M_{r,1}(D),$$ and put $V=\operatorname{diag}(v,\ldots,v)\in M_r(D)$. Then $$z=\sum_{i=1}^r x_i v^{1/2}y_i=X V^{1/2}Y.$$ The order and rectangular rules already proved give $$z^*z\le\|X\|^2Y^*VY,\qquad
 Y^*VY\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}V^{1/2}YY^*V^{1/2}\le\|Y\|^2V.$$ It follows that $z^*z\precsim V$. Proper infiniteness, block addition, and induction give $V\precsim v$ for every finite $r$. Now $z^*z\to d$, and the norm-limit rule yields $d\precsim v$. This proof includes $v=0$. ◻

</details>

In particular, Cuntz equivalence preserves proper infiniteness, and a block sum of properly infinite positives is properly infinite. These statements follow from block addition and transitivity. Also, $u\precsim v$ implies $\operatorname{Id}(u)\subset\operatorname{Id}(v)$, since each comparison approximant belongs to $\operatorname{Id}(v)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-transport"></a>
<div id="lem:transport" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:transport-supports"></a>
<a id="eq:transport-cutoffs"></a>
<a id="eq:transport-hereditary-image"></a>
<a id="eq:transport-represented-norm"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Transport after a cutoff). *Let $C$ be a $C^*$-algebra, let $m,n\ge1$ be integers, and let $d\in M_n(C)_+$ and $v\in M_m(C)_+$. Suppose $d\precsim v$ in $C\otimes\mathcal K$. For $\varepsilon>0$, put $h=(d-\varepsilon)_+$. There is $w\in M_{m,n}(C^{**})$ such that $$\label{eq:transport-supports}
 w^*w=e_h,\qquad ww^*\le e_v,$$ and <a id="eq:transport-membership"></a>$$\label{eq:transport-membership}
 w\operatorname{Her}_{M_n(C)}(h)\subset M_{m,n}(C),\qquad
 w\operatorname{Her}_{M_n(C)}(h)w^*\subset\operatorname{Her}_{M_m(C)}(v).$$ Conjugation by $w$ is an injective $*$-homomorphism on $\operatorname{Her}(h)$. For $l\in\operatorname{Her}(h)_+$, $$\label{eq:transport-cutoffs}
 (wlw^*-\tau)_+=w(l-\tau)_+w^*,\qquad
 w(l-\tau)_+w^*\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}(l-\tau)_+\quad(\tau\ge0),$$ and $$\label{eq:transport-hereditary-image}
 w\operatorname{Her}(l)w^*=\operatorname{Her}(wlw^*).$$ Every representation, with matching matrix amplifications, satisfies $$\label{eq:transport-represented-norm}
 \|\pi^{(m)}(wkw^*)\|=\|\pi^{(n)}(k)\|
 \qquad(k\in\operatorname{Her}(h)).$$ The products in these formulas are algebra elements; $w$ itself is only asserted to lie in the bidual.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transport"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transport</summary>

*Proof.* Compress a stabilization comparison coefficient to the first $m$ target and first $n$ source coordinates. These scalar multiplier projections give $s\in M_{m,n}(C)$ with $\|d-s^*vs\|<\varepsilon/2$. Put $z=v^{1/2}s h$. Order in the unitization and functional calculus for $d$ give <a id="eq:transport-order"></a>$$\label{eq:transport-order}
 \frac{\varepsilon}{2}h^2
 \le z^*z=h s^*vs h
 \le(\|d\|+\varepsilon/2)h^2.$$ For the lower bound, use $h d h\ge\varepsilon h^2$. The two inequalities and Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-rules" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-rules">4</a>(i) show $\operatorname{Her}(z^*z)=\operatorname{Her}(h)$. Consequently $\operatorname{Her}(|z|)=\operatorname{Her}(h)$ and $e_{z^*z}=e_h$. Let $z=w|z|$ be the rectangular polar decomposition in the bidual. Then $w^*w=e_h$; the factorization through $v^{1/2}$ gives $ww^*=e_{zz^*}\le e_v$.

For $k\in\operatorname{Her}(h)=\operatorname{Her}(|z|)$, take cutoff functions $g_j$ for $|z|$, zero near zero, with $g_j(|z|)k\to k$. Since $$w g_j(|z|)=z\,\frac{g_j(|z|)}{|z|}\in M_{m,n}(C),$$ norm closure gives $wk\in M_{m,n}(C)$. If $k\ge0$, then $wkw^*=(wk^{1/2})(wk^{1/2})^*$ belongs to $M_m(C)$. It is supported under $e_v$, hence belongs to $\operatorname{Her}(v)$. Linear decomposition handles arbitrary $k$. The identity $w^*w=e_h$ proves multiplicativity and injectivity. It also proves <a href="#eq:transport-cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-cutoffs">[eq:transport-cutoffs]</a>: functional calculus vanishing at zero commutes with the homomorphism, and the two squares of $w(l-\tau)_+^{1/2}$ are the asserted positives.

For <a href="#eq:transport-hereditary-image" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-hereditary-image">[eq:transport-hereditary-image]</a>, normal functional calculus gives $e_{wlw^*}=we_lw^*$. Thus the image of $\operatorname{Her}(l)$ is supported under $e_{wlw^*}$, which gives one inclusion by Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-rules" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-rules">4</a>(i). A sandwich by the image of $l$ has the form $$(wlw^*)b(wlw^*)
 =w\bigl((wl)^*b(wl)\bigr)w^*.$$ The inner algebra element is supported on both sides by $e_l$, so lies in $\operatorname{Her}(l)$. Since conjugation is an injective $*$-homomorphism on $\operatorname{Her}(l)$, its image is norm closed; taking closures gives the reverse inclusion. Finally, the normal extension of $\pi$ carries <a href="#eq:transport-supports" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-supports">[eq:transport-supports]</a> to the same identities with represented supports. Conjugating back by the represented $w^*$ recovers $\pi^{(n)}(k)$. The two contractive conjugations give both inequalities in <a href="#eq:transport-represented-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-represented-norm">[eq:transport-represented-norm]</a>. ◻

</details>

If $d_0\in M_\ell(C)_+$ and $d=d_0^{\oplus r}\in
M_{r\ell}(C)_+$, split $w\in M_{m,r\ell}(C^{**})$ into block columns $w_i\in M_{m,\ell}(C^{**})$.
