Then $$w_i^*w_j=\delta_{ij}e_{(d_0-\varepsilon)_+}.$$ The $r$ copies have orthogonal ranges and the same initial support. The single rectangular map also transports every entry of a matrix over the common hereditary algebra. Returning to the notation of Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a>, for $j\ge1$ the same support identities apply to $1_j\otimes w\in M_{jm,jn}(C^{**})$. Thus, for every $K\in M_j(\operatorname{Her}_{M_n(C)}(h))$, entrywise conjugation gives an algebra matrix and preserves its norm in every representation, with the matching $jm$ and $jn$ amplifications. This is the joint norm assertion needed when a finite tuple is transported.

<a id="eq:orthogonal-copy-cutoffs"></a>
For orthogonal $c_1,\ldots,c_r\in C_+$, the row $R=[c_1^{1/2},\ldots,c_r^{1/2}]\in M_{1,r}(C)$ satisfies $$R^*R=\operatorname{diag}(c_1,\ldots,c_r),\qquad
 RR^*=\sum_i c_i.$$ Thus their block sum and their sum in $C$ are Cuntz equivalent. Moreover, $f(\sum_i c_i)=\sum_i f(c_i)$ for every continuous $f$ with $f(0)=0$, first for polynomials with zero constant term and then by continuous functional calculus. Apply this calculation in $M_m(C)$ to the orthogonal transported copies $\theta_i(y)=w_i y w_i^*$ of $y\in\operatorname{Her}_{M_\ell(C)}((d_0-\varepsilon)_+)_+$. The cutoff and comparison assertions in Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a> give $$\label{eq:orthogonal-copy-cutoffs}
 \left(\sum_{i=1}^r\theta_i(y)-\tau\right)_+
 \sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}((y-\tau)_+)^{\oplus r}\qquad(\tau\ge0).$$ In particular this includes the zero cutoff.

<a id="stmt-lem-ideals-cutoffs"></a>
<div id="lem:ideals-cutoffs" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Ideals and spectral cutoffs). *Let $D$ be a $C^*$-algebra.*

1.  *If $J=\overline{\bigcup_\lambda J_\lambda}$ for an increasing directed family of closed ideals and $z\in J_+$, then for every $\varepsilon>0$, $(z-\varepsilon)_+$ lies in some $J_\lambda$. In particular, the ideals $\operatorname{Id}((z-\delta)_+)$, directed as $\delta\downarrow0$, densely exhaust $\operatorname{Id}(z)$.*

2.  *For $u,v\in D_+$, one has $u\in\operatorname{Id}(v)$ if and only if every irreducible representation $\pi$ with $\pi(v)=0$ also satisfies $\pi(u)=0$.*

3.  *If $f,g$ are continuous nonnegative functions of a positive element $a$, vanish at zero, and have the same open positivity set in $\operatorname{sp}(a)$, then $\operatorname{Her}(f(a))=\operatorname{Her}(g(a))$ and $f(a)\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}g(a)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-ideals-cutoffs"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-ideals-cutoffs</summary>

*Proof.* For (i), choose $b\in J_\lambda$ with $\|z-b\|<\varepsilon$. Then $\|z+J_\lambda\|<\varepsilon$, and functional calculus in the quotient gives $(z-\varepsilon)_+\in J_\lambda$. Since $(z-\delta)_+\to z$, the particular exhaustion follows. For (ii), apply separation of points by irreducible representations to the quotient $D/\operatorname{Id}(v)$. For (iii), the two support projections coincide. Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-rules" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-rules">4</a>(i) gives equality of the hereditary algebras and mutual comparison. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-def-rich"></a>
<div id="def:rich" class="definition">

**Definition 7**. A positive contraction $a$ is *rich* if $(a-\varepsilon)_+$ is properly infinite for every $\varepsilon\ge0$.

</div>

Zero is rich, and under $\textup{(P)}$ every positive contraction is rich. A *ramp* is a continuous nondecreasing function with values in $[0,1]$, zero on a neighborhood of zero and constant at $1$ beyond a specified larger threshold. A ramp of a rich contraction is rich. Indeed, for $0\le\tau<1$, the open positivity set of $(f(a)-\tau)_+$ is either empty or the positivity set of $(a-\rho)_+$ for some $\rho\ge0$. Lemma <a href="#lem:ideals-cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideals-cutoffs">6</a>(iii) and richness apply; for $\tau\ge1$ the cutoff is zero. Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a> gives the same conclusion for transported ramps.

<a id="sec:graph"></a>
### Graph measurements and conditional shrinking

For a $C^*$-algebra $C$, write $\widetilde C$ for its minimal unitization when $C$ is nonunital, and set $\widetilde C=C$ when $C$ is unital. We use its canonical action on $C^{**}$ in products with support projections. Given positive contractions $a,y\in C$, with $a$ rich, and a contraction $c\in\widetilde C$, we seek positive contractions $p_1\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ and $q_1\in\operatorname{Her}(y)$ with two properties: each has represented norm one wherever its corresponding input has norm greater than $1/2$, and $\|e_{p_1}ce_{q_1}\|<1$. The norm signals will later give ideal comparison.

<a id="eq:graph-constants"></a>
The construction has two parts. A graph of orthogonal copies first turns possible failure into finitely many explicit equalities. Once those equalities have been excluded, ideal cutoffs convert the resulting nonvanishing into one global support estimate. We prove both parts here while leaving the finite exclusion problem to the later arguments. Throughout this section put $$s=\frac14,\qquad \alpha_0=\frac1{32}.$$ When the integer $k\ge1$ has been chosen, fix $\gamma_g,\sigma_g>0$ with $$\label{eq:graph-constants}
 \gamma_g^2+k\sigma_g^2<1.$$ For example, $\gamma_g=1/2$ and $\sigma_g=1/(2\sqrt{k+1})$ satisfy this inequality. Both the number of coordinates and these coefficients are fixed before the measurements below are computed.

<a id="stmt-prop-graph"></a>
<div id="prop:graph" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:graph-failure"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Fixed measurements of a graph). *Let $C$ be a $C^*$-algebra, let $a,y\in C_+$ be contractions with $a$ rich, and let $c\in\widetilde C$ be a contraction. For an integer $k\ge1$ and fixed positive $\gamma_g,\sigma_g$ satisfying <a href="#eq:graph-constants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:graph-constants">[eq:graph-constants]</a>, there are $$X\in\operatorname{Her}((a-1/8)_+)_+,\qquad
 B_1,\ldots,B_k\in\operatorname{Her}((a-1/8)_+)$$ with the following property. For any contractions $$S_i\in\operatorname{Her}((X-\alpha_0)_+),\qquad 1\le i\le k,$$ one can form a rich positive contraction $p\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ such that, for every representation $\pi$ of $C$, $\|\pi(a)\|>1/2$ implies $\|\pi(p)\|=1$. If a representation $\pi$ of $C$ satisfies $$\pi\bigl(y(1-c^*p^2c)y\bigr)=0,\qquad \|\pi(y)\|>s,$$ then, in that same representation, $$\label{eq:graph-failure}
 \|\pi(X)\|>s,\qquad \pi(B_i)=\pi(S_i)\quad(1\le i\le k).$$ The elements $X,B_1,\ldots,B_k$ are fixed before the $S_i$ are chosen.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-graph"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-graph</summary>

<a id="eq:graph-slots"></a>
*Proof.* Let $e=e_{(a-1/8)_+}$ and $H=\operatorname{Her}((a-1/8)_+)$. Since $a$ is properly infinite, $a^{\oplus(k+2)}\precsim a$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a> with cutoff $1/8$, and split the resulting row into slots labelled $0,1,\ldots,k,*$. They satisfy $$\label{eq:graph-slots}
 v_i^*v_j=\delta_{ij}e,\qquad v_i v_i^*\le e_a,\qquad
 v_iH\subset C.$$ Choose a ramp $t=t(a)\in H$ that is zero on $[0,1/4]$ and one on $[1/2,1]$. It is rich, and its represented norm is one whenever the represented norm of $a$ exceeds $1/2$.

We now compute the measurements, before making any choice of $S_i$. Put $F=cyc^*\in C_+$. The block calculation behind their definition is as follows. Suppose $L\in C^{**}$ satisfies $L^*L=e$ and has slot coordinates $v_0^*L=\gamma_g e$, $v_i^*L=\sigma_g S_i$, and suppose $F=LH_0L^*$ for a positive $H_0\in eC^{**}e$. Then $$v_0^*Fv_0=\gamma_g^2H_0,\qquad
 v_i^*Fv_0=\sigma_g\gamma_g S_iH_0.$$ Vanishing of the defect in a representation will force this form there, with $tH_0=H_0$ and with $t$ acting identically on the prescribed supports of $S_i$. The first measurement therefore recovers $H_0$, and an inverse on its spectral cutoff recovers each $S_i$ from the second. These are conditional identities used to design the measurements; their verification follows after the graph is built.

<a id="eq:graph-measurements"></a>
Choose a continuous nonnegative function $h_{\rm inv}$ on $[0,\infty)$, zero at zero, equal to $\lambda^{-1}$ for $\lambda\ge\alpha_0$, and bounded by $\alpha_0^{-1}$. Define $$\label{eq:graph-measurements}
 X=\gamma_g^{-2}t v_0^*Fv_0t,\qquad
 B_i=(\sigma_g\gamma_g)^{-1}
       t v_i^*Fv_0t\,h_{\rm inv}(X).$$ The sandwiches are $(v_it)^*F(v_0t)$, hence belong to $C$. They are supported on both sides by $e$, so they lie in $H$; the same is true after multiplication by $h_{\rm inv}(X)$. Thus $X\ge0$ and $X,B_i$ have the asserted algebra membership. The slots, $t,c,y$ determine them without using any $S_i$.

For the chosen $S_i$, define in the corner $eC^{**}e$ $$J=\left((1-\gamma_g^2)e
       -\sigma_g^2\sum_{i=1}^kS_i^*S_i\right)^{1/2}.$$ Then define in $C^{**}$ $$L=\gamma_g v_0+\sigma_g\sum_{i=1}^k v_iS_i+v_*J,
 \qquad p=LtL^*.$$ Here $S_i\in H$, since $X\in H$. The radicand is at least $(1-\gamma_g^2-k\sigma_g^2)e$. Its square root belongs to $H+\mathbb C e$ and multiplies $H$ into itself. Hence $Lh\in C$ for every $h\in H$, term by term in <a href="#eq:graph-slots" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:graph-slots">[eq:graph-slots]</a>. The slot identities also give $$L^*L=e,\qquad e_aL=L.$$ It follows, by the membership and support argument of Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a>, that $h\mapsto LhL^*$ is an injective $*$-homomorphism from $H$ into $\operatorname{Her}(a)$. Indeed, for $h\ge0$ its value is $(Lh^{1/2})(Lh^{1/2})^*\in C$, supported under $e_a$; and $L^*L=e$ gives multiplicativity and recovers $h$ on conjugating back. The same identities give preservation of cutoffs, their Cuntz classes, and represented norms, exactly as in that lemma. Thus $p$ is a rich positive contraction with the stated representation signal. If $e=0$, all the operators on this corner are zero and these assertions still hold.

It remains to see what vanishing of the defect forces. Work on the essential subspace of a representation in which $$d=y(1-c^*p^2c)y=0,\qquad \|y\|>s,$$ and omit the representation symbols. The normal extensions preserve the preceding support identities. For every vector $\xi$, $$\|y\xi\|=\|pc\,y\xi\|
       \le\|c\,y\xi\|\le\|y\xi\|.$$ Equality first on $\operatorname{ran}y$ and then on its closure shows that $c$ is isometric on $e_y\mathcal H$. For a positive contraction $p$, equality $\|p\zeta\|=\|\zeta\|$ implies $p\zeta=\zeta$, by applying $1-p^2$. Consequently $ce_y\mathcal H$ lies in the $p=1$ subspace, and the positive operator $F=cyc^*$ is supported on that subspace. Since $p=LtL^*$ and $L^*L=e$, the $p=1$ subspace lies in the range of $L$, and its pullback lies in the $t=1$ subspace. With $H_0=L^*FL$, we obtain $$F=LH_0L^*,\qquad tH_0=H_0=H_0t,\qquad
 \|H_0\|=\|F\|=\|y\|>s.$$ The last equality uses the isometric action of $c$ on $e_y\mathcal H$.

Now $v_0^*L=\gamma_g e$, $v_i^*L=\sigma_g S_i$, and $L^*v_0=\gamma_g e$. Substituting these identities into <a href="#eq:graph-measurements" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:graph-measurements">[eq:graph-measurements]</a> yields $$X=tH_0t=H_0,\qquad
 B_i=tS_iH_0t\,h_{\rm inv}(X).$$ Since $tX=X$, $t$ is the identity on $\overline{\operatorname{ran}X}=e_X\mathcal H$. Each represented $S_i$ has both supports under $e_{(X-\alpha_0)_+}$. Therefore $tS_i=S_i$ and $S_iXh_{\rm inv}(X)=S_i$. The last display gives $B_i=S_i$, while $\|X\|=\|H_0\|>s$. This proves <a href="#eq:graph-failure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:graph-failure">[eq:graph-failure]</a>. ◻

</details>

Thus it is enough to choose the $S_i$ in their prescribed spectral corner so that the simultaneous relations <a href="#eq:graph-failure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:graph-failure">[eq:graph-failure]</a> cannot hold. The graph then supplies the nonvanishing implication assumed in the next lemma. The lemma uses the copied element only through its richness and its representation signal, so it is independent of how the finite equalities were excluded.

<a id="stmt-lem-conditional-shrinking"></a>
<div id="lem:conditional-shrinking" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:common-nonvanishing"></a>
<a id="eq:shrinking-signals"></a>
**Lemma 9** (From nonvanishing to shrinking). *Let $C$ be a $C^*$-algebra, let $a,y\in C_+$ be contractions with $a$ rich, and let $c\in\widetilde C$ be a contraction. Suppose $p\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ is a rich positive contraction satisfying $$\|\pi(a)\|>1/2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\pi(p)\|=1
 \qquad\text{for every representation }\pi\text{ of }C.$$ Put $d=y(1-c^*p^2c)y\in C_+$, and assume $$\label{eq:common-nonvanishing}
 \pi(d)=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\pi(y)\|\le\frac14
 \qquad\text{for every representation }\pi\text{ of }C.$$ Then there are positive contractions $$p_1\in\operatorname{Her}(a),\qquad q_1\in\operatorname{Her}(y)$$ such that $p_1$ is rich, $$\label{eq:shrinking-signals}
 \begin{split}
  \|\pi(a)\|>1/2&\ \Longrightarrow\ \|\pi(p_1)\|=1,\\
  \|\pi(y)\|>1/2&\ \Longrightarrow\ \|\pi(q_1)\|=1
 \end{split}
 \qquad\text{for every representation }\pi\text{ of }C,$$ and <a id="eq:shrinking-support"></a>$$\label{eq:shrinking-support}
 \|e_{p_1}c e_{q_1}\|_{C^{**}}<1.$$ The last inequality is a single norm bound in the universal bidual.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-conditional-shrinking"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-conditional-shrinking</summary>

<a id="eq:shrinking-second-ideal"></a>
*Proof.* The contraction assumptions give $0\le d\le y^2\le1$. By <a href="#eq:common-nonvanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:common-nonvanishing">[eq:common-nonvanishing]</a>, every irreducible representation vanishing on $d$ vanishes on $(y-s)_+$. The primitive ideal test in Lemma <a href="#lem:ideals-cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideals-cutoffs">6</a>(ii) therefore gives $(y-s)_+\in\operatorname{Id}(d)$. Set $s'=3/8$. Apply cutoff exhaustion in Lemma <a href="#lem:ideals-cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideals-cutoffs">6</a>(i) to $(y-s)_+$ with cutoff $s'-s$. After decreasing the resulting parameter if necessary, there is $0<\delta<1$ with <a id="eq:shrinking-first-ideal"></a>$$\label{eq:shrinking-first-ideal}
 (y-s')_+\in\operatorname{Id}(h_d),\qquad h_d=(d-\delta)_+.$$ Define $$z=yh_d^{1/2}\in C,\qquad q=zz^*=y h_d y\in\operatorname{Her}(y).$$ The order $d\le y^2\le1$ gives <a id="eq:shrinking-ideal-order"></a>$$\label{eq:shrinking-ideal-order}
 \delta h_d
 \le h_d^{1/2}d h_d^{1/2}
 \le z^*z=h_d^{1/2}y^2h_d^{1/2}
 \le h_d.$$ Hence $z^*z$ and $h_d$ have the same hereditary algebra, and $z^*z\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}zz^*=q$. It follows that $\operatorname{Id}(q)=\operatorname{Id}(h_d)$. A second application of cutoff exhaustion, to $(y-s')_+$ with cutoff $1/2-s'$, gives $\delta'>0$ such that $$\label{eq:shrinking-second-ideal}
 (y-1/2)_+\in\operatorname{Id}((q-\delta')_+).$$ Let $q_1$ be a ramp of $q$, zero on $[0,\delta'/2]$ and one on $[\delta',\infty)$. It is a positive contraction in $\operatorname{Her}(q)\subset\operatorname{Her}(y)$. If $\|\pi(y)\|>1/2$, then $\pi((y-1/2)_+)\ne0$. The ideal inclusion <a href="#eq:shrinking-second-ideal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shrinking-second-ideal">[eq:shrinking-second-ideal]</a> forces $\pi((q-\delta')_+)\ne0$, so $\|\pi(q)\|>\delta'$ and $\|\pi(q_1)\|=1$.

To bound the universal-bidual norm, work in the universal nondegenerate representation. For $\xi\in e_{h_d}\mathcal H
=\mathbf1_{(\delta,\infty)}(d)\mathcal H$, <a id="eq:shrinking-norm-loss"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|pc\,y\xi\|^2
 &=\|y\xi\|^2-\langle d\xi,\xi\rangle\notag\\
 &\le\|y\xi\|^2-\delta\|\xi\|^2
 \le(1-\delta)\|y\xi\|^2.
 \label{eq:shrinking-norm-loss}
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality uses that $y$ is a contraction. Apply this estimate to vectors in $\operatorname{ran}(h_d^{1/2})$. The closure of $\operatorname{ran}(yh_d^{1/2})$ is $e_q\mathcal H$, so continuity gives $$\|pc e_q\|\le\sqrt{1-\delta}.$$ Choose $\sqrt{1-\delta}<\tau<1$, and take $p_1$ to be a ramp of $p$, zero on $[0,\tau]$ and one at $1$. Functional calculus gives $e_{p_1}\le\tau^{-2}p^2$, while $e_{q_1}\le e_q$. Consequently <a id="eq:shrinking-final-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:shrinking-final-bound}
 \|e_{p_1}c e_{q_1}\|
 \le\tau^{-1}\|pc e_{q_1}\|
 \le\frac{\sqrt{1-\delta}}{\tau}<1.$$ The ramp of the rich $p$ is rich and remains in $\operatorname{Her}(a)$. Spectral mapping at its plateau shows that it has norm one in every representation where $p$ has norm one. This proves the first signal in <a href="#eq:shrinking-signals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shrinking-signals">[eq:shrinking-signals]</a>; the second was proved above. The proof also covers zero inputs. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:four-variables"></a>
### Four entries under individual proper infiniteness

Assume in this section that $C$ satisfies $\textup{(P)}$. We choose four entries that rule out the fixed measurement relations in Proposition <a href="#prop:graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:graph">8</a>. This will supply the nonvanishing hypothesis of Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-shrinking">9</a> and hence a strict contraction between hereditary supports. A spectral cutoff is essential: it places a support in an actual operator range, where the following positivity argument applies.

<a id="stmt-lem-actual-range"></a>
<div id="lem:actual-range" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (A spectral cutoff in an actual range). *Let $x,Q$ be bounded positive operators on a Hilbert space $\mathcal H$, and put $U=xQ^2x$. For every $\eta>0$, <a id="eq:actual-range"></a>$$\label{eq:actual-range}
 e_{(U-\eta)_+}\mathcal H\subset\operatorname{ran}(xQ),
 \qquad
 \ker Q\cap\operatorname{ran}(xQ)=\{0\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-actual-range"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-actual-range</summary>

*Proof.* Let $f_\eta$ be the bounded Borel function equal to $\lambda^{-1}$ for $\lambda>\eta$ and zero otherwise. Its norm on the spectrum is at most $\eta^{-1}$. If $\zeta\in e_{(U-\eta)_+}\mathcal H$, then $$\zeta=Uf_\eta(U)\zeta
      =xQ\bigl(Qx f_\eta(U)\zeta\bigr),$$ which proves the actual-range inclusion. If $\zeta=xQ\xi$ and $Q\zeta=0$, then $$0=\langle QxQ\xi,\xi\rangle=\|x^{1/2}Q\xi\|^2,$$ so $\zeta=xQ\xi=0$. The assertion concerns the actual range; no closed-range conclusion is being used. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-four-avoidance"></a>
<div id="prop:four-avoidance" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:four-forbidden"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Four entries avoid the fixed measurements). *Suppose $C$ satisfies $\textup{(P)}$. Let $X\in C_+$ and $B_1,B_2,B_3,B_4\in C$. With $$\alpha_0=\frac1{32}<\alpha=\frac1{16}
 <\beta=\frac18<s=\frac14,\qquad h=\frac12,$$ there exist contractions $T_1,\ldots,T_4$ in $\operatorname{Her}((X-\alpha_0)_+)$ for which no representation $\pi$ of $C$ satisfies $$\label{eq:four-forbidden}
 \|\pi(X)\|>s,\qquad \pi(B_i)=\pi(T_i)\quad(1\le i\le4).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-four-avoidance"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-four-avoidance</summary>

*Proof.* We argue by contradiction. If every four-entry choice could be matched in a representation, the fixed elements could be passed to a norm ultraproduct. In an irreducible representation of the resulting image of $C$, the first pair would give an isometry with nonzero defect, while the last pair would rule out that defect.

All formulas described as fixed below depend only on $X,B_i$ and the displayed constants. Define <a id="eq:four-first-fixed"></a>$$\label{eq:four-first-fixed}
 x=(X-\alpha)_+,\qquad V=(X-\beta)_+,\qquad
 Q=(h1-B_1B_1^*)_+,\qquad U=xQ^2x.$$ Here $Q\in\widetilde C_+$, whereas $x,V,U\in C_+$. Let $\operatorname{Fin}(C)$ denote the set of finite subsets of $C$, and order $$\Lambda=\mathbb N_{\ge1}\times\operatorname{Fin}(C),$$ by the usual order in the first coordinate and inclusion in the second. At $\lambda=(m,E)$, put $\eta=1/m$, so $\eta\to0$ along this directed set, and every fixed $w\in C$ eventually belongs to $E$.

<a id="eq:four-D"></a>
*The first two entries.* The first pair will force $UwV=0$ for every fixed $w\in C$ in a limiting image of $C$. At the index $(m,E)$, we test only the finitely many $w\in E$. A positive sum lets one norm bound control all of these tests: set $$\label{eq:four-D}
 A_\eta=(U-\eta)_+,\qquad
 D_0=A_\eta\left(\sum_{w\in E}wVw^*\right)A_\eta,\qquad
 D=g_\eta(D_0),$$ where $g_\eta$ is a ramp that is zero on $[0,\eta]$ and one on $[2\eta,\infty)$. In fact, $$D_0=\sum_{w\in E}
 (A_\eta wV^{1/2})(A_\eta wV^{1/2})^*.$$ Thus a small norm for $D_0$ makes each $A_\eta wV^{1/2}$ small. We have $$D_0\in\operatorname{Her}(A_\eta)\cap\operatorname{Id}(V),\qquad
 e_D\le e_{(D_0-\eta)_+}\le e_{A_\eta}\le e_x.$$ Since $V$ is properly infinite, ideal comparison gives $D_0\oplus D_0\precsim V$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a> with cutoff $\eta$, split the carrier into two columns, and multiply these columns by $D^{1/2}$. Taking adjoints of the resulting two elements gives contractions $T_1,T_2\in C$ with <a id="eq:four-first-entries"></a>$$\label{eq:four-first-entries}
 T_iT_j^*=\delta_{ij}D,\qquad e_VT_i^*=T_i^*
 \quad(i,j=1,2).$$ Their left supports lie under $e_D$ and their right supports under $e_V$. Both are under $e_x\le e_{(X-\alpha_0)_+}$; they therefore belong to the required hereditary algebra.

<a id="eq:four-first-bounds"></a>
Consider a representation satisfying <a href="#eq:four-forbidden" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-forbidden">[eq:four-forbidden]</a> for the entries at this index, and omit its symbols. Then $B_1B_1^*=D$ and $Q=(h1-D)_+$. The spectral subspace of $D$ for $(h,\infty)$ is killed by $Q$ and lies in $e_D\mathcal H\subset e_{(U-\eta)_+}\mathcal H$. Lemma <a href="#lem:actual-range" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:actual-range">10</a> shows that this subspace is zero. Thus $$\label{eq:four-first-bounds}
 B_1B_1^*=D\le h1,\qquad \|D_0\|\le2\eta
 \quad\text{in this representation}.$$ For the second conclusion, if the norm of $D_0$ were at least $2\eta$, spectral mapping and the plateau of $g_\eta$ would give $\|D\|=1>h$.

<a id="eq:four-r"></a>
*A fixed prospective defect and the last two entries.* Choose ramps $$H=h_0(X),\qquad K=k_0(X)$$ so that $h_0$ vanishes on a neighborhood of $[0,\alpha]$ and equals one on $[\beta,\infty)$, and $k_0$ vanishes on a neighborhood of $[0,\alpha_0]$ and equals one on $[\alpha,\infty)$. In particular $KH=H$. Set $$\label{eq:four-r}
 r_i=h^{-1/2}B_i^*H\quad(i=1,2),\qquad
 b_0=(1-H^2)^{1/2}.$$ The reason for these definitions is the following conditional calculation. In the limiting irreducible representation below, the first pair will give $$r_i^*r_j=\delta_{ij}H^2,\qquad Hr_i=r_i\quad(i,j=1,2),
 \qquad H\ne0.$$ These relations are not being asserted in $C$ at this stage. They imply $b_0r_i=0$, and hence $$v=r_1+b_0,\qquad v^*v=H^2+b_0^2=1,\qquad v^*r_2=0.$$ Since $r_2^*r_2=H^2\ne0$, the isometry $v$ has a nonzero defect projection $1-vv^*$. This is the scaling-element construction of Blackadar–Cuntz (B. E. Blackadar and Cuntz 1982) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[BlackadarCuntz1982](#ref-BlackadarCuntz1982)], in the form recalled in (Pasnicu and Rørdam 2007, Remark 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[PasnicuRordam2007](#ref-PasnicuRordam2007)]; the second entry supplies the additional nonzero orthogonal range.

<a id="eq:four-P0"></a>
To choose the last two entries before taking any limit, we encode this prospective defect by one fixed element of $C$. Compression by $K$ puts the formula in the required hereditary algebra, and clipping makes it a positive contraction even before the preceding relations hold. Let $\chi(t)=\min\{1,\max\{0,t\}\}$ on $\mathbb R$, and define $$\label{eq:four-P0}
 P_0=\chi\!\left(
 K\bigl[1-(r_1+b_0)(r_1+b_0)^*\bigr]K
 \right)\in C.$$ The compressed selfadjoint element is in $C$ and supported under $e_K$; since $\chi(0)=0$, continuous functional calculus in $\operatorname{Her}(K)$ gives $$e_{P_0}\le e_K\le e_{(X-\alpha_0)_+}.$$ This membership and support statement uses no relation from a representation.

<a id="eq:four-last-norm"></a>
Define the following additional fixed elements: <a id="eq:four-second-fixed"></a>$$\label{eq:four-second-fixed}
 x'=(P_0-1/2)_+,\qquad
 Q'=(h1-B_3B_3^*)_+,\qquad R=x'(Q')^2x'.$$ At the same index $\eta$, property $\textup{(P)}$ gives $$((R-\eta)_+)^{\oplus2}\precsim(R-\eta)_+.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a> to this comparison with cutoff $\eta$. If $t_R$ is a ramp of $R$, zero on $[0,2\eta]$ and one on $[4\eta,\infty)$, the two carrier columns times $t_R$ give contractions $T_3,T_4\in C$ such that <a id="eq:four-last-entries"></a>$$\label{eq:four-last-entries}
 T_3^*T_4=0,\qquad T_3^*T_3=T_4^*T_4=t_R^2.$$ Their left supports lie under $e_{(R-\eta)_+}$ and their right supports under $e_{(R-2\eta)_+}$. Both are under $e_{x'}\le e_{(X-\alpha_0)_+}$. Transport preserves the represented norms, so $$\label{eq:four-last-norm}
 \|\pi(R)\|>4\eta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \|\pi(T_3)\|=1.$$

In a representation satisfying <a href="#eq:four-forbidden" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-forbidden">[eq:four-forbidden]</a>, $B_3=T_3$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:actual-range" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:actual-range">10</a> to $x',Q',R$. The spectral subspace of $B_3B_3^*$ above $h$ is killed by $Q'$ and lies under $e_{(R-\eta)_+}$, so it is zero. Equation <a href="#eq:four-last-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-last-norm">[eq:four-last-norm]</a> then gives <a id="eq:four-last-bounds"></a>$$\label{eq:four-last-bounds}
 B_3B_3^*\le h1,\qquad \|R\|\le4\eta
 \quad\text{in this representation}.$$

*Passing only fixed elements to an ultraproduct.* Suppose, for a contradiction, that every index in $\Lambda$ admits a representation satisfying <a href="#eq:four-forbidden" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-forbidden">[eq:four-forbidden]</a>. Choose one, denoted $\pi_\lambda$, on its nondegenerate essential subspace. There is no size issue in this choice: from any such representation, choose a vector detecting $\|\pi_\lambda(X)\|>s$ and restrict to its reducing cyclic subrepresentation. A set of cyclic representations suffices. Choose an ultrafilter $\omega$ containing all tails of $\Lambda$, and form the norm ultraproduct $$\mathcal B=\prod_{\omega}B(\mathcal H_\lambda)
 =\frac{\prod_\lambda B(\mathcal H_\lambda)}
 {\{(z_\lambda):\lim_{\omega}\|z_\lambda\|=0\}}.$$ The norm of a class is $\lim_{\omega}\|z_\lambda\|$. Let $\Phi:C\to\mathcal B$ be $\Phi(z)=[(\pi_\lambda(z))]$, and write $B=\Phi(C)$. The image is a closed $C^*$-subalgebra. The unital extensions carry the unitized formulas into $C^*(B,1_{\mathcal B})$. We suppress $\Phi$ on fixed elements from now on.

<a id="eq:four-UCV-estimate"></a>
<a id="eq:four-ultraproduct"></a>
<a id="eq:four-B4-support"></a>
For any fixed $w\in C$, eventually $w\in E$. Positivity of the summands in <a href="#eq:four-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-D">[eq:four-D]</a> and <a href="#eq:four-first-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-first-bounds">[eq:four-first-bounds]</a> yield $$\|\pi_\lambda((U-\eta)_+wV^{1/2})\|^2\le2\eta$$ on that tail. Since $\|U-(U-\eta)_+\|\le\eta$, $$\label{eq:four-UCV-estimate}
 \|\pi_\lambda(UwV)\|
 \le\sqrt{2\eta}\,\|V\|^{1/2}
      +\eta\|w\|\|V\|\longrightarrow0.$$ It follows that $UBV=0$ in the image algebra. The other exact relations and bounds at each index give $$\label{eq:four-ultraproduct}
 \begin{gathered}
 \|X\|\ge s,\qquad B_1B_1^*\le h1,\qquad
 B_iB_j^*=\delta_{ij}B_1B_1^*,\quad HB_i^*=B_i^*
       \quad(i,j=1,2),\\
 UBV=0,\qquad R=0,\qquad B_3B_3^*\le h1,\qquad
 B_3^*B_4=0,\quad B_3^*B_3=B_4^*B_4.
 \end{gathered}$$ For $HB_i^*=B_i^*$, use $e_VT_i^*=T_i^*$ and $H=1$ on the support of $V$. Fix also a continuous $h_P:[0,1]\to[0,1]$ with $h_P(0)=0$ and $h_P=1$ on $[1/2,1]$. The left support of $T_4$ lies under $e_{x'}$, so we obtain $$\label{eq:four-B4-support}
 h_P(P_0)B_4=B_4.$$ Every element in <a href="#eq:four-ultraproduct" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-ultraproduct">[eq:four-ultraproduct]</a> and <a href="#eq:four-B4-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-B4-support">[eq:four-B4-support]</a> is the image of a fixed algebra element or of its fixed unitized functional calculus. We have not placed the varying $T_i,D,D_0,t_R$ in $B$, and we do not require proper infiniteness of $B$ or of $\mathcal B$. The directed finite sets in <a href="#eq:four-UCV-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-UCV-estimate">[eq:four-UCV-estimate]</a> handle all $w\in C$ without a separability assumption.

*A nonzero defect gives the contradiction.* Since $\|X\|\ge s>\beta$, the image $V=(X-\beta)_+$ is nonzero. Choose an irreducible representation of $B$ in which $V\ne0$. Extend this nondegenerate representation to $C^*(B,1_{\mathcal B})=B+\mathbb C1_{\mathcal B}$ by sending $1_{\mathcal B}$ to the identity operator; this remains valid if $B$ has a unit different from $1_{\mathcal B}$. Thus the unitized expressions $Q,b_0,Q'$ and the defect below have their stated represented meanings. Again suppress the representation symbols. The closed span of $BV\mathcal H$ is a nonzero reducing subspace, and hence all of $\mathcal H$. The relation $UBV=0$ therefore gives $U=0$. Put $D_1=B_1B_1^*$. Since $D_1\le h1$, we have $Q=h1-D_1$, and $$0=U=(Qx)^*(Qx)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad D_1x=hx.$$ By closure $D_1=h1$ on $e_x\mathcal H$. The support of $H$ lies there. Equations <a href="#eq:four-r" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-r">[eq:four-r]</a> and <a href="#eq:four-ultraproduct" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-ultraproduct">[eq:four-ultraproduct]</a> give <a id="eq:four-isometry-relations"></a>$$\label{eq:four-isometry-relations}
 r_i^*r_j=\delta_{ij}H^2,\qquad Hr_i=r_i
 \quad(i,j=1,2).$$ Also $HV=V\ne0$, so $H\ne0$ and $r_2\ne0$.

The conditional calculation preceding <a href="#eq:four-P0" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-P0">[eq:four-P0]</a> now applies: $v=r_1+b_0$ is an isometry with $v^*r_2=0$. Its defect projection $$P=1-vv^*$$ is nonzero, since $Pr_2=r_2\ne0$.

<a id="eq:four-P0-is-P"></a>
We check that this projection is the represented fixed element $P_0$, a point for which both sides of the cutoff matter. The scalar ramps give $KH=H$. From $Hr_1=r_1$ follows $Kr_1=r_1$, and the definition $r_1=h^{-1/2}B_1^*H$ gives $r_1K=r_1$. Also $K$ commutes with $b_0$. Expanding the defect gives $$P=H^2-r_1r_1^*-r_1b_0-b_0r_1^*.$$ Each term is fixed by multiplication by $K$ on both sides. Therefore $KPK=P$, and $\chi(P)=P$ because $P$ is a projection. Formula <a href="#eq:four-P0" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-P0">[eq:four-P0]</a> yields $$\label{eq:four-P0-is-P}
 P_0=P.$$

<a id="eq:four-last-contradiction"></a>
Now $x'=(P_0-1/2)_+=P/2$. Put $D_3=B_3B_3^*\le h1$; then $Q'=h1-D_3$. The relation $R=0$ gives $Q'x'=0$, so $$\label{eq:four-last-contradiction}
 D_3P=hP.$$ Equations <a href="#eq:four-B4-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-B4-support">[eq:four-B4-support]</a> and <a href="#eq:four-P0-is-P" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-P0-is-P">[eq:four-P0-is-P]</a> say $B_4=PB_4$. But $B_3^*B_4=0$, so $D_3B_4=0$, whereas <a href="#eq:four-last-contradiction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-last-contradiction">[eq:four-last-contradiction]</a> gives $D_3B_4=hB_4$. Thus $B_4=0$, and $B_3^*B_3=B_4^*B_4$ gives $B_3=0$. This contradicts <a href="#eq:four-last-contradiction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-last-contradiction">[eq:four-last-contradiction]</a>, since $h>0$ and $P\ne0$.

The contradiction shows that some index has no representation satisfying <a href="#eq:four-forbidden" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:four-forbidden">[eq:four-forbidden]</a>. The four entries chosen at that index prove the proposition. ◻

</details>

We now apply the avoidance proposition to the graph measurements. This gives the form of shrinking that will be used throughout the next section.

<a id="stmt-prop-shrinking-p"></a>
<div id="prop:shrinking-P" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12** (Shrinking under individual proper infiniteness). *Suppose $C$ satisfies $\textup{(P)}$. Let $a,y\in C$ be positive contractions and let $c\in\widetilde C$ be a contraction. There are positive contractions $p_1\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ and $q_1\in\operatorname{Her}(y)$, with $p_1$ rich, such that for every representation $\pi$ of $C$, <a id="eq:shrinking-P-signals"></a>$$\label{eq:shrinking-P-signals}
 \|\pi(a)\|>1/2\ \Longrightarrow\ \|\pi(p_1)\|=1,
 \qquad
 \|\pi(y)\|>1/2\ \Longrightarrow\ \|\pi(q_1)\|=1,$$ and, in the universal bidual, <a id="eq:shrinking-P-support"></a>$$\label{eq:shrinking-P-support}
 \|e_{p_1}c e_{q_1}\|<1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-shrinking-p"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-shrinking-p</summary>

*Proof.* Every positive contraction of $C$ is rich under $\textup{(P)}$, since each of its scalar cutoffs is properly infinite. In Proposition <a href="#prop:graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:graph">8</a>, fix $k=4$ before the measurements are computed, and choose, for example, $\gamma_g=1/2$ and $\sigma_g=1/(2\sqrt5)$. Then $\gamma_g^2+4\sigma_g^2<1$. The graph proposition gives fixed $X$ and $B_1,\ldots,B_4$ for the inputs $a,y,c$. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:four-avoidance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:four-avoidance">11</a> to these elements and use its contractions $T_i$ as the graph choices $S_i$. The resulting rich positive contraction $p\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ has the required norm-one signal for $a$.

Put $d=y(1-c^*p^2c)y$. If a representation $\pi$ had $\pi(d)=0$ and $\|\pi(y)\|>1/4$, the graph implication would give $\|\pi(X)\|>1/4$ and $\pi(B_i)=\pi(T_i)$ for every $i$. These are exactly the relations excluded by Proposition <a href="#prop:four-avoidance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:four-avoidance">11</a>. Thus <a id="eq:nonvanishing-P"></a>$$\label{eq:nonvanishing-P}
 \pi\bigl(y(1-c^*p^2c)y\bigr)=0
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \|\pi(y)\|\le1/4$$ for every representation. The same $p$ is rich and carries the $a$-signal, so Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-shrinking">9</a> applies to $a,y,c,p$ and proves the stated conclusions. This argument also covers zero inputs. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:separation"></a>
### Uniform separation and general diagonalization

We now prove Theorem <a href="#thm:individual-to-strong" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:individual-to-strong">1</a>. The strict support bound in Proposition <a href="#prop:shrinking-P" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:shrinking-P">12</a> initially depends on the inputs. Products turn it into a universal constant below one; iteration then makes the support compression arbitrarily small. A final cutoff comparison gives coefficients whose norms are bounded independently of that requested smallness.

<a id="stmt-lem-product-p"></a>
<div id="lem:product-P" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:product-row"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Products preserve individual proper infiniteness). *Let $(C_i)_{i\in I}$ be any family of $C^*$-algebras satisfying $\textup{(P)}$. Their bounded product $D=\prod_{i\in I}C_i$ satisfies $\textup{(P)}$. More precisely, for a positive contraction $d=(d_i)\in D$ and $0<\delta<1/2$, there is a row $s=(s_i)\in M_{1,2}(D)$ such that $$\label{eq:product-row}
 s^*ds=(d-2\delta)_+\oplus(d-2\delta)_+,\qquad
 \|s\|\le\delta^{-1/2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-product-p"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-product-p</summary>

*Proof.* For an empty index set, $D=0$ and the zero row has the asserted properties. Otherwise, for each $i$, put $$h_i=(d_i-\delta)_+,\qquad
 k_i=(d_i-2\delta)_+\oplus(d_i-2\delta)_+.$$ The positive $h_i$ is properly infinite. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a> to $h_i\oplus h_i\precsim h_i$ with cutoff $\delta$. If $v_i$ is the resulting rectangular partial isometry, then $$r_i=v_i k_i^{1/2}\in M_{1,2}(C_i),\qquad
 r_i^*r_i=k_i,\qquad e_{h_i}r_i=r_i,\qquad \|r_i\|\le1.$$ The algebra membership follows from transport regularity; no membership of $v_i$ itself is needed. Define $$\phi_\delta(t)=
 \begin{cases}
  t/\delta^{3/2},&0\le t\le\delta,\\
  t^{-1/2},&t\ge\delta.
 \end{cases}$$ This continuous function vanishes at zero, is bounded by $\delta^{-1/2}$, and satisfies $t^{1/2}\phi_\delta(t)=1$ for $t>\delta$. Consequently $$s_i=\phi_\delta(d_i)r_i\in M_{1,2}(C_i),\qquad
 d_i^{1/2}s_i=r_i,\qquad
 \|s_i\|\le\delta^{-1/2}.$$ The uniform bound places $(s_i)$ in $M_{1,2}(D)$. Coordinate functional calculus gives <a href="#eq:product-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:product-row">[eq:product-row]</a>. Its right-hand side converges to $d\oplus d$ in norm as $\delta\downarrow0$, proving proper infiniteness of $d$. Scaling covers every positive element of $D$. ◻

</details>

The general product-comparison criterion of Antoine–Perera–Thiel (Antoine, Perera, and Thiel 2020, Lemma 5.7) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[AntoinePereraThiel2020](#ref-AntoinePereraThiel2020)] also implies this permanence result: for each cutoff $\varepsilon$, one can take a target cutoff $\delta=\varepsilon/2$ and use proper infiniteness in every coordinate. The proof above records the particular bounded rows that make this inference possible. Coordinatewise comparison without a common cutoff bound would not suffice.

<a id="stmt-lem-uniform-shrinking"></a>
<div id="lem:uniform-shrinking" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (A uniform support contraction). *There exists $\theta\in(0,1)$ such that for every $C^*$-algebra $C$ satisfying $\textup{(P)}$, every pair of positive contractions $a,y\in C$, and every contraction $c\in C$, there are positive contractions $p\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$, $q\in\operatorname{Her}(y)$ such that, for every representation $\pi$ of $C$, $$\|\pi(a)\|>1/2\ \Longrightarrow\ \|\pi(p)\|=1,
 \qquad
 \|\pi(y)\|>1/2\ \Longrightarrow\ \|\pi(q)\|=1,$$ and <a id="eq:uniform-support"></a>$$\label{eq:uniform-support}
 \|e_p c e_q\|\le\theta.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-uniform-shrinking"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-uniform-shrinking</summary>

*Proof.* If no such bound existed, for each $m\ge2$ we could choose an algebra $C_m$ satisfying $\textup{(P)}$ and contractions $a_m,y_m,c_m$, with the first two positive, such that every pair with the stated membership and simultaneous signals has support norm greater than $1-1/m$. Form their bounded product $D$ and the three elements $a=(a_m)$, $y=(y_m)$, $c=(c_m)\in D$. Lemma <a href="#lem:product-P" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:product-P">13</a> gives $\textup{(P)}$ for $D$, so Proposition <a href="#prop:shrinking-P" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:shrinking-P">12</a> produces $p,q$ with the signals and $\kappa=\|e_pce_q\|<1$.

For each $m$, the coordinate homomorphism $D\to C_m$ preserves hereditary membership, and its normal extension sends the two support projections to the coordinate supports. Thus the coordinate support norm is at most $\kappa$. If $\pi$ is any representation of $C_m$, its composition with the coordinate homomorphism is a representation of $D$, so both signals hold for the coordinate pair as well. Choosing $m$ with $1-1/m>\kappa$ contradicts its selection. The resulting constant can be increased, if necessary, to lie in $(0,1)$. ◻

</details>

This proof uses the normal coordinate maps, not an identification of $D^{**}$ with a product of biduals. The input $c$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:uniform-shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:uniform-shrinking">14</a> belongs to $C$. This ensures that the sequence $(c_m)$ belongs to $D$; a sequence of arbitrary individual unitizations need not belong to $D$ or its unitization. We need the uniform statement only in the stated form.

<a id="stmt-prop-iterated-separation"></a>
<div id="prop:iterated-separation" class="proposition">

**Proposition 15** (Arbitrarily small support compression). *Suppose $C$ satisfies $\textup{(P)}$. For positive contractions $a_*,b_*\in C$, a contraction $c_*\in C$, and $\zeta>0$, there are positive contractions $p\in\operatorname{Her}(a_*)$, $q\in\operatorname{Her}(b_*)$ such that $$\|e_p c_*e_q\|<\zeta$$ and, for every representation $\pi$, $$\|\pi(a_*)\|>1/2\ \Longrightarrow\ \|\pi(p)\|=1,
 \qquad
 \|\pi(b_*)\|>1/2\ \Longrightarrow\ \|\pi(q)\|=1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-iterated-separation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-iterated-separation</summary>

<a id="eq:iteration-cutoffs"></a>
*Proof.* Fix $\theta$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:uniform-shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:uniform-shrinking">14</a>. On $[0,1]$ set $$f(t)=(2t-1)_+,\qquad g(t)=\min\{1,(4t-1)_+\}.$$ Both vanish near zero, $f(1)=1$, and $g=1$ on $[1/2,1]$. For a positive contraction $h$, $$\label{eq:iteration-cutoffs}
 g(h)=e_hg(h),\qquad g(h)e_{f(h)}=e_{f(h)}.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:uniform-shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:uniform-shrinking">14</a> once to $a_*,b_*,c_*$, obtaining $p_1,q_1$ with support norm at most $\theta$. Suppose inductively that $\|e_{p_k}c_*e_{q_k}\|\le\theta^k$. The next normalized input is <a id="eq:iteration-normalization"></a>$$\label{eq:iteration-normalization}
 c^{(k)}=\theta^{-k}g(p_k)c_*g(q_k)\in C,\qquad
 \|c^{(k)}\|\le1.$$ Apply the uniform lemma to $f(p_k),f(q_k),c^{(k)}$. The new supports lie under those of $f(p_k)$ and $f(q_k)$; by <a href="#eq:iteration-cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:iteration-cutoffs">[eq:iteration-cutoffs]</a>, $$e_{p_{k+1}}c_*e_{q_{k+1}}
 =\theta^k e_{p_{k+1}}c^{(k)}e_{q_{k+1}},
 \qquad
 \|e_{p_{k+1}}c_*e_{q_{k+1}}\|\le\theta^{k+1}.$$ The hereditary algebras are nested. In every representation, norm one of $p_k$ implies norm one of $f(p_k)$, by spectral mapping and $f(1)=1$, and similarly for $q_k$. Thus the two original signals persist at every step. All positive contractions needed as first inputs are rich under $\textup{(P)}$. Choose $k$ with $\theta^k<\zeta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-bounded-diagonalization"></a>
<div id="prop:bounded-diagonalization" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:bounded-diagonals"></a>
**Proposition 16** (Comparison with bounded coefficients). *Suppose $C$ satisfies $\textup{(P)}$. Let $a,b\in C_+$ be contractions and let $c\in C$. Given $$0<\delta<1/2,\qquad 0<\rho\le2\delta,\qquad \zeta>0,$$ there exist $s,t\in C$ such that <a id="eq:bounded-cross"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|s^*as-a\|,\ \|t^*bt-b\|&<2\delta+\rho,
 \label{eq:bounded-diagonals}\\
 \|s^*ct\|&<\zeta,\qquad
 \|s\|,\ \|t\|\le\delta^{-1/2}.
 \label{eq:bounded-cross}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-bounded-diagonalization"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-bounded-diagonalization</summary>

<a id="eq:bounded-chosen-separation"></a>
*Proof.* Let $a_*,b_*$ be ramps of $a,b$, zero on $[0,\delta]$ and one on $[2\delta,1]$. Put $M_c=\max\{1,\|c\|\}$. Choose a finite iteration in Proposition <a href="#prop:iterated-separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:iterated-separation">15</a> so that its outputs satisfy the signals and $$\label{eq:bounded-chosen-separation}
 \|e_p c e_q\|<\delta\zeta.$$ For example, apply the construction to $c/M_c$ and take $k$ with $M_c\theta^k<\delta\zeta$. This choice precedes the comparison coefficients below.

It remains to realize the diagonal cutoffs through $p$ and $q$ with a coefficient bound depending only on $\delta$ and $\rho$.

Put $d_a=(a-2\delta)_+$. If an irreducible representation detects $d_a$, its norm on $a$ is greater than $2\delta$. It then has norm one on $a_*$, and the signal gives norm one on $p$. Lemma <a href="#lem:ideals-cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideals-cutoffs">6</a>(ii) yields $d_a\in\operatorname{Id}(p)$. Since $$e_p\le e_{a_*}\le\mathbf1_{(\delta,\infty)}(a),
 \qquad pap\ge\delta p^2,$$ we have $\operatorname{Id}(pap)=\operatorname{Id}(p)$: one inclusion is immediate, and the displayed order gives the other. The positive $pap$ is properly infinite under $\textup{(P)}$, so Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-rules" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-rules">4</a>(iii) gives $d_a\precsim pap$.

<a id="eq:final-coefficient-bound"></a>
Compressing a stabilization comparison coefficient to the scalar source and target corners supplies $w\in C$ with $$\|w^*papw-d_a\|<\rho.$$ Set $s=pw$. Then $e_ps=s$, $s^*as=w^*papw$, and $$\label{eq:final-coefficient-bound}
 \delta s^*s\le s^*as,\qquad
 \|s\|^2
 \le\frac{\|d_a\|+\rho}{\delta}
 \le\frac{1-2\delta+\rho}{\delta}
 \le\frac1\delta.$$ In particular the more general estimate $\|pw\|^2\le(1+\rho)/\delta$ holds without the restriction $\rho\le2\delta$. The same argument for $b,q$ gives $t=qw'$, $e_qt=t$, and the identical bounds. Because $\|a-d_a\|\le2\delta$, these comparisons prove <a href="#eq:bounded-diagonals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bounded-diagonals">[eq:bounded-diagonals]</a>. Finally $$\|s^*ct\|
 \le\|s\|\|t\|\|e_p c e_q\|
 \le\delta^{-1}\|e_p c e_q\|<\zeta.$$ The bound in <a href="#eq:final-coefficient-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:final-coefficient-bound">[eq:final-coefficient-bound]</a> depends on $\delta,\rho$, not on $p,q$ or the separation accuracy. This is why the preceding choice <a href="#eq:bounded-chosen-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bounded-chosen-separation">[eq:bounded-chosen-separation]</a> remains valid after the comparison coefficients are found. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-bounded-diagonalization-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-bounded-diagonalization</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:individual-to-strong" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:individual-to-strong">1</a>.* For arbitrary $a,b\in C_+$, $c\in C$, and $\varepsilon>0$, put $M=\max\{1,\|a\|,\|b\|\}$. Choose $0<\delta<1/2$ and $0<\rho\le2\delta$ with $M(2\delta+\rho)<\varepsilon$. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:bounded-diagonalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:bounded-diagonalization">16</a> to $a/M,b/M,c$ with $\zeta=\varepsilon$. Multiplying the two diagonal inequalities by $M$ gives all three inequalities in <a href="#eq:main-diagonalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-diagonalization">[eq:main-diagonalization]</a>.

For positive $x,y\in C$, specialize these inequalities to $(a,b,c)=(x^2,y^2,xy)$. They are precisely <a href="#eq:SPI-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:SPI-definition">[eq:SPI-definition]</a>, so $C$ is strongly purely infinite. This uses the unrestricted definition directly. If one instead writes a block matrix $Z=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a&c\\c^*&b\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, the norm of $\operatorname{diag}(s,t)^*Z\operatorname{diag}(s,t)-(a\oplus b)$ is at most the maximum of the two diagonal errors plus $\|s^*ct\|$. Hence the same estimates also give diagonal approximation of every such block matrix, in particular of every positive one. The argument includes zero elements and the zero algebra. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:nuclear"></a>
### Removing amplification under nuclearity

We now remove the fixed amplification under nuclearity. This proof uses the graph construction of Section <a href="#sec:graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:graph">3</a>: a finite tuple of matrix entries rules out the graph’s failure relations, and the resulting shrinking operation allows us to join rich positive elements detected by different representations. Its second output need not be rich; the merger retains $n$ orthogonal copies so that $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$ makes their sum rich. A finite ideal argument then reaches every positive element. The proof of the following theorem is independent of the exact localization argument in Sections <a href="#sec:exactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:exactness">7</a>–<a href="#sec:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:localization">8</a>.

<a id="stmt-thm-nuclear-to-individual"></a>
<div id="thm:nuclear-to-individual" class="theorem">

**Theorem 17** (Removing amplification under nuclearity). *Let $C$ be a nuclear complex $C^*$-algebra. Suppose that one integer $n\ge1$, fixed for the whole algebra, makes $h^{\oplus n}$ properly infinite for every $h\in C_+$. Then every positive element of $C$ is properly infinite.*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:nuclear-to-individual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:nuclear-to-individual">17</a> assumes neither separability nor a unit. We keep the fixed integer $n$ throughout this section. In the tuple construction it controls the number of graph entries, while the dimensions of the finite matrix approximations may vary.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### A finite tuple that exact algebras cannot realize

The graph measurements are fixed before their entries are chosen. To choose those entries under nuclearity, we use a finite tuple whose linear matrix norms cannot all occur in an exact algebra, even though each fixed such norm is a limit of norms of finite matrix tuples. Nonexact examples originate in work of Wassermann (Wassermann 1976) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Wassermann1976](#ref-Wassermann1976)]. Pisier develops the operator-space formulation and finite compression mechanism in (Pisier 1995, Theorems 1 and 7, Lemma 2 and Equation (7)) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Pisier1995](#ref-Pisier1995)]. We use the existence of a nonexact algebra from this theory and give the tensor norm and compression arguments needed here. They retain the quantifiers across all matrix levels required for the graph entries.

Recall that a $C^*$-algebra $D$ is *exact* if tensoring every short exact sequence with $D$ in the minimal tensor norm preserves exactness. Thus exactness identifies the quotient norm on $(D\otimes_{\min}J)/(D\otimes_{\min}I)$ with the spatial norm on $D\otimes_{\min}(J/I)$, for every closed ideal $I\triangleleft J$. The next proof starts from a strict inequality between these norms.

<a id="stmt-lem-forbidden-tuple"></a>
<div id="lem:forbidden-tuple" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (A finite tuple and finite matrix approximations). *There are $\ell\ge1$, a $C^*$-algebra $W$, contractions $z_1,\ldots,z_\ell\in W$, and contractions $z_j^{(r)}\in M_{N_r}(\mathbb C)$, where $N_r\ge1$, $r\ge1$, and $1\le j\le\ell$, with the following properties.*

1.  *No tuple $b_1,\ldots,b_\ell$ in any exact $C^*$-algebra satisfies <a id="eq:forbidden-tuple"></a>$$\label{eq:forbidden-tuple}
     \left\|\sum_{j=1}^{\ell} A_j\otimes b_j\right\|
     =\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{\ell} A_j\otimes z_j\right\|
     \quad\text{for every }m\ge1\text{ and every }A_j\in M_m(\mathbb C).$$*

<a id="eq:tuple-approximations"></a>
2.  *For every fixed $m\ge1$ and $A_j\in M_m(\mathbb C)$, $$\label{eq:tuple-approximations}
     \lim_{r\to\infty}
     \left\|\sum_{j=1}^{\ell} A_j\otimes z_j^{(r)}\right\|
     =\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{\ell} A_j\otimes z_j\right\|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-forbidden-tuple"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-forbidden-tuple</summary>

<a id="eq:tuple-gap"></a>
*Proof.* Take a nonexact $C^*$-algebra $W$, whose existence is recorded in (Pisier 1995, Remark (i) following Theorem 1, pp. 163–164) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Pisier1995](#ref-Pisier1995)]. There are $I\triangleleft J$ such that the canonical surjection $$(W\otimes_{\min}J)/(W\otimes_{\min}I)
 \longrightarrow W\otimes_{\min}(J/I)$$ is not injective. The map is surjective because its algebraic image is dense and the range of a $C^*$-homomorphism is closed. Choose a representative $T_0\in W\otimes_{\min}J$ of a nonzero class in its kernel. Its norm modulo $W\otimes_{\min}I$ is positive, whereas its image in $W\otimes_{\min}(J/I)$ is zero. A sufficiently close finite algebraic approximation to $T_0$ retains a strict gap between these two norms. After normalizing its nonzero first factors and absorbing their norms into the second factors, we obtain contractions $z_j\in W$ and elements $h_j\in J$ for which $$\label{eq:tuple-gap}
 \left\|\sum_j z_j\otimes h_j+W\otimes_{\min}I\right\|
 >\left\|\sum_j z_j\otimes(h_j+I)\right\|_{\min}.$$ For a tuple $\mathbf v=(v_j)_{j=1}^{\ell}$ in a $C^*$-algebra $D$, write $$\mu_{\mathbf v,m}(A_1,\ldots,A_\ell)
 =\left\|\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}A_j\otimes v_j\right\|_{M_m(D)}.$$ We show that these functions determine both norms in <a href="#eq:tuple-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tuple-gap">[eq:tuple-gap]</a>, with the coefficient algebra and its ideal held fixed.

<a id="eq:tuple-compression-formula"></a>
For arbitrary coefficients $d_j\in J$, represent the fixed nonzero algebra $J$ faithfully on a Hilbert space $\mathcal H$. Then $$\label{eq:tuple-compression-formula}
 \left\|\sum_j v_j\otimes d_j\right\|_{\min}
 =\sup_{m,V}
 \mu_{\mathbf v,m}(V^*d_1V,\ldots,V^*d_\ell V),$$ where $V:\mathbb C^m\to\mathcal H$ ranges over finite-dimensional isometries. Indeed, also represent $D$ faithfully. The operators on the right are the two-sided compressions in the second Hilbert factor, so their norms are at most the left-hand norm. Finite-rank projections, directed by inclusion, converge strongly to the identity. Their two-sided compressions converge strongly to the tensor sum; evaluation at a fixed vector whose image nearly attains its norm gives the reverse inequality. This applies to arbitrary, including nonselfadjoint, tensor sums.

<a id="eq:tuple-quotient-formula"></a>
For the quotient norm, choose a positive contractive approximate identity $(f_\lambda)$ of the fixed ideal $I$. Put $J_D=D\otimes_{\min}I$. For $T\in D\otimes_{\min}J$, define $$R_\lambda(T)
 =(1\otimes(1-f_\lambda))T(1\otimes(1-f_\lambda)),$$ using the unitized multiplier operations. Elementary tensors and density give $$T-R_\lambda(T)\in J_D,\qquad
 \|R_\lambda(K)\|\longrightarrow0\quad\text{for each fixed }K\in J_D.$$ The cutdowns are contractions. Hence, for every fixed $K\in J_D$, $$\operatorname{dist}(T,J_D)\le\|R_\lambda(T)\|
 \le\|T-K\|+\|R_\lambda(K)\|.$$ Taking lower and upper limits and then the infimum over $K$ yields the net limit $$\label{eq:tuple-quotient-formula}
 \left\|\sum_j v_j\otimes d_j+J_D\right\|
 =\lim_\lambda
 \left\|\sum_j v_j\otimes
 (1-f_\lambda)d_j(1-f_\lambda)\right\|_{\min}.$$ The cutdown coefficients lie in $J$, and no quasicentrality is needed. This argument uses convergence on each fixed element of $J_D$. A related fixed-element cutdown mechanism appears in (Pisier 1995, proof of Lemma 3, p. 165) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Pisier1995](#ref-Pisier1995)]; the calculation above establishes the stated formula for general $I\triangleleft J$ and its approximate-identity net. Equations <a href="#eq:tuple-compression-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tuple-compression-formula">[eq:tuple-compression-formula]</a> and <a href="#eq:tuple-quotient-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tuple-quotient-formula">[eq:tuple-quotient-formula]</a> show that the displayed quotient norm depends only on the functions $\mu_{\mathbf v,m}$. Applying the same compression argument in $J/I$ shows the same for $\|\sum_j v_j\otimes(d_j+I)\|_{\min}$.

If a tuple in an exact algebra had the same functions $\mu_{\mathbf z,m}$ at every matrix level, the two formulas above would transfer this strict gap to that algebra. Exactness makes its canonical quotient map isometric, a contradiction. This proves (i). The conclusion excludes simultaneous equality for all finite matrix tests; it does not assert that a fixed finite list of tests suffices.

For (ii), set $W_0=C^*(z_1,\ldots,z_\ell)$. This algebra is separable, and its inclusion in $W$ preserves matrix norms. It has a faithful representation on a separable Hilbert space. Indeed, choose a dense sequence in the positive unit sphere and, for each member, a state attaining its norm. Their GNS representations have faithful direct sum: for every nonzero positive element, approximation by that sequence shows that some chosen state is positive on it. Choose increasing nonzero finite-rank projections $P_r\to1$ strongly in that representation, and set $$N_r=\dim P_r\mathcal H,\qquad
 z_j^{(r)}=P_rz_jP_r\big|_{P_r\mathcal H}.$$ These are contractions. For each fixed $m$ and $(A_j)$, the corresponding two-sided compressions of $\sum_j A_j\otimes z_j$ converge strongly to that operator and have norms at most its norm. Evaluation at a fixed near-norming vector gives the matching lower limit, proving <a href="#eq:tuple-approximations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tuple-approximations">[eq:tuple-approximations]</a>. The same sequence works for every fixed test. The assertion is pointwise in the test, and uses no multiplicativity of the compressions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Choosing graph entries under nuclearity

We use the finite tuples from Lemma <a href="#lem:forbidden-tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forbidden-tuple">18</a> to choose entries in a spectral hereditary subalgebra. The central point is that the transport lemma provides one rectangular map for the whole tuple. Its conjugation preserves the norm of every joint linear matrix expression, not merely the norms of the individual entries.

<a id="stmt-prop-nuclear-avoidance"></a>
<div id="prop:nuclear-avoidance" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:nuclear-forbidden"></a>
**Proposition 19** (Nuclear avoidance of the fixed measurements). *Let $C$ be nuclear and satisfy $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$ for a fixed $n\ge1$, and let $\ell$ be as in Lemma <a href="#lem:forbidden-tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forbidden-tuple">18</a>. For $X\in C_+$ and fixed elements $B_{j,pq}\in C$, where $1\le j\le\ell$ and $1\le p,q\le n$, there are contractions $$S_{j,pq}\in\operatorname{Her}((X-\alpha_0)_+)$$ such that no representation $\pi$ satisfies simultaneously $$\label{eq:nuclear-forbidden}
 \|\pi(X)\|>s,\qquad
 \pi(B_{j,pq})=\pi(S_{j,pq})
 \quad(1\le j\le\ell,\ 1\le p,q\le n).$$ Here $\alpha_0=1/32$ and $s=1/4$, as in Proposition <a href="#prop:graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:graph">8</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-nuclear-avoidance"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-nuclear-avoidance</summary>

<a id="eq:nuclear-tuple-signal"></a>
<a id="eq:nuclear-tuple-choices"></a>
*Proof.* Put $g=(X-\alpha_0)_+$, choose $0<\nu<s-\alpha_0$, and put $h_g=(g-\nu)_+$. Choose a ramp $t'=t'(X)$ that is zero on $[0,\alpha_0+\nu]$ and one on $[s,\infty)$. Then $t'\in\operatorname{Her}(h_g)$, and $$\label{eq:nuclear-tuple-signal}
 \|\pi(X)\|>s\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\pi(t')\|=1.$$ The element $v=g^{\oplus n}\in M_n(C)$ is properly infinite by $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$, applied to the scalar positive $g$. For the dimensions $N_r$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:forbidden-tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forbidden-tuple">18</a>, $$d_r=g^{\oplus N_r}\precsim v^{\oplus N_r}\precsim v.$$ The first comparison uses $g$ as a summand of $v$; the second uses proper infiniteness of $v$. Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a>, with cutoff $\nu$, gives one rectangular carrier $$w_r\in M_{n,N_r}(C^{**}),\qquad
 w_r^*w_r=1_{N_r}\otimes e_{h_g},\qquad
 w_rw_r^*\le1_n\otimes e_g.$$ Its conjugation sends $M_{N_r}(\operatorname{Her}(h_g))$ into $M_n(\operatorname{Her}(g))=\operatorname{Her}_{M_n(C)}(v)$. Use this same carrier for every $j$, defining $$\label{eq:nuclear-tuple-choices}
 S_j^{(r)}=w_r(z_j^{(r)}\otimes t')w_r^*
 \in M_n(\operatorname{Her}(g)),\qquad
 S_{j,pq}^{(r)}=(S_j^{(r)})_{pq}.$$ Each matrix has norm at most one, so its entries are contractions in the required hereditary subalgebra.

<a id="eq:nuclear-exact-matrix-norms"></a>
Suppose that every $r$ admits a representation $\pi_r$ satisfying <a href="#eq:nuclear-forbidden" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nuclear-forbidden">[eq:nuclear-forbidden]</a> for these entries. Restricting to a reducing cyclic subrepresentation that still detects $\|\pi_r(X)\|>s$, we may make these choices from a set of representations. Write $B_j=[B_{j,pq}]\in M_n(C)$; these elements are fixed independently of $r$. At every matrix level $m$, the normal amplification of the single $w_r$ is isometric on the supported source algebra, by Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a>. The represented entry equalities and <a href="#eq:nuclear-tuple-signal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nuclear-tuple-signal">[eq:nuclear-tuple-signal]</a> therefore give, for every $A_j\in M_m(\mathbb C)$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \left\|\sum_j A_j\otimes\pi_r^{(n)}(B_j)\right\|
 &=\left\|\left(\sum_j A_j\otimes z_j^{(r)}\right)
       \otimes\pi_r(t')\right\|\notag\\
 &=\left\|\sum_j A_j\otimes z_j^{(r)}\right\|.
 \label{eq:nuclear-exact-matrix-norms}
\end{aligned}$$ This equality is exact at each $r$. Using a different carrier for each $j$ would not give the joint equality.

Define the $*$-homomorphism $$\Psi:M_n(C)\longrightarrow
 \mathcal Q=
 \frac{\prod_r B(\mathcal H_r^{\oplus n})}
      {\bigoplus_r B(\mathcal H_r^{\oplus n})},
 \qquad
 \Psi(b)=[(\pi_r^{(n)}(b))_r],$$ where the numerator is the algebra of bounded sequences and the denominator is its ideal of norm-null sequences. Contractivity of the representations makes each displayed sequence bounded. The image $E=\Psi(M_n(C))$ is closed and isomorphic to $M_n(C)/\ker\Psi$. Since $M_n(C)\cong M_n\otimes C$, nuclearity of matrix algebras and permanence under tensor products make $M_n(C)$ nuclear (B. Blackadar 2006, 122:II.9.4.2 and IV.3.1.1) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[Blackadar](#ref-Blackadar)]. Its quotient $E$ is nuclear by (Choi and Effros 1977, Corollary 4) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[ChoiEffros1977](#ref-ChoiEffros1977)], and hence exact by (B. Blackadar 2006, 122:II.9.6.6) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Blackadar](#ref-Blackadar)]. These permanence facts require neither units nor separability. It is this quotient of a nuclear algebra that must be exact; the argument requires no exactness of the ambient $\mathcal Q$.

The norm of a class modulo norm-null sequences is the $\limsup$ of its coordinate norms. Subtracting a norm-null sequence gives the lower bound, and subtracting finitely many initial entries gives the upper bound. The same formula holds at each fixed matrix level under the canonical identifications of finite matrices over a product and its norm-null ideal. Since $E\subset\mathcal Q$ is completely isometric, <a href="#eq:nuclear-exact-matrix-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nuclear-exact-matrix-norms">[eq:nuclear-exact-matrix-norms]</a> and <a href="#eq:tuple-approximations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tuple-approximations">[eq:tuple-approximations]</a> give $$\left\|\sum_j A_j\otimes\Psi(B_j)\right\|_{M_m(E)}
 =\limsup_r\left\|\sum_j A_j\otimes\pi_r^{(n)}(B_j)\right\|
 =\left\|\sum_j A_j\otimes z_j\right\|$$ for every $m$ and every $(A_j)$. Only the fixed elements $B_j$ have been sent into $E$; the varying choices in <a href="#eq:nuclear-tuple-choices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nuclear-tuple-choices">[eq:nuclear-tuple-choices]</a> were used in the coordinatewise equalities. The displayed identity contradicts Lemma <a href="#lem:forbidden-tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forbidden-tuple">18</a>(i). Thus some $r$ admits no such representation, and its entries in <a href="#eq:nuclear-tuple-choices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nuclear-tuple-choices">[eq:nuclear-tuple-choices]</a> prove the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-shrinking-nuclear"></a>
<div id="prop:shrinking-nuclear" class="proposition">

**Proposition 20** (Shrinking two corners under nuclearity). *Let $C$ be nuclear and satisfy $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$ for one fixed $n\ge1$. Let $a,y\in C_+$ be contractions, with $a$ rich, and let $c\in\widetilde C$ be a contraction. There are positive contractions $p_1\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ and $q_1\in\operatorname{Her}(y)$ such that $p_1$ is rich, <a id="eq:nuclear-shrinking-signals"></a>$$\label{eq:nuclear-shrinking-signals}
 \begin{split}
 \|\pi(a)\|>1/2&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\pi(p_1)\|=1,\\
 \|\pi(y)\|>1/2&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\pi(q_1)\|=1
 \end{split}
 \qquad\text{for every representation }\pi,$$ and $\|e_{p_1}ce_{q_1}\|_{C^{**}}<1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-shrinking-nuclear"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-shrinking-nuclear</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $\ell$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:forbidden-tuple" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:forbidden-tuple">18</a>, set $k=\ell n^2$, and choose graph coefficients $\gamma_g,\sigma_g>0$ with $\gamma_g^2+k\sigma_g^2<1$. With these choices fixed, apply Proposition <a href="#prop:graph" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:graph">8</a> to obtain its measurements $X,B_i$. Group the $B_i$ into $\ell$ matrices $B_j=[B_{j,pq}]\in M_n(C)$, and choose the corresponding $S_{j,pq}$ by Proposition <a href="#prop:nuclear-avoidance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:nuclear-avoidance">19</a>. The graph gives a rich positive contraction $p\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ with $\|\pi(p)\|=1$ whenever $\|\pi(a)\|>1/2$. If $d=y(1-c^*p^2c)y$ vanishes in a representation where $\|\pi(y)\|>s$, the graph would give all of <a href="#eq:nuclear-forbidden" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nuclear-forbidden">[eq:nuclear-forbidden]</a>, contrary to the choice of the entries. Thus <a id="eq:nonvanishing-N"></a>$$\label{eq:nonvanishing-N}
 \pi\bigl(y(1-c^*p^2c)y\bigr)=0
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\pi(y)\|\le s=1/4$$ for every representation. Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-shrinking" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-shrinking">9</a> applies to this $p$, its norm signal, and the displayed implication, and gives the asserted $p_1,q_1$. The one fixed $n$ controlled both the number of entries and the algebra $M_n(C)$ in the quotient argument; only the finite dimensions $N_r$ varied. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Local rich elements and their merger

The shrinking proposition will let us join rich elements once they are available. For $u\in C_+$, we first produce such an element inside $\operatorname{Her}(u)$ at each irreducible representation that detects $u$. This local construction needs only the fixed-amplification hypothesis $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$; nuclearity will enter when the elements are joined.

<a id="stmt-lem-local-rich"></a>
<div id="lem:local-rich" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (A rich element detected by one representation). *Let $C$ be a $C^*$-algebra, and suppose that one integer $n\ge1$ makes $h^{\oplus n}$ properly infinite for every $h\in C_+$. If $u\in C_+$ and an irreducible representation $\pi:C\to B(\mathcal H)$ satisfies $\pi(u)\ne0$, then there is a rich positive contraction $p\in\operatorname{Her}(u)$ with $\|\pi(p)\|=1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-rich"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-rich</summary>

*Proof.* We will find $n$ orthogonal targets in $\operatorname{Her}(u)$, each detected by $\pi$, and one positive element comparing into every target. Transporting $n$ copies of its cutoff will allow $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$ to produce richness inside $\operatorname{Her}(u)$.

Put $\mathcal H_u=\overline{\pi(u)\mathcal H}$. This space is infinite dimensional. Otherwise $\pi(u)$ would have finite rank $r>0$. Applying the stabilized representation to proper infiniteness of $u^{\oplus n}$ would approximate $\pi(u)^{\oplus2n}$, of rank $2nr$, by positive operators of rank at most $nr$. On the $2nr$-dimensional range of the target, each such approximant has a unit vector in its kernel. Its distance from the target is therefore at least the least positive eigenvalue of $\pi(u)$, contradicting norm approximation.

The action of $D=\operatorname{Her}(u)$ on $\mathcal H_u$ is irreducible. Indeed, $\pi(u)$ has no kernel on its support, so $\pi(u)\xi\ne0$ for $0\ne\xi\in\mathcal H_u$. Irreducibility of $\pi$ gives $\overline{\pi(C)\pi(u)\xi}=\mathcal H$. Applying $\pi(u)$ and taking closure yields $$\overline{\pi(uCu)\xi}=\mathcal H_u.$$ Thus every nonzero vector is cyclic for the restricted hereditary algebra, which proves irreducibility.

Apply the selfadjoint form of Kadison transitivity (Kadison 1957, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Kadison1957](#ref-Kadison1957)] to the irreducible unitization of this action. Choose $n+1$ orthonormal vectors and prescribe $n+1$ distinct real eigenvalues. A selfadjoint interpolant has the form $h_0+\lambda1$, with $h_0\in D_{\rm sa}$. The corresponding eigenvalues of $\pi(h_0)$ remain distinct, so at most one is zero. Choose $n$ nonzero ones. Nonnegative continuous functions supported in pairwise disjoint neighborhoods of these values, avoiding zero and positive at the chosen values, give orthogonal elements $$b_1,\ldots,b_n\in D_+,\qquad \pi(b_i)\ne0.$$ The functions vanish at zero, so these elements belong to $D$ also when $D$ is nonunital.

<a id="eq:local-rich-comparison"></a>
There is an element $b'\in D_+$, still detected by $\pi$, with $b'\precsim b_i$ for every $i$. Start with $e'=b_1$. Given the current $e'$ and the next $b_i$, choose a vector on which $\pi(e')$ is nonzero. Cyclicity for $D$ and nonzero $\pi(b_i)$ give $h\in D$ such that $\pi(z)\ne0$, where $z=b_ihe'$. Then $$z^*z\in\operatorname{Her}(e'),\qquad zz^*\in\operatorname{Her}(b_i),\qquad
 z^*z\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}zz^*.$$ Replacing $e'$ by $z^*z$ preserves nonzero image and every previous comparison, by transitivity. At the end we obtain $b'$ and $$\label{eq:local-rich-comparison}
 (b')^{\oplus n}\precsim b_1\oplus\cdots\oplus b_n
 \sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_i\precsim u.$$ The middle equivalence uses orthogonality, and the last comparison uses $\sum_i b_i\in\operatorname{Her}(u)$.

Choose $\tau>0$ with $2\tau<\|\pi(b')\|$. Transport the $n$ source copies in <a href="#eq:local-rich-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-rich-comparison">[eq:local-rich-comparison]</a>, after cutoff $\tau$, into $\operatorname{Her}(u)$. Let $t$ be a ramp of $b'$, zero on $[0,\tau]$ and one on $[2\tau,\infty)$. It belongs to the common cutoff hereditary algebra and has represented norm one. The sum $p$ of its $n$ orthogonal transported copies is a positive contraction in $\operatorname{Her}(u)$ with $\|\pi(p)\|=1$. By <a href="#eq:orthogonal-copy-cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orthogonal-copy-cutoffs">[eq:orthogonal-copy-cutoffs]</a>, for every $\varepsilon\ge0$, $$(p-\varepsilon)_+\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}((t-\varepsilon)_+)^{\oplus n}.$$ The right side is properly infinite by the hypothesis applied to the scalar positive $(t-\varepsilon)_+$. Thus $p$ is rich. ◻

</details>

We now join two rich elements while retaining the union of their norm signals. At each step Proposition <a href="#prop:shrinking-nuclear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:shrinking-nuclear">20</a> is used with $c=1$ in the unitization. Its support bound is one global norm below one for that step; the numerical bound may change at the next step.

<a id="stmt-lem-merger"></a>
<div id="lem:merger" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:merger-signal"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Merging two rich elements). *Let $C$ be nuclear and satisfy $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$ for a fixed $n\ge1$. For $u\in C_+$ and rich positive contractions $a,b\in\operatorname{Her}(u)$, there is a rich positive contraction $c_0\in\operatorname{Her}(u)$ such that every representation $\pi$ satisfies $$\label{eq:merger-signal}
 \bigl(\|\pi(a)\|>1/2\ \text{or}\ \|\pi(b)\|>1/2\bigr)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\pi(c_0)\|=1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-merger"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-merger</summary>

<a id="eq:merger-induction"></a>
*Proof.* All comparisons remain in $C\otimes\mathcal K$, and all uses of shrinking are in $C$. Since $b$ is properly infinite, $b^{\oplus n}\precsim b$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a> with cutoff $1/8$ to obtain orthogonal copy maps $$\theta_i(h)=w_i h w_i^*:
 \operatorname{Her}((b-1/8)_+)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Her}(b)\subset\operatorname{Her}(u)
 \quad(1\le i\le n).$$ Let $y_0$ be a ramp of $b$, zero on $[0,1/4]$ and one on $[1/2,1]$, and set $a_0=a$. For $1\le j\le n$ we construct positive contractions $a_j,y_j$ such that $a_j$ is rich and belongs to $\operatorname{Her}(u)$, $y_j$ belongs to the common domain of the copy maps, and the following invariants hold: $$\label{eq:merger-induction}
 \begin{gathered}
 a_j\theta_i(y_j)=0\qquad(1\le i\le j),\\
 \|\pi(a)\|>1/2\ \Longrightarrow\ \|\pi(a_j)\|=1,\\
 \|\pi(b)\|>1/2\ \Longrightarrow\ \|\pi(y_j)\|=1
 \quad\text{for every representation }\pi.
 \end{gathered}$$

Once the induction reaches $j=n$, we will set $c_0=a_n+\sum_{i=1}^n\theta_i(y_n)$. The induction will make the summands orthogonal, and the final use of $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$ will prove the sum of the $n$ copies rich without requiring $y_n$ itself to be rich.

<a id="eq:merger-pullback"></a>
Suppose $a_{j-1},y_{j-1}$ have been constructed. At $j=1$, use the initial pair $a_0,y_0$; at later stages use the preceding invariants. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:shrinking-nuclear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:shrinking-nuclear">20</a> to $a_{j-1},\theta_j(y_{j-1})$, and $1\in\widetilde C$, and call its outputs $p_j,q_j$. Thus $p_j$ is rich and belongs to $\operatorname{Her}(a_{j-1})$, while $$\kappa_j=\|e_{p_j}e_{q_j}\|<1.$$ The hereditary-image equality <a href="#eq:transport-hereditary-image" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-hereditary-image">[eq:transport-hereditary-image]</a> supplies a positive contraction $z_j\in\operatorname{Her}(y_{j-1})$ such that $$\label{eq:merger-pullback}
 q_j=\theta_j(z_j).$$ This pullback is an algebra element. Its represented norms equal those of $q_j$ by <a href="#eq:transport-represented-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transport-represented-norm">[eq:transport-represented-norm]</a>.

For $0\le t\le1$, put $f(t)=(2t-1)_+$ and $g(t)=\min\{1,2t\}$. We next move $p_j$ away from a cutoff of $q_j$. The support bound ensures that multiplication by $1-g(q_j)$ is bounded below on the support of $p_j$. The polar part of $(1-g(q_j))p_j$ will therefore transport $p_j$, preserving its cutoff Cuntz classes and represented norms. Define $$y_j=f(z_j),\qquad H_j=1-g(q_j),\qquad T_j=H_jp_j.$$ Here $T_j=p_j-g(q_j)p_j\in\operatorname{Her}(u)$. In the universal representation, $g(q_j)$ is supported under $e_{q_j}$. For $\xi\in e_{p_j}\mathcal H$, this gives $$\|H_j\xi\|^2
 \ge\|(1-e_{q_j})H_j\xi\|^2
 =\|(1-e_{q_j})\xi\|^2
 \ge(1-\kappa_j^2)\|\xi\|^2.$$ Since $H_j$ is a contraction, we obtain the order bounds <a id="eq:merger-polar-order"></a>$$\label{eq:merger-polar-order}
 (1-\kappa_j^2)p_j^2\le T_j^*T_j\le p_j^2.$$ Let $T_j=l_j|T_j|$ be its polar decomposition in the bidual. The order bounds give $\operatorname{Her}(|T_j|)=\operatorname{Her}(p_j)$ and $l_j^*l_j=e_{p_j}$. The polar membership argument in the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a>, applied now to $T_j$, gives $$a_j=l_jp_jl_j^*\in\operatorname{Her}(T_jT_j^*)\subset\operatorname{Her}(u).$$ It is a positive contraction. The same argument makes each transported cutoff Cuntz equivalent to the corresponding cutoff of $p_j$, so $a_j$ is rich. The normal extensions of the representations preserve $l_j^*l_j=e_{p_j}$; conjugating back by $l_j^*$ shows that $a_j$ and $p_j$ have equal norm in every representation.

We check that this change preserves every earlier orthogonality relation. Since $gf=f$, functional calculus and <a href="#eq:merger-pullback" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:merger-pullback">[eq:merger-pullback]</a> give $$\theta_j(y_j)H_j=f(q_j)(1-g(q_j))=0,$$ and hence $\theta_j(y_j)T_j=0$. For $i<j$, the previous orthogonality and $p_j\in\operatorname{Her}(a_{j-1})$ give $\theta_i(y_{j-1})p_j=0$, while orthogonality of the copy ranges gives $\theta_i(y_{j-1})g(q_j)=0$. Thus $\theta_i(y_{j-1})T_j=0$. Each of these positive elements annihilates $\overline{\operatorname{ran}(T_j)}$, whose projection is $l_jl_j^*$, and so annihilates $a_j$. Because $y_j\in\operatorname{Her}(y_{j-1})$, the same is true of $\theta_i(y_j)$ for $i<j$. This proves the orthogonality in <a href="#eq:merger-induction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:merger-induction">[eq:merger-induction]</a>.

The norm signals also persist simultaneously. Wherever $\|\pi(a)\|>1/2$, the first shrinking step gives $\|\pi(p_1)\|=1$, hence $\|\pi(a_1)\|=1$; that signal is above $1/2$ and survives every subsequent step. Wherever $\|\pi(b)\|>1/2$, the initial ramp has $\|\pi(y_0)\|=1$. If $\|\pi(y_{j-1})\|=1$, its copy under $\theta_j$ has norm one, shrinking gives $\|\pi(q_j)\|=1$, and transport gives $\|\pi(z_j)\|=1$. Since $f(1)=1$, spectral mapping gives $\|\pi(y_j)\|=1$.

Finally set $$c_0=a_n+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_i(y_n).$$ All summands are orthogonal positive contractions in $\operatorname{Her}(u)$. Their sum is a contraction, and in each representation its norm is the maximum of the summand norms. The preceding signals prove <a href="#eq:merger-signal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:merger-signal">[eq:merger-signal]</a>. For every $\tau\ge0$, orthogonal functional calculus and <a href="#eq:orthogonal-copy-cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orthogonal-copy-cutoffs">[eq:orthogonal-copy-cutoffs]</a> give $$(c_0-\tau)_+
 \sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}(a_n-\tau)_+\oplus((y_n-\tau)_+)^{\oplus n}.$$ The first summand is properly infinite because $a_n$ is rich. The second is properly infinite by $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$, applied to the scalar positive $(y_n-\tau)_+$. Their block sum is properly infinite, so $c_0$ is rich. The construction includes zero inputs through zero copy maps and zero cutoffs. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Finite ideal assembly and absorption

Lemma <a href="#lem:local-rich" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-rich">21</a> supplies a rich element wherever $u$ is detected, and Lemma <a href="#lem:merger" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:merger">22</a> joins any finite family while retaining all of its norm signals. These two facts put $(u-\varepsilon)_+$, for every $\varepsilon>0$, in the ideal of a single rich element. Ideal comparison then supplies the doubled cutoff comparison needed for Theorem <a href="#thm:nuclear-to-individual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:nuclear-to-individual">17</a>.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-merger-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-merger</summary>
