*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:nuclear-to-individual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:nuclear-to-individual">17</a>.* Fix $u\in C_+$, and let $\mathcal R_u$ be the set of rich positive contractions in $\operatorname{Her}(u)$. Let $J$ be the closed ideal generated by all $(p-1/2)_+$ with $p\in\mathcal R_u$. We claim that $u\in J$. Otherwise an irreducible representation of $C/J$ would detect $u+J$. Its composition with the quotient map is an irreducible representation of $C$, so Lemma <a href="#lem:local-rich" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-rich">21</a> gives $p\in\mathcal R_u$ with represented norm one. Then $(p-1/2)_+$ has nonzero image, contrary to its membership in $J$.

The ideals generated by finite subfamilies of these cutoffs form an increasing directed family with dense union in $J$. For $\varepsilon>0$, Lemma <a href="#lem:ideals-cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideals-cutoffs">6</a>(i) therefore puts $(u-\varepsilon)_+$ in the ideal generated by $(p_i-1/2)_+$ for finitely many $p_1,\ldots,p_r\in\mathcal R_u$. Starting from the rich zero element, merge them successively by Lemma <a href="#lem:merger" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:merger">22</a>. The result is a rich $p\in\operatorname{Her}(u)$ for which every representation satisfies $$\|\pi(p_i)\|>1/2\text{ for some }i
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|\pi(p)\|=1.$$ A norm-one signal survives the next merger because it is above $1/2$. The primitive ideal test in Lemma <a href="#lem:ideals-cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideals-cutoffs">6</a>(ii) now gives $(p_i-1/2)_+\in\operatorname{Id}(p)$ for every $i$. Consequently <a id="eq:nuclear-final-ideal"></a>$$\label{eq:nuclear-final-ideal}
 (u-\varepsilon)_+\in\operatorname{Id}(p).$$ If the cutoff is zero, the zero element already suffices.

<a id="eq:nuclear-final-comparison"></a>
Since $p$ is properly infinite, ideal comparison with this target in the stabilization, followed by hereditary comparison, yields $$\label{eq:nuclear-final-comparison}
 ((u-\varepsilon)_+)^{\oplus2}\precsim p\precsim u.$$ The target $u$ is fixed as $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. Explicitly, choose $\varepsilon_j\downarrow0$ and, for each $j$, a comparison coefficient in <a href="#eq:nuclear-final-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nuclear-final-comparison">[eq:nuclear-final-comparison]</a> with error less than $\varepsilon_j$ to the doubled cutoff. That cutoff differs from $u\oplus u$ by at most $\varepsilon_j$. The resulting coefficients therefore compare $u\oplus u$ to $u$ in norm. Thus $u$ is properly infinite. The fixed $n$ was applied only to scalar positives and their scalar cutoffs throughout the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-tuple-exact"></a>
<div id="rem:tuple-exact" class="remark">

*Remark 23* (Extension of the tuple proof to exact algebras). The amplification-removal argument uses nuclearity only to obtain exactness of $E=\Psi(M_n(C))$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:nuclear-avoidance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:nuclear-avoidance">19</a>. Quotients of exact $C^*$-algebras are exact, including in the nonseparable case (B. Blackadar 2006, 122:IV.3.4.20) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[Blackadar](#ref-Blackadar)]. Finite matrix amplification also preserves exactness, since its tensor products and ideal kernels are the corresponding finite matrices over those for $C$. This deeper quotient-permanence theorem therefore makes the same avoidance, shrinking, merger, and ideal assembly valid when $C$ is merely exact. It gives another proof of the amplification-removal conclusion of Theorem <a href="#thm:exact-infiniteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:exact-infiniteness">2</a>. The perturbation and localization proof below obtains that conclusion without invoking quotient permanence of exactness.

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-rem-tuple-exact"><summary>Proof of stmt-rem-tuple-exact</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:absorption" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:absorption">3</a>.* Set $C=A$, the separable nuclear algebra in the corollary, with its stated fixed-amplification hypothesis. Theorem <a href="#thm:nuclear-to-individual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:nuclear-to-individual">17</a> gives $\textup{(P)}$, and Theorem <a href="#thm:individual-to-strong" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:individual-to-strong">1</a> gives strong pure infiniteness. The general nonunital absorption implication is recorded in the paragraph following (Kirchberg and Sierakowski 2016, Definition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[KS](#ref-KS)]. We spell out the central-sequence interface that includes the nonunital case.

Let $C_\omega$ be the norm ultrapower of $C$ along a free ultrafilter on $\mathbb N$, with $C$ embedded diagonally, and put $$F(C)=\frac{C_\omega\cap C'}{\operatorname{Ann}(C,C_\omega)},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Ann}(C,C_\omega)
 =\{x\in C_\omega:xC=Cx=0\}.$$ Suppose first that $C\ne0$. Separability makes $C$ $\sigma$-unital, so $F(C)$ is unital by (Kirchberg 2006, Corollary 1.10(1)) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[Kirchberg2006](#ref-Kirchberg2006)]. The central-sequence embedding statement immediately before (Kirchberg 2006, Proposition 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[Kirchberg2006](#ref-Kirchberg2006)] gives a unital embedding $\mathcal O_\infty\hookrightarrow F(C)$: its ambient original algebra is the strongly purely infinite $C$, and the diagonal copy of $C$ is the required separable nuclear subalgebra of $C_\omega$. The algebra $\mathcal O_\infty$ is unital and separable, and Remark 4.6 supplies self-absorption and connectedness of its unitary group. The “in particular” clause of Proposition 4.4(4) therefore gives stable $\mathcal O_\infty$-absorption. Since $U(\mathcal O_\infty)=U_0(\mathcal O_\infty)$, where $U_0$ is the identity component, the commutator subgroup of $U(\mathcal O_\infty)$ lies in $U_0(\mathcal O_\infty)$; part (5) therefore removes stabilization. Hence $C\cong C\otimes_{\min}\mathcal O_\infty$. The Proposition permits $C$ to be nonunital; the unital embedding is into the annihilator quotient $F(C)$. If $C=0$, the asserted isomorphism is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:exactness"></a>
### Uniform perturbation from exactness

We now begin the exact proof that <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span> implies <span class="upright">(P)</span>. Its first step is a perturbation estimate that applies simultaneously to every primitive quotient in which a fixed positive element has norm at least a prescribed positive threshold. The constant in that estimate comes from the following obstruction: there is one positive error threshold, independent of the unital exact algebra and the matrix size, below which a contraction cannot contain approximately reducing copies of every finite matrix contraction.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The universal contraction obstruction

Recall that a $C^*$-algebra $B$ is *exact* if, for every closed two-sided ideal $I$ of every $C^*$-algebra $C$, the canonical map $$(B\otimes_{\min}C)/(B\otimes_{\min}I)
 \longrightarrow B\otimes_{\min}(C/I)$$ is an isomorphism. Here and below, homomorphisms of $C^*$-algebras are $*$-homomorphisms. In the obstruction, the same finite-dimensional copy must approximately intertwine both a contraction and its adjoint.

<a id="stmt-prop-uniform-gap"></a>
<div id="prop:uniform-gap" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:forbidden-models"></a>
**Proposition 24** (Uniform gap). *There is a constant $\gamma>0$ such that no unital exact $C^*$-algebra $B$ contains a contraction $v$ with the following property: for every integer $d\geq1$ and every contraction $D\in M_d(\mathbb C)$, there are a unital representation $\pi:B\to B(H_\pi)$ and an isometry $J:\mathbb C^d\to H_\pi$ satisfying $$\label{eq:forbidden-models}
 \|\pi(v)J-JD\|<\gamma,
 \qquad
 \|\pi(v)^*J-JD^*\|<\gamma.$$ The representation and the isometry are allowed to depend on $d$ and $D$.*

</div>

The proof has two parts. The universal unital algebra of a contraction fails exactness, as witnessed by a tensor quotient. If exact targets admitted the proposed reducing copies with arbitrarily small common error, their evaluations would give a lower bound on every matrix norm of each fixed polynomial space. Holding that space and one evaluation fixed while an ideal approximate unit varies would force the tensor quotient map to be injective. The two relations in <a href="#eq:forbidden-models" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:forbidden-models">[eq:forbidden-models]</a> are what allow this argument to test words containing both the generator and its adjoint.

Let $\mathcal U=C^*(1,u)$ be the universal unital $C^*$-algebra generated by a contraction. Thus every contraction $T$ on a Hilbert space determines a unital homomorphism $u\mapsto T$. One construction is to complete the unital $*$-algebra of polynomials in $u,u^*$ in the supremum seminorm over all such evaluations, after dividing out its null space. The seminorm of each polynomial is finite because a word in a contraction and its adjoint has norm at most one.

<a id="eq:tensor-quotient-norm"></a>
We shall use the following elementary quotient-norm formula. If $D$ is unital and $(e_\lambda)$ is a positive contractive approximate unit of an ideal $I\subset C$, then, for $z\in D\otimes_{\min}C$, $$\label{eq:tensor-quotient-norm}
 \|z+D\otimes_{\min}I\|
 =\lim_\lambda\|z(1\otimes(1-e_\lambda))\|.$$ When $C$ is nonunital, the multiplication is performed in its unitization; its result still belongs to $D\otimes_{\min}C$. Indeed, $1\otimes e_\lambda$ is an approximate unit for $D\otimes_{\min}I$. The product on the right represents the same quotient class as $z$, so its norm bounds the left side from below. For any $y\in D\otimes_{\min}I$, contractivity gives $$\limsup_\lambda\|z(1\otimes(1-e_\lambda))\|
 \leq \|z-y\|
       +\limsup_\lambda\|y(1\otimes(1-e_\lambda))\|
 =\|z-y\|.$$ Taking the infimum over $y$ proves the formula, including existence of the limit.

The formula also gives the form of subalgebra permanence that we need: exactness passes to unital $C^*$-subalgebras; see also (B. Blackadar 2006, 122:IV.3.4.3) <a id="pm-cite-50"></a>[[Blackadar](#ref-Blackadar)]. For a unital inclusion $D\subset B$, the spatial tensor norms on $D\otimes C$ and $D\otimes(C/I)$ are the norms inherited from the corresponding tensor products with $B$. Formula <a href="#eq:tensor-quotient-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tensor-quotient-norm">[eq:tensor-quotient-norm]</a> therefore gives, for $z\in D\otimes_{\min}C$, $$\|z+D\otimes_{\min}I\|
 =\|z+B\otimes_{\min}I\|.$$ If $B$ is exact, this equals $\|(\operatorname{id}_D\otimes q)z\|$, where $q:C\to C/I$ is the quotient map. This proves exactness of $D$.

<a id="stmt-lem-nonexact-universal"></a>
<div id="lem:nonexact-universal" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25**. *The universal contraction algebra $\mathcal U$ is not exact.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-nonexact-universal"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-nonexact-universal</summary>

*Proof.* We first embed the full group algebra $Q=C^*(\mathbb F_2)$ into $\mathcal U$. The two free generators are sent to $$\exp(2\pi i\operatorname{Re}u),
 \qquad \exp(2\pi i\operatorname{Im}u).$$ To see that the resulting homomorphism is injective, take any pair of unitaries $S,T$ on a Hilbert space. Bounded Borel functional calculus gives selfadjoint contractions $H,K$ such that $S=\exp(\pi iH)$ and $T=\exp(\pi iK)$. The contraction $(H+iK)/2$ evaluates the two displayed unitaries to $S,T$. Thus every unital representation of $Q$ is a restriction of an evaluation of $\mathcal U$, and the homomorphism is isometric. The logarithms need only belong to the bounded operators on the representation space. Courtney and Sherman give a related embedding with a different normalization in (Courtney and Sherman 2020, Theorem 6.10) <a id="pm-cite-51"></a>[[CS](#ref-CS)].

<a id="eq:diagonal-state"></a>
Nonexactness of $C^*(\mathbb F_2)$ was established by Wassermann (Wassermann 1976) <a id="pm-cite-52"></a>[[Wassermann1976](#ref-Wassermann1976)]. Here is an explicit proof for $Q$. We construct a representation of $Q\otimes_{\min}Q$ that annihilates $Q\otimes_{\min}I$, where $I$ is the kernel of the regular quotient. Exactness would make it descend to $Q\otimes_{\min}(Q/I)$. A concrete tensor sum, however, has spatial norm in that algebra smaller than the norm the proposed descended representation would assign to it. This will exhibit the required failure of the tensor quotient map. Put $G=\mathbb F_2$, and write $u_s$ for its canonical unitaries in $Q$. On $\ell^2(G)$ let $$\lambda_s\delta_g=\delta_{sg},
 \qquad \rho_s\delta_g=\delta_{gs^{-1}}.$$ There is a state $\varphi$ on $Q\otimes_{\min}Q$ with $$\label{eq:diagonal-state}
 \varphi(u_s\otimes u_t)=
 \begin{cases}1,&s=t,\\0,&s\ne t.\end{cases}$$ For completeness, finite quotients supplying this state can be constructed directly. On the finite word ball of radius $m$ in $G$, partially defined left translation by each of the two generators is an injection between subsets of equal size, hence extends to a permutation of the ball. Freeness gives a homomorphism $q_m$ from $G$ to the finite permutation group generated by these extensions. The inverse of each extension agrees with inverse left translation whenever both endpoints lie in the ball. If a reduced word has length at most $m$, applying its permutations to the identity follows its successive suffixes inside the ball and ends at that word. Consequently $q_m$ distinguishes distinct elements of the word ball of radius $m$.

For the finite group $F_m=q_m(G)$, its commuting left and right regular representations give a representation of $C^*(F_m)\otimes_{\min}C^*(F_m)$: these algebras are finite direct sums of matrix algebras, for which the spatial and maximal tensor norms agree. Pulling its vector state at the identity back to $Q\otimes_{\min}Q$ gives the coefficient $\boldsymbol{1}_{q_m(s)=q_m(t)}$ on $u_s\otimes u_t$. These coefficients tend to those in <a href="#eq:diagonal-state" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:diagonal-state">[eq:diagonal-state]</a>. The group tensors span a dense $*$-subalgebra, and all the states have norm one, so their limit on that span extends to a state on the spatial completion.

<a id="eq:left-right-gns"></a>
The GNS representation of $\varphi$ is precisely $$\label{eq:left-right-gns}
 u_s\otimes u_t\longmapsto\lambda_s\rho_t
 \quad\hbox{on }\ell^2(G),$$ with cyclic vector $\delta_e$. Indeed the cyclic vector associated with $u_s\otimes u_t$ corresponds to $\delta_{st^{-1}}$; the inner products agree because $$s^{-1}a=t^{-1}b\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad st^{-1}=ab^{-1},$$ and left multiplication by $u_x\otimes u_y$ becomes $\lambda_x\rho_y$. These vectors span $\ell^2(G)$.

Let $I=\ker(Q\to C_r^*(G))$. The linear unitary $R\delta_g=\delta_{g^{-1}}$ satisfies $R\lambda_sR^*=\rho_s$. Thus the representation in <a href="#eq:left-right-gns" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:left-right-gns">[eq:left-right-gns]</a> annihilates $Q\otimes_{\min}I$. Exactness of $Q$ would make it descend to a representation of $Q\otimes_{\min}C_r^*(G)$, in which every $u_s\otimes\lambda_s$ fixes $\delta_e$.

This contradicts a spatial norm bound. In a faithful unital representation $\pi:Q\to B(H)$, the unitary $$V(\xi\otimes\delta_g)=\pi(u_g)\xi\otimes\delta_g$$ satisfies $V(1\otimes\lambda_s)V^*=\pi(u_s)\otimes\lambda_s$. If $a,b$ are the free generators, the norm of $$u_a\otimes\lambda_a+u_{a^{-1}}\otimes\lambda_{a^{-1}}
 +u_b\otimes\lambda_b+u_{b^{-1}}\otimes\lambda_{b^{-1}}$$ in this faithful spatial representation is therefore the norm of the adjacency operator on the $4$-regular tree. That norm is at most $2\sqrt3<4$. To check the bound, orient each edge away from the identity, and for a finitely supported vector $\xi$ bound the contribution of a parent–child edge by $$2|\xi(p)\xi(c)|
 \leq 3^{-1/2}|\xi(p)|^2+3^{1/2}|\xi(c)|^2.$$ Every nonroot vertex has three children and one parent, and hence total coefficient $2\sqrt3$; the root has coefficient $4/\sqrt3\leq2\sqrt3$. The quadratic-form bound extends by density and proves the asserted operator norm bound. In the purported descended representation the same sum has eigenvalue $4$ on $\delta_e$, which is impossible for a $*$-representation. Thus $Q$ is not exact. Its unital inclusion in $\mathcal U$ and the inheritance proved above show that $\mathcal U$ is not exact either. ◻

</details>

We have obtained the algebraic obstruction. To turn it into a fixed error bound, we next test polynomial norms on finite contractions and control those tests at every matrix amplification. The latter uniformity will allow one polynomial model to be held fixed while an ideal approximate unit varies.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-matrix-tests"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-matrix-tests" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:finite-matrix-tests"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Finite matrix tests). *For every integer $r\geq1$ and every $Z\in M_r(\mathcal U)$ whose entries are $*$-polynomials in $u,u^*$, $$\label{eq:finite-matrix-tests}
 \|Z\|_{M_r(\mathcal U)}
 =\sup_{d\geq1,\ D\in M_d(\mathbb C),\ \|D\|\leq1}\|Z(D,D^*)\|.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-matrix-tests"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-matrix-tests</summary>

*Proof.* Contractivity of evaluations proves one inequality. Every unital representation of $\mathcal U$ is evaluation at the image of $u$, so these evaluations also compute the matrix norm on the left. For the other inequality, evaluate at a contraction $T\in B(H)$, and take a unit vector $\xi=(\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_r)\in H^r$ arbitrarily close to attaining the norm of $Z(T,T^*)$. If the entries of $Z$ have degree at most $m$, let $$H_0=\operatorname{span}
 \{w(T,T^*)\xi_j:|w|\leq m,\ 1\leq j\leq r\},$$ where the empty word is included. This is a finite-dimensional nonzero space. Set $D=P_{H_0}T|_{H_0}$, so that $D^*=P_{H_0}T^*|_{H_0}$. Applying a word successively from the right never changes the relevant vectors under compression, since all its suffix vectors lie in $H_0$. Hence $$w(D,D^*)\xi_j=w(T,T^*)\xi_j\quad (|w|\leq m),$$ and $Z(D,D^*)\xi=Z(T,T^*)\xi$. Taking the supremum over evaluations and over these vectors proves <a href="#eq:finite-matrix-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-matrix-tests">[eq:finite-matrix-tests]</a>. The scalar norm-test formula is proved in (Courtney and Sherman 2020, Proposition 2.2) <a id="pm-cite-53"></a>[[CS](#ref-CS)]; the finite-word compression used here is the argument in the proof of (Courtney and Sherman 2020, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-54"></a>[[CS](#ref-CS)]. Including all components of the test vector gives the matrix-valued assertion needed here. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-uniform-matrix-bound"></a>
<div id="lem:uniform-matrix-bound" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:all-amplification-bound"></a>
**Lemma 27** (A bound at every amplification). *Let $E\subset\mathcal U$ be a finite-dimensional linear space consisting of $*$-polynomials in $u,u^*$. There is a finite constant $C_E\geq0$ with the following property. Suppose $v$ is a contraction in a unital $C^*$-algebra $B$, and fix one number $\theta>0$. Assume that for every integer $d\geq1$ and every contraction $D\in M_d(\mathbb C)$, there are a unital representation $\pi:B\to B(H_\pi)$ and an isometry $J:\mathbb C^d\to H_\pi$ satisfying the two inequalities in <a href="#eq:forbidden-models" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:forbidden-models">[eq:forbidden-models]</a> with $\theta$ in place of $\gamma$. For the homomorphism $\Phi:\mathcal U\to B$ with $\Phi(u)=v$, one then has $$\label{eq:all-amplification-bound}
 \|\Phi^{(r)}(Z)\|\geq(1-C_E\theta)\|Z\|
 \qquad (r\geq1,\ Z\in M_r(E)).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-uniform-matrix-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-uniform-matrix-bound</summary>

<a id="eq:explicit-CE"></a>
*Proof.* If $E=0$, take $C_E=0$. Otherwise choose a basis $P_1,\ldots,P_k$ of $E$, with coordinate functionals $f_1,\ldots,f_k$, and choose polynomial representatives $P_i=\sum_w a_{iw}w$. Define $$\label{eq:explicit-CE}
 C_E=\sum_{i=1}^k\|f_i\|\sum_w|a_{iw}|\,|w|.$$ Constants are allowed in the representatives; the empty word has length zero. For $Z=\sum_i B_i\otimes P_i\in M_r(E)$ and scalar unit vectors $\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb C^r$, $$|\alpha^*B_i\beta|
 =|f_i(\alpha^*Z\beta)|\leq\|f_i\|\|Z\|.$$ It follows that $\|B_i\|\leq\|f_i\|\|Z\|$ for every matrix size $r$.

Given a finite contraction $D$, choose $\pi,J$ as in the hypothesis. Both $\pi(v)$ and $D$ are contractions, and telescoping the two intertwining relations gives $$\|w(\pi(v),\pi(v)^*)J-Jw(D,D^*)\|\leq|w|\theta$$ for every word $w$, including words in both letters. More explicitly, the difference for a word $A_1\cdots A_m$ is the sum of $m$ terms, each consisting of contractions on either side of one difference $\pi(v)J-JD$ or $\pi(v)^*J-JD^*$. Put $J_r=1_{\mathbb C^r}\otimes J$. The coefficient bound and <a href="#eq:explicit-CE" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:explicit-CE">[eq:explicit-CE]</a> yield $$\|\pi^{(r)}(\Phi^{(r)}(Z))J_r-J_r Z(D,D^*)\|
 \leq C_E\theta\|Z\|.$$ Since $J_r$ is an isometry and $\pi^{(r)}$ is contractive, $$\|Z(D,D^*)\|\leq\|\Phi^{(r)}(Z)\|+C_E\theta\|Z\|.$$ Taking the supremum supplied by Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-matrix-tests" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-matrix-tests">26</a> proves <a href="#eq:all-amplification-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:all-amplification-bound">[eq:all-amplification-bound]</a>. In particular, $C_E$ depends on the fixed polynomial space and its chosen representatives, but on none of $r,d,D,B,v,\pi,J$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-uniform-matrix-bound-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-uniform-matrix-bound</summary>

<a id="eq:fixed-j-matrix-bound"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:uniform-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:uniform-gap">24</a>.* If the Proposition were false, for every integer $j\geq1$ there would be a unital exact algebra $B_j$ and a contraction $v_j\in B_j$ satisfying <a href="#eq:forbidden-models" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:forbidden-models">[eq:forbidden-models]</a> with $1/j$ in place of $\gamma$. Let $\Phi_j:\mathcal U\to B_j$ send $u$ to $v_j$. Fix a finite-dimensional polynomial space $E$. Before choosing any matrix amplification, tensor factor, compression, or ideal, choose one integer $j=j(E)\geq\max\{1,2C_E\}$. Lemma <a href="#lem:uniform-matrix-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:uniform-matrix-bound">27</a> then gives $$\label{eq:fixed-j-matrix-bound}
 \|Z\|\leq2\|\Phi_j^{(r)}(Z)\|
 \qquad(r\geq1,\ Z\in M_r(E)).$$

<a id="eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound"></a>
This same $j$ works after tensoring with an arbitrary $C^*$-algebra $C$: for every $z\in E\odot C$, $$\label{eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound}
 \|z\|\leq2\|(\Phi_j\otimes\operatorname{id}_C)z\|,$$ where $\odot$ denotes the algebraic tensor product and all norms are spatial. The assertion is immediate if $C=0$. Otherwise, represent $C$ faithfully on a nonzero Hilbert space $K$, and use faithful representations of $\mathcal U$ and $B_j$ for their respective spatial tensor products. Compress the $C$ representation to a nonzero finite-dimensional subspace $K_0\subset K$. If $z=\sum_i P_i\otimes c_i$, its compression has coefficients $P_{K_0}c_i|_{K_0}\in M_d(\mathbb C)$ and, after interchanging the tensor factors, belongs to $M_d(E)$. Inequality <a href="#eq:fixed-j-matrix-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-j-matrix-bound">[eq:fixed-j-matrix-bound]</a> bounds its norm by twice the norm of the corresponding compression on the $B_j$ side, which is at most $2\|(\Phi_j\otimes\operatorname{id}_C)z\|$. The norms of these compressions have supremum $\|z\|$: as $K_0$ increases, the projections $1\otimes P_{K_0}$ converge strongly to the identity, and their two-sided compressions converge strongly to $z$. This proves <a href="#eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound">[eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound]</a>.

Now fix any ideal $I\subset C$, with quotient map $q:C\to C/I$ and a positive contractive approximate unit $(e_\lambda)$ of $I$. For $z\in E\odot C$, every $z(1\otimes(1-e_\lambda))$ still belongs to $E\odot C$. Consequently <a href="#eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound">[eq:fixed-j-tensor-bound]</a> applies to the entire net with the single index $j(E)$. Formula <a href="#eq:tensor-quotient-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tensor-quotient-norm">[eq:tensor-quotient-norm]</a> and exactness of $B_j$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 \|z+\mathcal U\otimes_{\min}I\|
 &=\lim_\lambda\|z(1\otimes(1-e_\lambda))\|\\
 &\leq2\lim_\lambda
   \|(\Phi_j\otimes\operatorname{id}_C)(z)(1\otimes(1-e_\lambda))\|\\
 &=2\|(\Phi_j\otimes\operatorname{id}_{C/I})((\operatorname{id}_\mathcal U\otimes q)z)\|\\
 &\leq2\|(\operatorname{id}_\mathcal U\otimes q)z\|.
\end{aligned}$$ Finite sums of polynomial tensors are dense in $\mathcal U\otimes_{\min}C$. Although $j(E)$ may vary with the polynomial space, the resulting constant $2$ does not. The last inequality therefore extends by continuity to every $z\in\mathcal U\otimes_{\min}C$. It says that the canonical surjective homomorphism $$\kappa:(\mathcal U\otimes_{\min}C)/(\mathcal U\otimes_{\min}I)
 \longrightarrow\mathcal U\otimes_{\min}(C/I)$$ has zero kernel: if $\kappa(z+\mathcal U\otimes_{\min}I)=0$, the quotient class has norm zero. An injective $*$-homomorphism of $C^*$-algebras is isometric, so the fixed factor $2$ suffices to prove that $\kappa$ is an isomorphism. Since $C,I$ were arbitrary, $\mathcal U$ would be exact, contrary to Lemma <a href="#lem:nonexact-universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:nonexact-universal">25</a>. ◻

</details>

Proposition <a href="#prop:uniform-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:uniform-gap">24</a> therefore supplies a single $\gamma>0$ for all unital exact targets and all matrix sizes. The remaining part of this section converts that obstruction into a perturbation with a quotient norm lower bound.

<a id="sec:perturbation"></a>
#### Uniform perturbation in primitive quotients

We now turn the uniform gap into a perturbation that works in many primitive quotients at once. The fixed amplification condition <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span> supplies finite reducing copies on a spectral cut; the gap then prevents one fixed element from nearly cancelling every such copy. We first record the two consequences of the comparison and transport results in Section <a href="#sec:foundations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:foundations">2</a> that this argument uses.

<a id="stmt-lem-ideal-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:ideal-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:ideal-multiplicity"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Fixed-amplification ideal comparison). *Let $C$ be a $C^*$-algebra, let $h\in C_+$, and let $n\geq1$ be an integer such that $h^{\oplus n}$ is properly infinite. For every $x\in\operatorname{Id}(h)_+$ and every integer $k\geq1$, $$x^{\oplus k}\precsim h^{\oplus n}.
 \label{eq:ideal-multiplicity}$$ In particular, if $C$ satisfies <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span>, this conclusion holds for every $h\in C_+$ with the same $n$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-ideal-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-ideal-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* In the common stabilization, $$\operatorname{Id}_{C\otimes\mathcal K}(h^{\oplus n})
   =\operatorname{Id}_C(h)\otimes\mathcal K.$$ Thus $x^{\oplus k}$ belongs to the ideal generated by $h^{\oplus n}$. The proper-target ideal comparison in Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-rules" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-rules">4</a> gives <a href="#eq:ideal-multiplicity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ideal-multiplicity">[eq:ideal-multiplicity]</a>. This argument takes place in the stabilization and therefore covers every finite rectangular corner and nonunital $C$. It also covers $h=0$, when $x=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-cut-implementation"></a>
<div id="lem:cut-implementation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (Exact implementation of a spectral cut). *Let $C$ be a $C^*$-algebra, let $m,s\geq1$, and suppose that $x\in M_m(C)_+$ and $h\in M_s(C)_+$ satisfy $x\precsim h$. If $f\colon[0,\|x\|]\to[0,\infty)$ is continuous and vanishes on a neighborhood of zero, then there is $R\in M_{s,m}(C)$ such that <a id="eq:exact-cut"></a>$$R^*R=f(x),\qquad RR^*\in\operatorname{Her}(h),\qquad
 \|R\|^2=\|f(x)\|.
 \label{eq:exact-cut}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cut-implementation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cut-implementation</summary>

*Proof.* If $f(x)=0$, take $R=0$. Otherwise, choose $\delta>0$ so that $f$ vanishes on $[0,\delta]$, and choose $0<\varepsilon<\delta$. Then $f(x)^{1/2}\in\operatorname{Her}((x-\varepsilon)_+)$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transport">5</a> to $x\precsim h$ at this cutoff, and let $w\in M_{s,m}(C^{**})$ be its rectangular partial isometry. Its initial projection is the support of $(x-\varepsilon)_+$. The membership and support conclusions of that lemma therefore give $$R=w f(x)^{1/2}\in M_{s,m}(C),\qquad
 R^*R=f(x),\qquad RR^*=w f(x)w^*\in\operatorname{Her}(h).$$ The norm identity follows from $\|R\|^2=\|R^*R\|$. Only the product $R$, rather than $w$ itself, is asserted to belong to the rectangular algebra. ◻

</details>

For $P\in\operatorname{Prim}(C)$, a subscript $P$ denotes the image in $C/P$, also at matrix levels. An irreducible representation with kernel $P$ faithfully represents this quotient and computes its norm. The primitive ideal test in Lemma <a href="#lem:ideals-cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideals-cutoffs">6</a> will let us recognize ideal membership from these quotient images.

The uniform gap will be applied to the unitization of a matrix algebra over $C$. We record the two permanence facts that make this a valid exact target, also when $C$ has no unit.

<a id="stmt-lem-exact-matrix-unitization"></a>
<div id="lem:exact-matrix-unitization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (The exact target for matrix models). *If $A$ is an exact $C^*$-algebra and $n\ge1$, then $\widetilde{M_n(A)}$ is exact.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-exact-matrix-unitization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-exact-matrix-unitization</summary>

*Proof.* Finite matrix exactness follows, for every $C^*$-algebra $D_0$, from the identifications $$M_n(A)\otimes_{\min}D_0=M_n(A\otimes_{\min}D_0),$$ which preserve the ideal kernels in the definition of exactness. For the unitization step, let $A_0$ be a nonunital exact algebra, let $I\triangleleft D_0$ be a closed two-sided ideal, and write $q_0\colon D_0\to D_0/I$. The scalar character $\chi\colon\widetilde A_0\to\mathbb C$ gives, for every $z\in\widetilde A_0\otimes_{\min}D_0$, the bounded decomposition $$z=z_0+1\otimes d,\qquad
 d=(\chi\otimes\operatorname{id})(z),\quad z_0\in A_0\otimes_{\min}D_0.$$ Indeed, both projections are bounded, and the decomposition holds first on algebraic tensors and then on their completion. If $(\operatorname{id}\otimes q_0)(z)=0$, applying $\chi\otimes\operatorname{id}$ gives $d\in I$. The isometric inclusion $$A_0\otimes_{\min}(D_0/I)
 \subset\widetilde A_0\otimes_{\min}(D_0/I)$$ then shows $(\operatorname{id}\otimes q_0)(z_0)=0$. Exactness of $A_0$ gives $z_0\in A_0\otimes_{\min}I$, hence $z\in\widetilde A_0\otimes_{\min}I$. If $M_n(A)$ is already unital, this second step is unnecessary. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:perturbation-constant"></a>
Fix, once and for all, a constant $$0<\sigma<\min\{1,\gamma/3\},
 \label{eq:perturbation-constant}$$ where $\gamma$ is supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:uniform-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:uniform-gap">24</a>. This $\sigma$ is independent of the algebra and of the fixed amplification integer.

<a id="stmt-prop-perturbation"></a>
<div id="prop:perturbation" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:perturbation-hypotheses"></a>
<a id="eq:perturbation-conclusion"></a>
**Proposition 31** (Uniform perturbation). *Let $C$ be an exact $C^*$-algebra satisfying <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span> for one fixed integer $n\geq1$. Let $p,q\in C$ be contractions and let $h,k,c\in C_+$ satisfy $$p^*ph=h,\qquad qq^*k=k,\qquad
 c^{\oplus n}\precsim h,\qquad c^{\oplus n}\precsim k.
 \label{eq:perturbation-hypotheses}$$ For every $T\in C$ and every $\eta,\alpha>0$, there is a contraction $y\in C$ such that $$\|(T+\eta pyq)_P\|\geq\sigma\eta
 \quad\text{for every }P\in\operatorname{Prim}(C)\text{ with }\|c_P\|\geq\alpha.
 \label{eq:perturbation-conclusion}$$ If $h,k$ belong to a hereditary subalgebra $\mathcal D\subset C$, then $y$ may be chosen in $\mathcal D$. If, in addition or independently, $p,q$ are positive and $pq=0$, then $y$ may be chosen selfadjoint. Both conclusions may be imposed simultaneously. The constant $\sigma$ is independent of all the data in the Proposition, including $n$ and the norms of $T,h,k,c$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-perturbation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-perturbation</summary>

*Proof.* Write $K=\{P\in\operatorname{Prim}(C):\|c_P\|\geq\alpha\}$. If $K$ is empty, $y=0$ satisfies all conclusions. Suppose henceforth that $K$ is nonempty. The contradiction will have three levels of choices. First we fix comparison rows and a single contraction in $M_n(C)$. Each finite matrix contraction then determines an admissible perturbation. If it fails, a primitive quotient witnessing that failure supplies an approximate reducing copy for that fixed contraction. All these copies therefore come from one element of the same exact algebra $\widetilde{M_n(C)}$.

<a id="eq:perturbation-row-identities"></a>
<a id="eq:fixed-testing-element"></a>
*Fixed comparison rows.* Choose a continuous function $e\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,1]$ that vanishes near zero and is one on $[\alpha/2,\infty)$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:cut-implementation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cut-implementation">29</a> to the last two comparisons in <a href="#eq:perturbation-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:perturbation-hypotheses">[eq:perturbation-hypotheses]</a>. This gives rows $R,S\in M_{1,n}(C)$ with <a id="eq:perturbation-rows"></a>$$R^*R=S^*S=E=e(c)^{\oplus n},\qquad
 RR^*\in\operatorname{Her}(h),\quad SS^*\in\operatorname{Her}(k),\qquad
 \|R\|,\|S\|\leq1.
 \label{eq:perturbation-rows}$$ The relation $p^*ph=h$, together with its adjoint, shows that left and right multiplication by $p^*p$ are the identity on $\operatorname{Her}(h)$. Hence $(1-p^*p)RR^*(1-p^*p)=0$, which implies $(1-p^*p)R=0$. The same argument for $S$ gives $$p^*pR=R,\qquad qq^*S=S.
 \label{eq:perturbation-row-identities}$$ At this point fix the single element $$V=\eta^{-1}R^*p^*Tq^*S\in M_n(C).
 \label{eq:fixed-testing-element}$$ The rows $R,S$ and the element $V$ will not depend on any finite matrix model. Fix now also the global contraction $$\widehat V=V(\max\{1,|V|\})^{-1}\in M_n(C).$$ The inverse is evaluated in the unitization, so its product with $V$ remains in $M_n(C)$.

<a id="eq:finite-model-column"></a>
*Reducing copies of arbitrary finite contractions.* Let $d\geq1$ and let $D\in M_d(\mathbb C)$ be a contraction. Put $c_1=(c-\alpha/2)_+$. Condition <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span> and Lemma <a href="#lem:ideal-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideal-comparison">28</a>, applied to the scalar element $c_1$, give $$c_1^{\oplus d}\precsim c_1^{\oplus n}.$$ Choose a continuous $\varphi\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,1]$, vanishing near zero and equal to one on $[\alpha/4,\infty)$, and put $e_1(c)=\varphi(c_1)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:cut-implementation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cut-implementation">29</a> yields $L\in M_{n,d}(C)$ such that $$L^*L=e_1(c)^{\oplus d},\qquad
 LL^*\in\operatorname{Her}(c_1^{\oplus n}),\qquad \|L\|\leq1.
 \label{eq:finite-model-column}$$ Because $e(c)c_1=c_1$, multiplication by $E$ is the identity on $\operatorname{Her}(c_1^{\oplus n})$. The range-square argument used for <a href="#eq:perturbation-row-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:perturbation-row-identities">[eq:perturbation-row-identities]</a> gives $EL=L$. Define <a id="eq:finite-model"></a>$$w=LDL^*\in M_n(C),\qquad \|w\|\leq1,\qquad EwE=w.
 \label{eq:finite-model}$$ Scalar matrix multiplication here requires no unit in $C$.

<a id="eq:reducing-model"></a>
For each $P\in K$, choose an irreducible representation $\pi_P\colon C\to B(H_P)$ with kernel $P$, and use its matrix amplifications. Identify $C/P$ with the image of the induced faithful representation on $H_P$, and make the same identification at rectangular matrix levels. The spectral subspace $1_{(3\alpha/4,\infty)}(\pi_P(c))H_P$ is nonzero, since $\|c_P\|\geq\alpha$. Choose a unit vector $\xi_P$ in that subspace, so that $\pi_P(e_1(c))\xi_P=\xi_P$. Define $$J_P\colon\mathbb C^d\longrightarrow H_P^{\oplus n},\qquad
 J_P\lambda=L_P(\lambda\otimes\xi_P).$$ Equation <a href="#eq:finite-model-column" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-model-column">[eq:finite-model-column]</a> proves $J_P^*J_P=1_d$. Both $D$ and $D^*$ preserve $\mathbb C^d\otimes\xi_P$, and the same equation gives $$w_PJ_P=J_PD,\qquad w_P^*J_P=J_PD^*.
 \label{eq:reducing-model}$$ Thus one copy reduces both $w_P$ and its adjoint. No eigenvector at the norm of $c_P$, and no continuous choice of the vectors $\xi_P$, is required.

*Admissible tests and the two optional conclusions.* For the chosen $d,D$, take $$y_0=-RwS^*\in C,\qquad\|y_0\|\leq1.$$ If $h,k\in\mathcal D$, then $RR^*,SS^*\in\mathcal D$. For a positive contractive approximate unit $(a_\lambda)$ of $\mathcal D$, the identity $$\|(1-a_\lambda)R\|^2
 =\|(1-a_\lambda)RR^*(1-a_\lambda)\|\longrightarrow0$$ and its analogue for $S$ give $a_\lambda RwS^*a_\lambda\to RwS^*$ in norm. Each approximant belongs to $\mathcal D C\mathcal D\subset\mathcal D$, so $y_0\in\mathcal D$. This uses the range squares of the rows and makes no assertion that their individual entries belong to $\mathcal D$.

If $p,q\geq0$ and $pq=0$, then $p^2R=R$ implies $pR=R$: multiply $(1-p^2)R=0$ by $(1+p)^{-1}$ in the unitization. Likewise $qS=S$. It follows that $pS=qR=0$ and $S^*R=0$. Consequently $y_0^2=0$ and $py_0^*q=0$. The positive elements $y_0y_0^*$ and $y_0^*y_0$ are orthogonal, whence $$\|y_0+y_0^*\|^2
 =\|y_0y_0^*+y_0^*y_0\|=\|y_0\|^2\leq1.$$ We may therefore use $y=y_0+y_0^*$ when selfadjointness is requested, and $y=y_0$ otherwise. In both cases $pyq=py_0q$; hereditary membership is preserved if it is also requested.

<a id="eq:local-near-model"></a>
*One global contraction and the uniform gap.* Suppose that no admissible $y$ satisfies <a href="#eq:perturbation-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:perturbation-conclusion">[eq:perturbation-conclusion]</a>. For each $d,D$, choose a witness $P\in K$ for the test just constructed. Then $$\|(T-\eta pRwS^*q)_P\|<\sigma\eta.$$ Multiplying by the contractive outer factors $R^*p^*$ and $q^*S$, and using <a href="#eq:perturbation-row-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:perturbation-row-identities">[eq:perturbation-row-identities]</a>, gives $$\|(V-EwE)_P\|=\|(V-w)_P\|<\sigma.
 \label{eq:local-near-model}$$ Although $P$ may depend on $d,D$, the element $V$ is the one fixed in <a href="#eq:fixed-testing-element" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-testing-element">[eq:fixed-testing-element]</a>.

<a id="eq:quotient-clipping"></a>
<a id="eq:forbidden-intertwining"></a>
Functional calculus commutes with quotient maps for the fixed global clipping $\widehat V$. Hence in each witness quotient $$\|V_P-\widehat V_P\|
 =\|(|V_P|-1)_+\|
 =(\|V_P\|-1)_+<\sigma.
 \label{eq:quotient-clipping}$$ The last strict inequality follows from <a href="#eq:local-near-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-near-model">[eq:local-near-model]</a> and $\|w_P\|\leq1$. The clipping identity follows by squaring the absolute value of $V_P-V_P\max\{1,|V_P|\}^{-1}$ and applying scalar functional calculus. Combining <a href="#eq:local-near-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-near-model">[eq:local-near-model]</a>, <a href="#eq:quotient-clipping" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:quotient-clipping">[eq:quotient-clipping]</a>, and <a href="#eq:reducing-model" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:reducing-model">[eq:reducing-model]</a> gives $$\|\widehat V_PJ_P-J_PD\|<2\sigma<\gamma,\qquad
 \|\widehat V_P^*J_P-J_PD^*\|<2\sigma<\gamma.
 \label{eq:forbidden-intertwining}$$

By Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-matrix-unitization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-matrix-unitization">30</a>, $B=\widetilde{M_n(C)}$ is a unital exact algebra. The amplified representations $\pi_P^{(n)}$ extend unitally to $B$. Although the witnessing $P$ varies with $d,D$, the contraction $\widehat V$ and the exact algebra $B$ remain fixed. Thus $\widehat V\in B$ has, for every $d,D$, a unital representation and an isometry satisfying both inequalities in <a href="#eq:forbidden-intertwining" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:forbidden-intertwining">[eq:forbidden-intertwining]</a>. This contradicts Proposition <a href="#prop:uniform-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:uniform-gap">24</a> and proves the Proposition with both optional conclusions and the same $\sigma$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:localization"></a>
### Exact localization and removal of amplification

We now use Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">31</a> to prove the fixed-amplification part of Theorem <a href="#thm:exact-infiniteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:exact-infiniteness">2</a>. Throughout this section, let $C$ be an exact $C^*$-algebra satisfying <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span> for one fixed integer $n\geq1$, and retain the constant $0<\sigma<1$ from <a href="#eq:perturbation-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:perturbation-constant">[eq:perturbation-constant]</a>. For $P\in\operatorname{Prim}(C)$, write $a_P=a+P$, with its norm computed in any irreducible representation having kernel $P$. A hereditary subalgebra $\mathcal D\subset C$ is nonzero at $P$ when $\mathcal D\not\subset P$.

Our immediate objective is to construct a square-zero element in a hereditary subalgebra that is nonzero on a prescribed quasi-compact subset of $\operatorname{Prim}(C)$. Its initial and range squares are orthogonal and Cuntz equivalent. Repeating the construction on one fixed norm slice will remove the amplification integer $n$ and give <span class="upright">(P)</span>. Theorem <a href="#thm:individual-to-strong" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:individual-to-strong">1</a> will then supply the strong pure infiniteness conclusion. All finite covers in the construction concern the primitive ideal space; the partition of unity used below is on a real interval.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Local elements and finite covers

The next lemma uses only <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span>: finite-dimensional hereditary images are excluded, and irreducibility then supplies common positive elements locally. Exactness enters later through Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">31</a>, when these local choices must work simultaneously on a whole quasi-compact set.

<a id="stmt-lem-local-common-element"></a>
<div id="lem:local-common-element" class="lemma">

**Lemma 32** (Local common elements). *Let $C$ be an exact $C^*$-algebra satisfying <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span> for one fixed integer $n\geq1$, and let $P\in\operatorname{Prim}(C)$.*

1.  *If $h\in C_+$ and $h_P\ne0$, then for every integer $m\ge1$ there are $m$ pairwise orthogonal elements of $\operatorname{Her}(h)_+$, each nonzero at $P$.*

2.  *If $h,k\in C_+$ are nonzero at $P$, there is $c\in C_+$ such that $c_P\ne0$ and $$c^{\oplus n}\precsim h,\qquad c^{\oplus n}\precsim k.$$ If $h,k$ belong to a hereditary subalgebra $\mathcal D$, $c$ can also be chosen in $\mathcal D$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-common-element"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-common-element</summary>

*Proof.* Fix an irreducible representation $\pi:C\to B(H_\pi)$ with kernel $P$. First, no nonzero $\pi(a)$, $a\in C_+$, has finite rank. Indeed, if its rank were $d\in\mathbb N$, applying the amplified representation to $a^{\oplus 2n}\precsim a^{\oplus n}$ would express an operator of rank $2nd$ as a norm limit of operators of rank at most $nd$. This is impossible, since the set of operators of rank at most a fixed finite integer is norm closed.

Put $H_h=\overline{\pi(h)H_\pi}$. We claim that the restriction of $\pi(\operatorname{Her}(h))$ to $H_h$ is strongly dense in $B(H_h)$. Irreducibility and the bicommutant theorem (B. Blackadar 2006, 122:I.9.1.1) <a id="pm-cite-55"></a>[[Blackadar](#ref-Blackadar)] give strong density of $\pi(C)$ in $B(H_\pi)$. Consequently the strong closure of $\pi(\operatorname{Her}(h))$ contains $\pi(h)B(H_\pi)\pi(h)$. For $t>0$, let $E_t=1_{[t,\infty)}(\pi(h))$. Every operator $E_tTE_t$, with $T\in B(H_h)$ extended by zero on $H_h^\perp$, has the form $\pi(h)S\pi(h)$: the inverse of $\pi(h)$ on $E_tH_\pi$ is bounded. As $t\downarrow0$, $E_tTE_t\to T$ strongly on $H_h$. This proves the claim.

The space $H_h$ is infinite dimensional by the finite-rank obstruction. Choose orthonormal vectors $\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_m$ in $H_h$ and distinct numbers $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_m\in(0,1)$. Let $T\in B(H_h)$ be the positive contraction with $T\xi_j=\lambda_j\xi_j$ and zero on their orthogonal complement. Choose nonnegative continuous functions $f_j$ on $\mathbb R$, with disjoint compact supports in $(0,1)$ and $f_j(\lambda_j)=1$. Kaplansky density (B. Blackadar 2006, 122:I.9.1.3) <a id="pm-cite-56"></a>[[Blackadar](#ref-Blackadar)] supplies a bounded net of selfadjoint elements $b_\lambda$ of the represented hereditary algebra converging strongly to $T$. Continuous functional calculus on bounded selfadjoint sets is strongly continuous; this follows by uniform polynomial approximation on a common bounded interval. Hence $f_j(b_\lambda)\xi_j\to\xi_j$. One $b_\lambda$ makes all these $m$ vectors nonzero. Lift it to a selfadjoint $b\in\operatorname{Her}(h)$. The elements $f_j(b)$ lie in $\operatorname{Her}(h)$, are pairwise orthogonal, and are nonzero at $P$. The functions vanish at zero, so this argument also applies to a nonunital hereditary algebra. This proves <span class="upright">(i)</span>.

For two positives $u,v$ nonzero at $P$, strong density of $\pi(C)$ gives $x\in C$ with $\pi(uxv)\ne0$. For example, choose a vector on which $\pi(v)$ is nonzero and a rank-one operator carrying its image to a vector not annihilated by $\pi(u)$; approximate that operator strongly on the image under $\pi(v)$. With $z=uxv$, the positive $d=zz^*$ satisfies $$d_P\ne0,\qquad d\in\operatorname{Her}(u),\qquad
 d\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}z^*z\in\operatorname{Her}(v).$$ Here and below $\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}$ denotes Cuntz equivalence, and the comparison rules are those of Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-rules" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-rules">4</a>. For a finite list of positives nonzero at $P$, repeating this operation produces one positive, nonzero at $P$, subequivalent to every element of the list. At each step the new element lies in the hereditary algebra of the preceding one, so all previous comparisons and any prescribed hereditary membership persist.

Apply <span class="upright">(i)</span> with $m=n$ separately to $h$ and $k$, obtaining orthogonal lists $h_1,\ldots,h_n$ and $k_1,\ldots,k_n$. Take a common element $c$ for the resulting list of $2n$ positives. Then $$c^{\oplus n}\precsim\bigoplus_{j=1}^n h_j
 \sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}\sum_{j=1}^n h_j\precsim h,$$ and the same argument gives $c^{\oplus n}\precsim k$. The asserted choice in $\mathcal D$ follows from the hereditary membership just observed. ◻

</details>

We record the norm-attainment fact that ensures a strict inequality in the finite-cover argument.

<a id="stmt-lem-purestateattainment"></a>
<div id="lem:purestateattainment" class="lemma">

**Lemma 33** (Pure-state norm attainment). *If $B$ is a $C^*$-algebra and $0\ne b\in B_+$, there is an irreducible representation $\pi$ of $B$ with $\|\pi(b)\|=\|b\|$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-purestateattainment"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-purestateattainment</summary>

*Proof.* In the state space of the unitization $\widetilde B$, the set $$F_b=\{\varphi:\varphi(b)=\|b\|\}$$ is nonempty: evaluation at the largest spectral value of $b$ on $C^*(1,b)$ extends to a state of $\widetilde B$ by the positive Hahn–Banach extension theorem (B. Blackadar 2006, 122:II.6.3.1) <a id="pm-cite-57"></a>[[Blackadar](#ref-Blackadar)]. It is a weak-star compact face, since every state has value at most $\|b\|$ on $b$. By the Krein–Milman theorem it has an extreme point, which is therefore a pure state of $\widetilde B$; see (B. Blackadar 2006, 122:II.6.3.2–3) <a id="pm-cite-58"></a>[[Blackadar](#ref-Blackadar)]. Let $\widetilde\pi$ be its irreducible GNS representation (B. Blackadar 2006, 122:II.6.4.8) <a id="pm-cite-59"></a>[[Blackadar](#ref-Blackadar)]. It does not annihilate $B$, because its value on $b$ is positive. The closure of $\widetilde\pi(B)H_{\widetilde\pi}$ is a nonzero reducing subspace for $\widetilde\pi(\widetilde B)$, hence the whole space. If an operator commutes with $\widetilde\pi(B)$, then its commutator with $\widetilde\pi(x)$ annihilates $\widetilde\pi(B)H_{\widetilde\pi}$ for every $x\in\widetilde B$, since $xB\subset B$. It therefore commutes with $\widetilde\pi(\widetilde B)$. Thus the restriction $\pi=\widetilde\pi|_B$ is nondegenerate and irreducible. The vector state gives $\|\pi(b)\|\ge\|b\|$, and contractivity gives equality. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-primitive-compactness"></a>
<div id="lem:primitive-compactness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 34** (Primitive norm slices). *Let $C$ be a $C^*$-algebra. For $c\in C_+$ and $\alpha>0$, the set $$\Omega(c,\alpha)=\{P\in\operatorname{Prim}(C):\|c_P\|>\alpha\}$$ is open, and $$K(c,\alpha)=\{P\in\operatorname{Prim}(C):\|c_P\|\ge\alpha\}$$ is quasi-compact. No Hausdorff hypothesis is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-primitive-compactness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-primitive-compactness</summary>

*Proof.* The first set is $\{P:(c-\alpha)_+\notin P\}$, which is open in the hull-kernel topology. For the second assertion, extend any relative open cover to ambient open sets. Write those sets as $$\mathcal O_\lambda=\{P:J_\lambda\not\subset P\},
 \qquad I=\overline{\sum_\lambda J_\lambda},$$ where each $J_\lambda$ is a closed ideal of $C$. If $\|c+I\|\ge\alpha$, Lemma <a href="#lem:purestateattainment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:purestateattainment">33</a> applied in $C/I$ gives an irreducible representation attaining that norm. Its pullback has a primitive kernel containing $I$, belongs to $K(c,\alpha)$, and lies outside every $\mathcal O_\lambda$. This contradicts the covering property. Thus $\|c+I\|<\alpha$; if $C/I=0$, the same inequality is immediate. Choose $d\in I$ with $\|c-d\|<\alpha$, and then approximate $d$ by an element $d_F\in\sum_{\lambda\in F}J_\lambda$ for a finite index set $F$, closely enough that $\|c-d_F\|<\alpha$ still holds. Any primitive ideal containing every $J_\lambda$, $\lambda\in F$, has $\|c_P\|<\alpha$. Hence the finitely many $\mathcal O_\lambda$, $\lambda\in F$, cover $K(c,\alpha)$. The proof uses quasi-compactness only and does not assert that $K(c,\alpha)$ is closed. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-coupling"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-coupling" class="lemma">

**Lemma 35** (Finite coupling). *Let $C$ be an exact $C^*$-algebra satisfying <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span> for one fixed integer $n\geq1$. Let $K_0\subset\operatorname{Prim}(C)$ be quasi-compact and let $\mathcal D\subset C$ be hereditary. Suppose that at each $P\in K_0$ one can choose positive contractions $p,q\in\mathcal D$ and $h,k\in\mathcal D_+$ satisfying $$pq=0,\qquad ph=h,\qquad qk=k,\qquad h_P\ne0,\quad k_P\ne0.$$ There are finitely many such choices $(p_i,q_i,h_i,k_i)$ and one selfadjoint $U\in\mathcal D$ such that, at every $P\in K_0$, $$(p_iUq_i)_P\ne0$$ for at least one selected index $i$. Any support conditions in the original choices are retained in the selected ones.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-coupling"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-coupling</summary>

*Proof.* If $K_0$ is empty, choose $U=0$ and the empty family. Otherwise, Lemma <a href="#lem:local-common-element" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-common-element">32</a> associates to every original choice a positive $c$ nonzero at its specified point, with $c^{\oplus n}\precsim h,k$. Choose $0<\alpha<\|c_P\|$. The resulting open sets $\Omega(c,\alpha)$ cover $K_0$, so choose a finite subcover $\Omega_i=\Omega(c_i,\alpha_i)$, $1\le i\le N$.

Set $\theta=\sigma/4$, $\eta_i=\theta^{i-1}$, and $U_0=0$. At stage $i$, apply Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">31</a> with $T=p_iU_{i-1}q_i$, scale $\eta_i$, and the selected data. The identities $p_ih_i=h_i$, $q_ik_i=k_i$ imply $p_i^2h_i=h_i$, $q_i^2k_i=k_i$. The simultaneous hereditary and selfadjoint options give a selfadjoint contraction $y_i\in\mathcal D$ such that $U_i=U_{i-1}+\eta_i y_i$ satisfies $$\|(p_iU_iq_i)_P\|\ge\sigma\eta_i
 \qquad(P\in K(c_i,\alpha_i)).$$ Let $U=U_N$. Later perturbations change the $i$th corner by at most $$\sum_{j>i}\eta_j
 \le\eta_i\frac{\theta}{1-\theta}
 =\eta_i\frac{\sigma}{4-\sigma}
 <\frac{\sigma\eta_i}{2}.$$ Consequently <a id="eq:finite-coupling-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:finite-coupling-bound}
 \|(p_iUq_i)_P\|>\frac{\sigma\eta_i}{2}
 \qquad(P\in K(c_i,\alpha_i)).$$ In particular each selected corner is nonzero throughout its entire open set $\Omega_i$, proving the assertion. ◻

</details>

When applying Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-coupling">35</a>, it suffices to produce two orthogonal positives $u,v$ nonzero at the specified point. Indeed, choose $0<\beta<\|u_P\|$ and a continuous $f:[0,\infty)\to[0,1]$ that is zero near zero and one on $[\beta,\infty)$. Then $p=f(u)$ and $h=(u-\beta)_+$ satisfy $ph=h$ and $h_P\ne0$. The analogous construction from $v$ gives $q,k$. All four elements retain the hereditary and spectral support restrictions of $u,v$.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### A square-zero element on a quasi-compact set

<a id="stmt-prop-square-zero"></a>
<div id="prop:square-zero" class="proposition">

**Proposition 36**. *Let $C$ be an exact $C^*$-algebra satisfying <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span> for one fixed integer $n\geq1$. Let $K_0\subset\operatorname{Prim}(C)$ be quasi-compact, and let $\mathcal D\subset C$ be a hereditary subalgebra nonzero at every point of $K_0$. There exists $x\in\mathcal D$ such that $$x^2=0,\qquad x_P\ne0\quad(P\in K_0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-square-zero"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-square-zero</summary>

*Proof.* If $K_0=\varnothing$, take $x=0$. Assume henceforth that $K_0$ is nonempty. We first construct a selfadjoint element that is nonscalar on every relevant hereditary support space. Coupling its separated spectral windows and deleting the nearby spectral blocks will produce a selfadjoint element whose positive and negative parts are both nonzero at every point of $K_0$. A final coupling of those two orthogonal parts gives the required square-zero element.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### First coupling: a nonscalar selfadjoint element.

<a id="eq:nonscalarU"></a>
At each $P\in K_0$, choose $h\in\mathcal D_+$ with $h_P\ne0$. Lemma <a href="#lem:local-common-element" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-common-element">32</a><span class="upright">(i)</span>, with $m=2$, provides two orthogonal positives in $\operatorname{Her}(h)\subset\mathcal D$ nonzero at $P$. Apply the preceding cutoff construction to these two positives and use the resulting four inputs in Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-coupling">35</a> to obtain $U=U^*\in\mathcal D$. For an irreducible representation $\pi$ with kernel $P$, put $H_{\mathcal D,\pi}=\overline{\pi(\mathcal D)H_\pi}$. This space reduces $\pi(\mathcal D)$, which acts as zero on its orthogonal complement. At $P$ one selected $pUq$ is nonzero. Since $p,q\in\mathcal D$ and $pq=0$, this implies $$\label{eq:nonscalarU}
 \pi(U)|_{H_{\mathcal D,\pi}}\text{ is not a scalar operator}
 \qquad(P\in K_0).$$ In particular, nonscalarity is obtained on the support space of $\mathcal D$, without using its zero action on the complement.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
###### Second coupling: separated spectral windows.

At a specified $P\in K_0$, the selfadjoint operator in <a href="#eq:nonscalarU" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:nonscalarU">[eq:nonscalarU]</a> has two distinct spectral values. Choose disjoint closed intervals $I,J\subset\mathbb R$ at positive distance, and continuous nonnegative functions $F,G$ supported in $I,J$, respectively, such that both $F(\pi(U))|_{H_{\mathcal D,\pi}}$ and $G(\pi(U))|_{H_{\mathcal D,\pi}}$ are nonzero. The functions may be chosen on all of $\mathbb R$ with compact support. If a window contains zero, its function need not vanish at zero. We evaluate scalar functions in $\mathcal D+\mathbb C1$ inside a fixed unitization $\widetilde C$ of $C$, and take spectral projections in $\widetilde C^{**}$. Let $(d_\lambda)$ be a positive contractive approximate identity of $\mathcal D$. The positive elements $$F(U)d_\lambda F(U),\qquad G(U)d_\lambda G(U)$$ belong to $\mathcal D$. Their images on $H_{\mathcal D,\pi}$ converge strongly to $F(\pi(U))^2$ and $G(\pi(U))^2$, respectively, so a choice of middle elements makes both images nonzero. These positives are supported, globally in the bidual, on $1_I(U)$ and $1_J(U)$.

<a id="eq:spectralwindowV"></a>
The same cutoff construction gives the corresponding $p,q,h,k$, retaining these spectral supports. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-coupling">35</a> again to obtain $V=V^*\in\mathcal D$ and finitely many selected pairs $(p_i,q_i)$, supported in windows $(I_i,J_i)$, such that $$\label{eq:spectralwindowV}
 \begin{gathered}
 p_i=1_{I_i}(U)p_i1_{I_i}(U),\qquad
 q_i=1_{J_i}(U)q_i1_{J_i}(U),\\
 \text{for every }P\in K_0\text{ there is }i
 \text{ with }(p_iVq_i)_P\ne0.
 \end{gathered}$$ Put $\rho=\min_i\operatorname{dist}(I_i,J_i)>0$. The positive elements $p_i,q_i$ need not commute with $U$; only the displayed support identities will be used.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### Spectral deletion produces both signs.

We retain the corners in <a href="#eq:spectralwindowV" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spectralwindowV">[eq:spectralwindowV]</a> while removing all sufficiently narrow diagonal spectral blocks. A positive operator with all those diagonal blocks zero must vanish; the same holds for a negative operator. Since the retained corners prevent vanishing, this will force both signs in every quotient over $K_0$.

<a id="eq:deletionH"></a>
Fix a compact interval $[-M,M]$ containing the spectrum of $U$ in the unitization. Take a finite continuous nonnegative partition of unity $f_1,\ldots,f_s$ on this interval, whose supports $S_j$ have diameter less than $\rho/8$. Define $$\label{eq:deletionH}
 H=\sum_{\operatorname{dist}(S_j,S_k)>\rho/2}
       f_j(U)Vf_k(U).$$ The summation uses ordered pairs, and its condition is symmetric in $j,k$, so $H=H^*$. Although a factor $f_j(U)$ may have a scalar part, it lies in $\mathcal D+\mathbb C1$; the middle factor $V\in\mathcal D$ implies $H\in\mathcal D$.

<a id="eq:window-preservation"></a>
For each selected pair $(p_i,q_i)$ we have $$\label{eq:window-preservation}
 p_iHq_i=p_iVq_i.$$ Indeed, expand $p_iVq_i$ using $\sum_jf_j(U)=1$ on both sides. For a summand $p_if_j(U)Vf_k(U)q_i$ to be nonzero, the supports $S_j,S_k$ must meet $I_i,J_i$, respectively, by <a href="#eq:spectralwindowV" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spectralwindowV">[eq:spectralwindowV]</a>. Their distances then satisfy $$\operatorname{dist}(S_j,S_k)
 \ge\operatorname{dist}(I_i,J_i)
       -\operatorname{diam}(S_j)-\operatorname{diam}(S_k)
 >\frac{3\rho}{4}>\frac\rho2.$$ Every potentially nonzero summand is therefore retained in <a href="#eq:deletionH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:deletionH">[eq:deletionH]</a>. This argument requires no commutation of $p_i,q_i$ with $U$. Equations <a href="#eq:spectralwindowV" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spectralwindowV">[eq:spectralwindowV]</a> and <a href="#eq:window-preservation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:window-preservation">[eq:window-preservation]</a> show that $H_P\ne0$ for every $P\in K_0$.

Let $B$ be any Borel interval of length less than $\rho/4$. The two supports in a retained pair cannot both meet $B$, since their distance exceeds $\rho/2$. Hence $$1_B(U)H1_B(U)=0.$$ Partition $[-M,M]$ into finitely many disjoint Borel intervals $B_j$ of length less than $\rho/4$, assigning endpoints so that their spectral projections sum to the identity. In any unital extension of a representation, the resulting projections $E_j$ thus satisfy $E_j\pi(H)E_j=0$ and $\sum_jE_j=1$. If $\pi(H)\ge0$, then $$\|\pi(H)^{1/2}E_j\xi\|^2
 =\langle E_j\pi(H)E_j\xi,\xi\rangle=0$$ for every $j,\xi$, whence $\pi(H)=0$. Applying the same argument to $-\pi(H)$ excludes a nonzero negative semidefinite image. The spectral projection calculations can equivalently be made in the bidual and passed through the normal extensions of the representations. We conclude that <a id="eq:bothsigns"></a>$$\label{eq:bothsigns}
 (H_+)_P\ne0,\qquad (H_-)_P\ne0
 \qquad(P\in K_0).$$ Both positive parts belong to $\mathcal D$ and are orthogonal.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### Third coupling: the square-zero product.

At every $P\in K_0$, apply the same cutoff construction to $H_+,H_-$ to obtain $p,h\in\operatorname{Her}(H_+)$ and $q,k\in\operatorname{Her}(H_-)$ for Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-coupling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-coupling">35</a>. The contractions $p,q$ are orthogonal. The resulting $U'=U'^*\in\mathcal D$ has a selected nonzero corner at every point of $K_0$. Put $$x=H_+U'H_-\in\mathcal D.$$ If $x_P=0$, then $(pU'q)_P=0$ for every $p\in\operatorname{Her}(H_+)$ and $q\in\operatorname{Her}(H_-)$: approximate them in norm by elements $H_+aH_+$ and $H_-bH_-$, and use $$(H_+aH_+)U'(H_-bH_-)=H_+a\,x\,bH_-.$$ This contradicts the selected nonzero corner at $P$. Thus $x_P\ne0$ throughout $K_0$, while $x^2=H_+U'(H_-H_+)U'H_-=0$. All constructed elements lie in $\mathcal D$; spectral projections have only been used to verify their supports. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Removing the amplification

We have constructed a square-zero element on every prescribed quasi-compact set where a hereditary subalgebra is nonzero. We now apply this result repeatedly on the same primitive norm slice. The resulting number of orthogonal equivalent copies can be made at least the single amplification integer in <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span>.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-square-zero-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-square-zero</summary>

<a id="eq:individual-infiniteness"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:exact-infiniteness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:exact-infiniteness">2</a>.* Fix $a\in C_+$ and $r>0$. The set $K_0=K(a,r)$ is quasi-compact by Lemma <a href="#lem:primitive-compactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:primitive-compactness">34</a>. If it is empty, every primitive image of $(a-r)_+$ is zero, so $(a-r)_+=0$, and $((a-r)_+)^{\oplus2}\precsim a$ is immediate. Suppose that $K_0$ is nonempty. Proposition <a href="#prop:square-zero" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:square-zero">36</a> applied to $\operatorname{Her}(a)$ gives $x\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ with $x^2=0$ and $x_P\ne0$ throughout $K_0$. Set $z_1=x^*x$. Its Cuntz-equivalent positive $xx^*$ is orthogonal to it, since $(x^*x)(xx^*)=x^*x^2x^*=0$. Therefore $$z_1^{\oplus2}\sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}z_1\oplus xx^*
 \sim_{\mathrm{Cu}}z_1+xx^*\precsim a,
 \qquad (z_1)_P\ne0\quad(P\in K_0).$$ Repeat the construction inside $\operatorname{Her}(z_1)$, and then inside successive hereditary subalgebras, always over the same $K_0$. It yields $z_j\in C_+$ nonzero throughout $K_0$ and $z_{j+1}^{\oplus2}\precsim z_j$. Finite direct sums and transitivity give $$z_j^{\oplus 2^j}\precsim a.$$ Choose a finite $j\ge1$ with $2^j\ge n$. Dropping extra summands gives $z_j^{\oplus n}\precsim a$. If $(z_j)_P=0$, then $P\notin K_0$, so $\|a_P\|<r$ and $((a-r)_+)_P=0$. The primitive ideal test in Lemma <a href="#lem:ideals-cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideals-cutoffs">6</a> therefore gives $(a-r)_+\in\operatorname{Id}(z_j)$. Condition <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span> makes $z_j^{\oplus n}$ properly infinite because $z_j$ is a scalar positive element. Lemma <a href="#lem:ideal-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideal-comparison">28</a>, used with two copies on the left, then implies $$((a-r)_+)^{\oplus2}\precsim z_j^{\oplus n}\precsim a.$$ Only scalar positives $z_j\in C$ have been tested against <span class="upright">(F$_n$)</span>. Finally, $\|a-(a-r)_+\|\le r$. As $r\downarrow0$, norm-limit closure of comparison with the fixed target $a$ gives $$\label{eq:individual-infiniteness}
 a\oplus a\precsim a\qquad(a\in C_+).$$ This includes $a=0$; when $n=1$, it also follows directly from the hypothesis. Therefore $C$ satisfies <span class="upright">(P)</span>. Theorem <a href="#thm:individual-to-strong" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:individual-to-strong">1</a> now gives strong pure infiniteness, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

The removal of amplification also gives the comparison form needed for the direct quantitative argument in Section <a href="#sec:quantitative" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:quantitative">9</a>. The following conclusion concerns the entire closed ideal, rather than only one cutoff or primitive norm slice.

<a id="stmt-cor-ideal-comparison"></a>
<div id="cor:ideal-comparison" class="corollary">

**Corollary 37** (Whole-ideal comparison). *Let $C$ be a $C^*$-algebra satisfying <span class="upright">(P)</span>. For every $h\in C_+$, $c\in\operatorname{Id}(h)_+$, and integer $k\ge1$, $$c^{\oplus k}\precsim h.$$ If $C$ is exact, Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">31</a> may be applied with multiplicity $n=1$, with the same constant $\sigma$ and all its stated options.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-ideal-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-ideal-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Property <span class="upright">(P)</span> makes $h$ properly infinite, so Lemma <a href="#lem:ideal-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ideal-comparison">28</a> applies with multiplicity one. If $h=0$, the ideal is zero and the assertion is immediate. For exact $C$, property <span class="upright">(P)</span> is precisely the amplification hypothesis of Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">31</a> with $n=1$ for every scalar positive element. Its constant was fixed independently of $n$, so neither the constant nor the hereditary and selfadjoint options change. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:quantitative"></a>
### Direct quantitative separation under exactness

We give a direct proof of strong pure infiniteness from the exact perturbation principle. Throughout this section, $C$ is exact and satisfies $\textup{(P)}$. This is $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$ with $n=1$, so Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">31</a> is available with that multiplicity and its fixed constant $0<\sigma<1$. Corollary <a href="#cor:ideal-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:ideal-comparison">37</a> applies to the whole ideal $\operatorname{Id}(h)$ generated by each positive $h$. For an exact algebra satisfying $\textup{(F\ensuremath{_n})}$, the scalar conclusion <a href="#eq:individual-infiniteness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:individual-infiniteness">[eq:individual-infiniteness]</a> proved in Section <a href="#sec:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:localization">8</a> supplies $\textup{(P)}$ before that section invokes the general theorem.

The quantitative feature of this proof is a fixed lower norm bound in primitive quotients, retained while a mixed product tends to zero. We first construct positive elements in two prescribed hereditary subalgebras with this property. Spectral clipping then supplies bounded comparison coefficients, so the small product survives Cuntz implementation. The resulting estimates verify the strong-infiniteness inequalities directly. Thus this proof uses the comparison tools and the exact perturbation and localization results in Sections <a href="#sec:foundations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:foundations">2</a>, <a href="#sec:exactness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:exactness">7</a>, and <a href="#sec:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:localization">8</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-separating-positives"></a>
<div id="lem:separating-positives" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:separation-quotient-bounds"></a>
<a id="eq:separation-product-bound"></a>
**Lemma 38** (Separation with a fixed quotient norm bound). *Let $C$ be an exact $C^*$-algebra satisfying $\textup{(P)}$, and let $0<\sigma<1$ be the constant in Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">31</a> with $n=1$. Let $a,b\in C_+$ be contractions, and let $0<r<1$. Set $$m=\frac{r\sigma^2}{1024}.$$ For every $0<\ell<1/10$ there are positives $Q_a\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ and $Q_b\in\operatorname{Her}(b)$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \|(Q_a)_P\|&\ge m &&\text{if }\|a_P\|\ge r,\notag\\
 \|(Q_b)_P\|&\ge m &&\text{if }\|b_P\|\ge r,
 \label{eq:separation-quotient-bounds}\\
 \|Q_aQ_b\|&\le20\sqrt\ell.
 \label{eq:separation-product-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ Here and below $P$ ranges over $\operatorname{Prim}(C)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-separating-positives"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-separating-positives</summary>

*Proof.* Write the polar decomposition in $C^{**}$ as $$ab=vu,\qquad u=|ab|,\qquad u_*=|(ab)^*|.$$ All three positive elements $a,b,u$ are contractions. Choose continuous functions on $[0,1]$ to separate the small singular values of $ab$ from a spectral corner on which an inverse square root is available. The small values will give small cross products; on the remaining corner we will preserve exact cancellation while perturbing the two sides separately. Use the following functions:

- $0\le L\le1$, $L=1$ on $[0,5\ell]$, and $L=0$ on $[6\ell,1]$; put $N=1-L$;

- $0\le g\le1$, $g=0$ on $[0,\ell]$, and $g=1$ on $[2\ell,1]$;

- $0\le R\le1$, $R=0$ on $[0,\ell/2]$, and $R(s)=\sqrt{\ell/s}$ for $s\ge\ell$.

Linear interpolation on the unspecified intervals gives such functions. A function letter without an argument denotes evaluation at $u$. $L$ selects the small singular values, $g$ confines the perturbations away from zero, and $R$ normalizes $u$ on that region. Functional calculus and the polar decomposition give <a id="eq:separation-functional-identities"></a>$$\label{eq:separation-functional-identities}
 L(u_*)v=vL(u),\qquad N(u_*)=vNv^*,\qquad
 gN=N,\qquad (RuR-\ell)g=0.$$ The second identity uses $N(0)=0$; the first remains valid with $L(0)=1$. Moreover $$vR=ab\,\frac{R(u)}u\in C,\qquad
 vN=ab\,\frac{N(u)}u\in C,$$ where both quotient functions are extended continuously by zero at $s=0$, since $R$ and $N$ vanish near zero. We use the unitization for scalar terms throughout this proof.

<a id="eq:separation-inverse-conditions"></a>
<a id="eq:separation-four-columns"></a>
*The four columns and their products.* We first specify conditions under which four columns have the product estimates we need. Suppose that orthogonal positive contractions $e,f\in C$ and invertibles $Z,W\in\widetilde C$ satisfy $$\label{eq:separation-inverse-conditions}
 ge=e,\quad gf=f,\quad Z^*W=1,\quad
 Z=1+gzg,\quad W=1+gwg,\quad
 \|Z\|,\|W\|\le2$$ for some $z,w\in C$. For such a choice, define $$\label{eq:separation-four-columns}
 \begin{array}{ll}
 X_0=aL(u_*),&X=\ell^{-1/2}avRZe,\\[2pt]
 Y_0=bL(u),&Y=\ell^{-1/2}bRWf.
 \end{array}$$ These elements belong to $C$: the only polar factor is $vR\in C$, and all unitized factors occur multiplied by elements of $C$. For any such admissible choice, the proposed outputs are <a id="eq:separation-final-positives"></a>$$\label{eq:separation-final-positives}
 Q_a=(X_0X_0^*+XX^*)^{1/2},\qquad
 Q_b=(Y_0Y_0^*+YY^*)^{1/2}.$$ The norm of each positive output dominates the norms of its two columns in every primitive quotient. At the end, a row polar decomposition will bound the product of these outputs by the four cross products estimated next.

<a id="eq:separation-high-orthogonality"></a>
<a id="eq:separation-mixed-bounds"></a>
Put $E_R=RuR-\ell$. Since $E_Rg=0$ and $f=gf$, we have $$E_RWf=E_Rf+E_Rgwgf=0.$$ In particular, no commutation of $W$ with $g$ is needed. Using $v^*ab=u$ and retaining the indicated order of factors gives $$\label{eq:separation-high-orthogonality}
 X^*Y=\ell^{-1}eZ^*RuRWf=eZ^*Wf=ef=0.$$ The other three products obey $$\label{eq:separation-mixed-bounds}
 \|X_0^*Y_0\|\le6\ell,\qquad
 \|X^*Y_0\|\le2\sqrt{6\ell},\qquad
 \|X_0^*Y\|\le2\sqrt{6\ell}.$$ Indeed, $$\begin{aligned}
 X_0^*Y_0&=vL(u)uL(u),\\
 X^*Y_0&=\ell^{-1/2}eZ^*RuL(u),\\
 X_0^*Y&=\ell^{-1/2}vL(u)uRWf.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\|uL(u)\|\le6\ell$. For $0\le s\le\ell$, $\ell^{-1/2}R(s)sL(s)\le\sqrt\ell$; for $s\ge\ell$ the same expression equals $\sqrt{s}L(s)\le\sqrt{6\ell}$. Together with <a href="#eq:separation-inverse-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-inverse-conditions">[eq:separation-inverse-conditions]</a>, these scalar estimates prove <a href="#eq:separation-mixed-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-mixed-bounds">[eq:separation-mixed-bounds]</a>. We now construct $e,f,Z,W$ so that, in each prescribed primitive quotient, at least one of the two relevant columns also has a fixed lower norm bound.

<a id="eq:separation-exact-cores"></a>
<a id="eq:separation-core-norms"></a>
*Two orthogonal cores.* Set $h_0=(u-2\ell)_+$. Since $h_0\oplus h_0\precsim h_0$, Lemma <a href="#lem:cut-implementation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cut-implementation">29</a> supplies a row $[r_1,r_2]\in M_{1,2}(C)$ such that $$\label{eq:separation-exact-cores}
 r_i^*r_j=\begin{cases}\theta(h_0),&i=j,\\0,&i\ne j,\end{cases}
 \qquad r_1r_1^*+r_2r_2^*\in\operatorname{Her}(h_0),$$ where $\theta\colon[0,\infty)\to[0,1]$ is continuous, vanishes near zero, and equals one on $[2\ell,\infty)$. Define $$e_0=r_1r_1^*,\qquad f_0=r_2r_2^*.$$ They are orthogonal positive contractions in $\operatorname{Her}(h_0)$; each belongs to this hereditary algebra because it is dominated by their sum. In any primitive quotient with $\|u_P\|\ge4\ell$, $\|(h_0)_P\|\ge2\ell$, and hence $$\label{eq:separation-core-norms}
 \|(e_0)_P\|=\|(f_0)_P\|=\|\theta((h_0)_P)\|=1.$$ This includes equality at the threshold $4\ell$ by the chosen plateau.

<a id="eq:separation-core-identities"></a>
Put $$k_e=(e_0-1/2)_+,\qquad k_f=(f_0-1/2)_+,$$ and let $e=\chi(e_0)$ and $f=\chi(f_0)$, where $\chi\colon[0,1]\to[0,1]$ is continuous, vanishes near zero, and is one on $[1/2,1]$. Then $$\label{eq:separation-core-identities}
 ef=0,\qquad ek_e=k_e,\qquad fk_f=k_f,\qquad ge=e,\qquad gf=f.$$ For the last two identities, $gh_0=h_0$ implies that multiplication by $g$ is the identity on $\operatorname{Her}(h_0)$. By <a href="#eq:separation-core-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-core-norms">[eq:separation-core-norms]</a>, both $(k_e)_P$ and $(k_f)_P$ are nonzero whenever $\|u_P\|\ge4\ell$.

*The quotient regions requiring a high column.* Define <a id="eq:separation-normalization-data"></a>$$\label{eq:separation-normalization-data}
 \tau=\frac{r\sqrt\ell}{8},\qquad
 C_1=avR,\quad C_2=bR,\qquad
 h_i=(|C_ig|-\tau)_+,\quad d_i=(|C_iN|-2\tau)_+.$$ We first check why a lower bound on the region $\|(d_i)_P\|\ge\tau$ will recover any signal missed by the corresponding low column. Suppose $\|a_P\|\ge r$ and $\|(X_0)_P\|<r/2$. Since $a=aL(u_*)+aN(u_*)$, we have $\|(aN(u_*))_P\|>r/2$. The identity $N(u_*)=vNv^*$ gives $\|(avN)_P\|>r/2$. On $[0,1]$, the scalar inequality $R^2N^2\ge\ell N^2$ holds: where $N$ is nonzero, $s>5\ell$ and $R(s)^2=\ell/s\ge\ell$. Consequently $$(C_1N)(C_1N)^*
 =avR^2N^2v^*a
 \ge\ell avN^2v^*a,$$ and therefore $$\|(C_1N)_P\|>\frac{r\sqrt\ell}{2}=4\tau.$$ It follows that $\|(d_1)_P\|>2\tau$. Similarly, if $\|b_P\|\ge r$ and $\|(Y_0)_P\|<r/2$, then $\|(bN)_P\|>r/2$. The order inequality $bR^2N^2b\ge\ell bN^2b$ gives $\|(d_2)_P\|>2\tau$. Thus every prescribed quotient either has a low column of norm at least $r/2$ or belongs to the corresponding perturbation region.

<a id="eq:separation-core-comparisons"></a>
*Comparison inputs and normalized left factors.* We now verify the inputs to Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">31</a> on these two regions. If $(d_i)_P\ne0$, then $C_iN=C_igN$ implies $\|(C_ig)_P\|>2\tau$, so $(h_i)_P\ne0$. The same assumption implies $N_P\ne0$, and therefore $\|u_P\|>5\ell$. Consequently $(k_e)_P,(k_f)_P\ne0$. The primitive ideal test and Corollary <a href="#cor:ideal-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:ideal-comparison">37</a> give $$\label{eq:separation-core-comparisons}
 d_1\precsim h_1,\quad d_1\precsim k_e,\qquad
 d_2\precsim h_2,\quad d_2\precsim k_f.$$

<a id="eq:separation-normalized-core"></a>
Let $D_i=C_ig$, and use continuous functional calculus in the unitization to set <a id="eq:separation-left-normalization"></a>$$\label{eq:separation-left-normalization}
 F_i=\bigl(\max\{\tau,|D_i^*|\}\bigr)^{-1},\qquad
 p_i=F_iD_i.$$ Thus $\|F_i\|\le\tau^{-1}$ and $p_i\in C$. The functional-calculus intertwining identity $$F_iD_i=D_i\bigl(\max\{\tau,|D_i|\}\bigr)^{-1}$$ follows first for polynomials in $D_iD_i^*$ and $D_i^*D_i$, and then by uniform approximation. In particular, $$\label{eq:separation-normalized-core}
 p_i^*p_i=|D_i|^2\bigl(\max\{\tau,|D_i|\}\bigr)^{-2}\le1,
 \qquad p_i^*p_ih_i=h_i.$$ The use of $|D_i^*|$ in the left factor $F_i$ is essential to this identity.

<a id="eq:separation-first-test"></a>
*Two compatible inverse perturbations.* Fix $$\eta=\frac18,\qquad \eta'=\frac{\sigma\eta}{16}
 =\frac{\sigma}{128}.$$ Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">31</a>, with $n=1$, to $$(p,q,h,k,c,T,\alpha)
 =(p_1,e,h_1,k_e,d_1,F_1C_1e,\tau)$$ at scale $\eta$.
