Its hypotheses follow from <a href="#eq:separation-core-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-core-identities">[eq:separation-core-identities]</a>, <a href="#eq:separation-core-comparisons" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-core-comparisons">[eq:separation-core-comparisons]</a>, and <a href="#eq:separation-normalized-core" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-normalized-core">[eq:separation-normalized-core]</a>. For the resulting contraction $y\in C$, put $$Z_1=1+\eta gyg.$$ Since $\|Z_1-1\|\le\eta<1$, the element $Z_1$ is invertible; define $W_1=(Z_1^*)^{-1}$. Since $ge=e$, the conclusion of the Proposition becomes $$\label{eq:separation-first-test}
 \|(F_1C_1Z_1e)_P\|\ge\sigma\eta
 \qquad\text{if }\|(d_1)_P\|\ge\tau.$$ Next apply the same Proposition at scale $\eta'$ to $$(p,q,h,k,c,T,\alpha)
 =(p_2,f,h_2,k_f,d_2,F_2C_2W_1f,\tau).$$ The element $T$ is in $C$; the Proposition imposes no bound on its norm. With the resulting contraction $y'\in C$, set $$W=W_1+\eta'gy'g.$$ Since $gf=f$, the second conclusion is <a id="eq:separation-second-test"></a>$$\label{eq:separation-second-test}
 \|(F_2C_2Wf)_P\|\ge\sigma\eta'
 \qquad\text{if }\|(d_2)_P\|\ge\tau.$$

<a id="eq:separation-factored-series"></a>
We record the inverse estimates, including the factorizations needed to preserve <a href="#eq:separation-first-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-first-test">[eq:separation-first-test]</a>. For $a_0\in C$ with $\|a_0\|<1$, the Neumann series gives $$\label{eq:separation-factored-series}
 (1+ga_0g)^{-1}=1+g\left(
 \sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-1)^k a_0(g^2a_0)^{k-1}\right)g.$$ The series of middle factors converges in $C$ with norm at most $\|a_0\|/(1-\|a_0\|)$. This formula does not require $g$ to be a projection. Applying it to $Z_1^*$ gives $$W_1=1+gw_1g,\qquad \|w_1\|\le\frac17.$$ Thus $W=1+gwg$ for $w=w_1+\eta'y'\in C$, with $$\|w\|\le\frac17+\frac1{128}<\frac14.$$ Applying <a href="#eq:separation-factored-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-factored-series">[eq:separation-factored-series]</a> to $W^*=1+gw^*g$ now proves that $W$ is invertible. Define $Z=(W^*)^{-1}$. The same series gives <a id="eq:separation-inverse-bounds"></a>$$\label{eq:separation-inverse-bounds}
 Z=1+gzg,\qquad \|z\|\le\frac13,
 \qquad \|Z\|\le\frac43,
 \quad\|W\|\le\frac54,
 \quad\|Z_1\|\le\frac98.$$ In particular, all conditions in <a href="#eq:separation-inverse-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-inverse-conditions">[eq:separation-inverse-conditions]</a> hold.

<a id="eq:separation-high-lower-bounds"></a>
The second perturbation changes the inverse used in the first column. To keep the first lower bound independent of $\ell$, we must estimate this change without the potentially large factor $\|F_1\|$. The inverse difference has the exact order $$Z-Z_1=-\eta' Zg(y')^*gZ_1.$$ As $Zg=g(1+zg^2)$, its effect on the first normalized column is bounded by <a id="eq:separation-no-normalization-loss"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|F_1C_1(Z-Z_1)e\|
 &=\eta'\|p_1(1+zg^2)(y')^*gZ_1e\|\notag\\
 &\le\eta'\left(1+\frac13\right)\frac98
 =\frac32\eta'.
 \label{eq:separation-no-normalization-loss}
\end{aligned}$$ Since $3\eta'/2\le\sigma\eta/2$, <a href="#eq:separation-first-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-first-test">[eq:separation-first-test]</a> remains bounded below by $\sigma\eta/2$ with $Z$ in place of $Z_1$. Using $\|F_i\|\le\tau^{-1}$ in each primitive quotient, and $\tau\ell^{-1/2}=r/8$, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 \|X_P\|&\ge\frac r8\frac{\sigma\eta}{2}
 &&\text{if }\|(d_1)_P\|\ge\tau,\notag\\
 \|Y_P\|&\ge\frac r8\sigma\eta'
 &&\text{if }\|(d_2)_P\|\ge\tau.
 \label{eq:separation-high-lower-bounds}
\end{aligned}$$ The cancellation $\tau\ell^{-1/2}=r/8$ is what removes $\ell$ from both lower bounds.

*Assembling the two positive outputs.*

To check the hereditary membership of $Q_a,Q_b$, <a href="#eq:separation-four-columns" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-four-columns">[eq:separation-four-columns]</a> and the bounds on $Z,W$ give $$X_0X_0^*\le a^2,\quad XX^*\le4\ell^{-1}a^2,\qquad
 Y_0Y_0^*\le b^2,\quad YY^*\le4\ell^{-1}b^2.$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:comparison-rules" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:comparison-rules">4</a>, the first two summands belong to $\operatorname{Her}(a)$ and the last two to $\operatorname{Her}(b)$. Thus $Q_a\in\operatorname{Her}(a)$ and $Q_b\in\operatorname{Her}(b)$. The earlier coverage reduction and <a href="#eq:separation-high-lower-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-high-lower-bounds">[eq:separation-high-lower-bounds]</a>, together with domination of the column norms by the output norms, prove <a href="#eq:separation-quotient-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-quotient-bounds">[eq:separation-quotient-bounds]</a> with $$m=\frac r8\sigma\eta'=\frac{r\sigma^2}{1024}.$$ Here $m\le r/2$ and $\eta'\le\eta/2$, so this single lower bound applies to all cases. It is independent of $\ell$.

Finally put $U_a=[X_0,X]$ and $U_b=[Y_0,Y]$ in $M_{1,2}(C)$. Their range squares are $Q_a^2$ and $Q_b^2$. The rectangular polar decompositions in the bidual have the form $U_a=Q_av_a$ and $U_b=Q_bv_b$, where $v_av_a^*$ and $v_bv_b^*$ are the support projections of $Q_a$ and $Q_b$, respectively. Hence both identities $$U_a^*U_b=v_a^*Q_aQ_bv_b,\qquad
 Q_aQ_b=v_a(U_a^*U_b)v_b^*$$ hold. Since partial isometries have norm at most one, they imply $$\|Q_aQ_b\|=\|U_a^*U_b\|
 \le6\ell+4\sqrt{6\ell}\le20\sqrt\ell,$$ by <a href="#eq:separation-high-orthogonality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-high-orthogonality">[eq:separation-high-orthogonality]</a> and <a href="#eq:separation-mixed-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-mixed-bounds">[eq:separation-mixed-bounds]</a>. This proves <a href="#eq:separation-product-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-product-bound">[eq:separation-product-bound]</a>.

The construction also covers zero elements and empty norm regions. If $h_0=0$, the row in <a href="#eq:separation-exact-cores" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-exact-cores">[eq:separation-exact-cores]</a> may be zero. Empty perturbation targets permit zero perturbations, and the coverage argument then assigns every prescribed quotient to a low column. All inverse operations have either the positive floor $\tau$ or the convergent series <a href="#eq:separation-factored-series" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-factored-series">[eq:separation-factored-series]</a>. ◻

</details>

The norms of the preceding outputs may grow as $\ell$ decreases. The next observation controls the comparison coefficients using only the fixed spectral floor $m$. This is enough for their small product to survive Cuntz implementation.

<a id="stmt-lem-bounded-implementation"></a>
<div id="lem:bounded-implementation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 39** (Comparison above a spectral floor). *Let $C$ be a $C^*$-algebra, let $Q,c\in C_+$ with $\|c\|\le1$, and let $m>0$. If $c\precsim(Q^2-m^2/4)_+$, then for each $0<\delta<1$ there is $x\in C$ such that <a id="eq:separation-bounded-implementation"></a>$$\label{eq:separation-bounded-implementation}
 \|(Qx)^*(Qx)-c\|<\delta,\qquad
 \|Qx\|\le2,\qquad \|x\|\le\frac4m.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-bounded-implementation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-bounded-implementation</summary>

*Proof.* Choose a comparison implementer $v\in C$ with $$\|v^*(Q^2-m^2/4)_+v-c\|<\delta.$$ Such an implementer lies in $C$ by compression to the two scalar matrix corners. On $[0,\|Q\|]$ define the continuous function $$j(s)=\begin{cases}s^{-1}(s^2-m^2/4)_+^{1/2},&s>0,\\0,&s=0,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad x=j(Q)v.$$ Then $(Qx)^*(Qx)=v^*(Q^2-m^2/4)_+v$ and $\|Qx\|^2<1+\delta<2$, which in particular gives $\|Qx\|\le2$. Moreover the scalar inequality $$j(s)^2\le\frac4{m^2}s^2j(s)^2$$ holds because $j$ vanishes on $[0,m/2]$. Functional calculus followed by multiplication by $v^*$ and $v$ gives $$x^*x\le\frac4{m^2}(Qx)^*(Qx).$$ Thus $\|x\|\le(2/m)\|Qx\|\le4/m$, independently of the norm of the original comparison implementer $v$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-direct-strong-infiniteness"></a>
<div id="prop:direct-strong-infiniteness" class="proposition">

**Proposition 40** (Direct exact proof). *Let $C$ be an exact $C^*$-algebra satisfying $\textup{(P)}$. For every $a,b\in C_+$ and every $\varepsilon>0$, there are $s,t\in C$ such that $$\|s^*a^2s-a^2\|<\varepsilon,\qquad
 \|t^*b^2t-b^2\|<\varepsilon,\qquad
 \|s^*abt\|<\varepsilon.$$ Consequently $C$ is strongly purely infinite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-direct-strong-infiniteness"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-direct-strong-infiniteness</summary>

<a id="eq:separation-final-ell"></a>
<a id="eq:separation-final-comparisons"></a>
*Proof.* Fix the constant $\sigma$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:perturbation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:perturbation">31</a> with $n=1$. Let $a,b\in C_+$ be contractions and let $\varepsilon>0$. Choose $0<r<1$ with $r^2<\varepsilon/3$ and put $m=r\sigma^2/1024$. Next choose $0<\ell<1/10$ so small that $$\label{eq:separation-final-ell}
 \frac{320\sqrt\ell}{m^2}<\frac\varepsilon3.$$ Take $Q_a,Q_b$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:separating-positives" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:separating-positives">38</a>. If $(a^2-r^2)_+$ has nonzero image at $P$, then $\|a_P\|>r$, so $\|(Q_a)_P\|\ge m$. Thus $(Q_a^2-m^2/4)_+$ has nonzero image there. The primitive ideal test and Corollary <a href="#cor:ideal-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:ideal-comparison">37</a> imply $$\label{eq:separation-final-comparisons}
 (a^2-r^2)_+\precsim(Q_a^2-m^2/4)_+,\qquad
 (b^2-r^2)_+\precsim(Q_b^2-m^2/4)_+.$$

<a id="eq:separation-final-delta"></a>
Finally choose $0<\delta<1$ such that $$\label{eq:separation-final-delta}
 \delta+(4+\delta)\delta<\frac\varepsilon3.$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:bounded-implementation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:bounded-implementation">39</a> to the comparisons in <a href="#eq:separation-final-comparisons" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-final-comparisons">[eq:separation-final-comparisons]</a>. We obtain $x_a,x_b\in C$ with $$\begin{aligned}
 \|(Q_ax_a)^*(Q_ax_a)-(a^2-r^2)_+\|&<\delta,\\
 \|(Q_bx_b)^*(Q_bx_b)-(b^2-r^2)_+\|&<\delta,
\end{aligned}$$ and $$\|Q_ax_a\|,\|Q_bx_b\|\le2,\qquad
 \|x_a\|,\|x_b\|\le\frac4m.$$ Consequently <a id="eq:separation-implemented-product"></a>$$\label{eq:separation-implemented-product}
 \|x_a^*Q_aQ_bx_b\|
 \le\frac{16}{m^2}\|Q_aQ_b\|
 \le\frac{320\sqrt\ell}{m^2}.$$

Since $Q_a\in\operatorname{Her}(a)=\overline{aCa}$, $Q_ax_a\in\overline{aC}$; similarly $Q_bx_b\in\overline{bC}$. Choose $s,t\in C$ such that $$\|as-Q_ax_a\|<\delta,\qquad
 \|bt-Q_bx_b\|<\delta.$$ For elements $U,V$ of norm at most $2$, replacing them by elements within $\delta$ changes $U^*U$ or $U^*V$ by at most $(4+\delta)\delta$, by expanding the difference and using the triangle inequality. Also $\|a^2-(a^2-r^2)_+\|,\|b^2-(b^2-r^2)_+\|\le r^2$. Therefore $$\begin{aligned}
 \|s^*a^2s-a^2\|,
 \ \|t^*b^2t-b^2\|
 &\le r^2+\delta+(4+\delta)\delta<\varepsilon,\\
 \|s^*abt\|
 &\le\frac{320\sqrt\ell}{m^2}+(4+\delta)\delta<\varepsilon,
\end{aligned}$$ by <a href="#eq:separation-final-ell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-final-ell">[eq:separation-final-ell]</a> and <a href="#eq:separation-final-delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:separation-final-delta">[eq:separation-final-delta]</a>. The choice order is $r$, then $m$, then $\ell$, then $\delta$; the lower bound $m$ has no dependence on the later parameters.

For arbitrary positives $a,b$, put $M=\max\{1,\|a\|,\|b\|\}$. Apply the preceding result to $a/M,b/M$ with tolerance $\varepsilon/M^2$. Multiplication of all three inequalities by $M^2$ gives the required inequalities for $a,b$ with the same $s,t\in C$. Zero elements, including the zero algebra, are included. These are precisely the three inequalities in <a href="#eq:SPI-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:SPI-definition">[eq:SPI-definition]</a>, so $C$ is strongly purely infinite. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Arbitrary algebra, $\\textup{(P)}$ Nuclear algebra, $\\textup{(F\\ensuremath{_n})}$ Exact algebra, $\\textup{(F\\ensuremath{_n})}$ $⇓$ $⇓$ $⇓$ $⇓$ $⇓$ $⇓$ Strong pure infiniteness $\\textup{(P)}$ $\\textup{(P)}$ $\\textup{(P)}\\ \\xRightarrow{\\ \\text{general route}\\ }\\ \\text{strong pure infiniteness} \\ \\xRightarrow{\\ \\text{separable nuclear}\\ }\\ \\mathcal O_\\infty\\text{-absorption}$ Proof routes and their hypotheses. Comparison transport supports all three routes. Either output $\\textup{(P)}$ can enter the left-hand route. Remark 23 extends the middle route to exact algebras using quotient permanence. Under exactness, Section 9 gives an alternative passage from $\\textup{(P)}$ to strong pure infiniteness. The final arrow is the external absorption criterion used in Corollary 3.",
      "figure_id": "fig:proof-routes",
      "pdf_page": 4,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:proof-routes",
          "exact_quote": "Proof routes and their hypotheses.  Comparison transport\nsupports all three routes.  Either output \\(\\propP\\) can enter the\nleft-hand route.  Remark~\\ref{rem:tuple-exact} extends the middle\nroute to exact algebras using quotient permanence.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "other",
      "tikz_pgf_source": null,
      "witness_description": "Flowchart diagram showing proof routes and hypotheses.\nAt the top, a spanning box labeled \"Comparison and cutoff transport (Section 2)\".\nBelow it are three parallel vertical branches:\n- Left branch: Header \"Arbitrary algebra, (P)\". Box \"Graph and four entries / Sections 3–4\" $\\Downarrow$ Box \"Products and iteration / Section 5\" $\\Downarrow$ Outcome \"Strong pure infiniteness\".\n- Middle branch: Header \"Nuclear algebra, $(F_n)$\". Box \"Graph and finite tuples / Sections 3, 6\" $\\Downarrow$ Box \"Local elements and merger / Section 6\" $\\Downarrow$ Outcome \"(P)\".\n- Right branch: Header \"Exact algebra, $(F_n)$\". Box \"Uniform perturbation / Section 7\" $\\Downarrow$ Box \"Square-zero localization / Section 8\" $\\Downarrow$ Outcome \"(P)\".\nAt the bottom, a horizontal sequence of arrows connects:\n\"(P)\" $\\xlongequal{\\text{general route}}$ \"strong pure infiniteness\" $\\xlongequal{\\text{separable nuclear}}$ \"$\\mathcal{O}_\\infty$-absorption\"."
    }
